**Startup and self-heal (`server.py:14780`–`15040`).** The `__main__` block is where the process assembles itself, and its ordering is load-bearing. It parses two flags — `--scheduler` (task scheduler, `server.py:14784`) and `--oversight` (meta-layer daemon, `server.py:14785`). It then binds the first free port in the range 5000–5010 by trying `socket.bind` on each in turn (`server.py:14789`); the first success wins and the loser ports are skipped. If all eleven are busy, the loop falls through with `port` still at its initial `5000` and `app.run` will then fail to bind — there is no explicit "all ports busy" error path.

Port selection is immediately followed by the core job queue's one-shot paid-provider reconciliation, before active-project or Trigger migrations, any worker start, or Flask HTTP serving.
This core-owned startup seam runs whether or not the optional video feature package is installed. It reattaches only a Replicate prediction durably bound to the same existing Dialogue and local job.
An indeterminate paid contact without that exact prediction binding is failed locally and is never submitted again on restart.

After the bind, a sequence of self-heal hooks runs, each wrapped in its own `try/except` so a failure prints and continues rather than aborting boot: `platform_check.startup_check` (engine-matches-machine validation, `server.py:14797`), the API in-flight pool cap sized from `install-state.json` via `mlx_mutex.configure_api_pool_from_install_state` (`server.py:14808`), an MLX worker heartbeat every 30s for the oversight health surface (`server.py:14822`), an embedding-readiness probe against the Ollama daemon (`server.py:14833`), index/RAG-manifest regeneration (shelling out to `bash generate-indexes.sh` / `compile-rag-manifest.sh` at `server.py:14856` / `14893` when the on-disk indexes drift from the directories), OpenRouter and direct-API catalog refreshes, local-MLX-model discovery that rewrites the `local_models` section of `config/models.json`, and a Lucide icon-set rebuild. Each is best-effort by design: this is a personal tool that should boot degraded rather than refuse to boot.

Two of these hooks are the durability spine. The **orphan pending-submission scan** (`_scan_orphaned_pending_submissions` in `server/app.py`) walks the pending submission directory at boot. Recovery holds the existing Dialogue lifecycle and envelope-write locks, including the cross-process file lock, while preparing the errored chunk and envelope. A missing envelope may be created; an unreadable existing envelope stays byte-for-byte at its original path. A readable envelope retains its prior messages and metadata while receiving the interrupted prompt, retry identity, and error state. The chunk and envelope each use their existing atomic writer. Only after both writes succeed does the scanner move the pending record to `processed/`; a refused or failed recovery leaves that source record pending, with read or write failures logged. This is the crash-recovery half of the lost-work guarantee. The **SIGINT/SIGTERM shutdown handler** fires `session_end` hooks, clears all in-memory sidebar windows, and cleans up bash-execution state before exiting.

**Trusted feature host.** During module assembly the server gives
`server/feature_plugins.py` an explicit ordered source list. Production lists
only Video, preserving its routes, asset URLs, and lifecycle callbacks. The host
can retain multiple valid descriptors, inject each one's ordered browser assets,
and dispatch lifecycle phases across them. An absent or broken source is logged
and skipped independently; there is no filesystem discovery, installer, or
third-party loading surface.

**The `/chat` request lifecycle.** This is the path to read first for the whole subsystem.

1. `chat()` (`server.py:6297`) parses the payload and immediately calls `_log_pending_submission` (`server.py:5352`) — *before* validation, before attachment processing, before anything that could fail. It writes the full submission (input, history, mode/framework selections, attachments) to a `pending/<submission_id>.json` record. This write is the recoverable record. If it fails, the handler logs and proceeds anyway — a disk error must not be the thing that drops the user's input.
2. Attachments are partitioned: text is inlined into the prompt, images are passed separately. Multipart image uploads (via `/chat/multipart`, `server.py:6439`) land under `~/ora/sessions/<conversation_id>/uploads/`.
3. `_invoke_pipeline` (`server.py:5997`) drives the pipeline. It iterates `agentic_loop_stream` synchronously (`server.py:6091`) — the generator still yields SSE-framed chunks internally, but the server consumes them (`json.loads(chunk[6:])`, `server.py:6101`) rather than forwarding them, collecting the original producer response and last-seen stage/mode/gear for bridge state. `agentic_loop_stream` (`server.py:3828`) itself dispatches to `_pipeline_stream` (analytical, `server.py:3003`) or `_direct_stream` (bypass, `server.py:3666`); the bypass guard lives inside the former (`server.py:3524`). After production, `_run_pipeline_from_step2` folds the after-clock over that original text and passes its signals and the original response through `_execution_review_terminal` before emitting one response frame. The terminal may return it unchanged, revised, or with a visible mandatory-handoff failure. Browser and resumed-clarification callers receive that value exactly once; route-observation and producer-claim bookkeeping remain tied to the original response. The conversation is marked Pending for the duration and cleared in a `finally`.
4. On a final response, `_save_conversation` (`server.py:5626`) runs synchronously under the Dialogue lifecycle lock so the `chunk_id` is known before the reply. It accepts only an exact Standard, Private, or Stealth authority for this exchange and requires Stealth authority to agree with the Dialogue's creation identity. The two new envelope messages receive the same local `turn_index`, `chunk_id`, and `turn_privacy`. The raw recovery log remains in its Dialogue-owned session file and marks the pair number and privacy; the processed chunk, index manifest, and Chroma row bind the same exchange through the Dialogue, turn/pair, chunk, and privacy fields available on those representations. One Dialogue may therefore contain Standard and Private exchanges side by side. Its current composer tag does not fill in authority missing from an older exchange. The envelope and raw exchange remain authoritative content; chunks, manifests, and index rows are owned representations, and false or partial metadata cannot make an unauthenticated turn eligible. The `_save_conversation` docstring still names `nomic-embed-text-v1.5` as the embedder; per the project's own migration notes the embedder is now bge-m3 — a docstring drift, harmless to this chapter but worth flagging when you read that function.
5. On success, `_finalize_pending_submission` moves the pending file to `processed/`; on failure it is left in `pending/` for the next boot's orphan scan. The reply is `{"status": "ok", ...}` or `{"status": "errored", ..., "failure_summary": ...}`. Failure marking updates a readable envelope; it does not replace an unreadable one. Existing unreadable Dialogue privacy authority is refused rather than treated as a new Standard Dialogue, and Close returns `409` when that authority cannot be read. The failure summary is deliberately informative: when the generator returned without a response and without an error, the code synthesizes a breadcrumb from the last observed stage rather than the bare "no response" string.

**Current Model response and protected-operation boundaries.** Gear-1/2 and required Gear-3/4 model-call boundaries return usable text with invocation identities or raise a typed failure; exhaustion does not create a completed assistant answer from diagnostic text. The ordinary response handler drains request-thread Pipeline Health warnings, including one warning when recovery selected a different model. Parallel workers return their outcomes for that request-thread aggregation. Same-model retries emit no substitution warning, and a warning-surface failure logs without breaking usable output. This is not a claim that resumed clarification paths already share the complete warning collector; that integration remains with D103.

`DELETE /api/configurations/<name>` and `POST /api/retrieval/rebuild/start` complete deterministic preflight before one-shot approval. The former binds the exact custom profile, active pointer, and existing validated Free fallback when needed; the latter binds the vector-store/configuration/script state and one immutable profile/script plan. Existing locks govern their immediate rechecks before mutation or worker admission. Invalid initial targets consume no approval or start receipt; later drift, contention, launch failure, or effect failure closes with a failed receipt. Receipt infrastructure failure is surfaced, never reported as a successful operation. Chapters 4 and 8 give the respective effect boundaries.

Side effects that do not gate delivery begin only after the complete exchange is durable. In particular, background document extraction does not trust the prompt and answer retained by its worker. It reacquires the same Dialogue lifecycle lock, rereads the envelope, and authenticates the current local turn number, chunk id, content, and privacy before processing. It receives that one freshly authenticated exchange, not the preceding Dialogue history. Extraction failure is logged loudly but does not retract an answer already delivered.

The browser exposes two different privacy operations because their consequences differ. **Compose next as Standard/Private** changes only the next exchange's proposed authority. **Make displayed turn Standard/Private** identifies the exact displayed owner, reacquires the displayed and canonical owner locks in deterministic order, resolves the owner again, and then changes one complete non-Stealth exchange. Tightening to Private updates only exact Ora-owned representations and the exact managed Daily Note summary. Missing, conflicting, edited, or ambiguous material is retained and returned as incomplete; the UI must not claim that every copy changed.

The Findings export toolbar also treats browser state as a view, not evidence. **Save output to Vault**, Word, and PDF send only the owner tuple emitted with the displayed exchange; the server rereads effective history and the canonical local owner before using canonical assistant content and deriving a title. A Private output remains visibly labelled and Private-tagged. An explicit Stealth output omits privacy markers and source identity. **Save full Dialogue** authenticates every effective exchange while holding all owner lifecycle locks in deterministic order; a non-Stealth target rejects Stealth-owned turns, while a Stealth target may include canonically eligible inherited Standard or Private ancestry and uses a neutral source-free fallback name when its canonical title is unsafe.

**Cross-platform path resolution.** Every filesystem root the server touches now flows through `orchestrator/runtime_paths.py`. `WORKSPACE` honors `$ORA_HOME` before falling back to `expanduser("~/ora")` (`server.py:22`); conversation roots come from `runtime_paths.CONVERSATIONS_STR`, which resolves `$ORA_CONVERSATIONS` or `expanduser("~")/Documents/conversations` (`runtime_paths.py:27`,`38`). Because the defaults are built from `expanduser("~")` rather than literal `/Users/...`, they resolve to `%USERPROFILE%` on Windows. The module also carries the pieces a Windows port actually needs: `norm_key` / `within_base` (`runtime_paths.py:49`,`61`) do case- and separator-normalized, directory-boundary-anchored path comparison — so a protected-prefix check is not fooled by a sibling like `~/ora-project` matching `~/ora` — and `locked_file` (`runtime_paths.py:100`) is a cross-platform advisory lock that uses `fcntl` on POSIX and `msvcrt` on Windows, each imported under a guard so neither platform crashes at import (`runtime_paths.py:88`,`92`). One residual hardcode survives the retrofit inside `server.py`: `VISUAL_UPLOADS_ROOT = os.path.expanduser("~/ora/sessions/")` (`server.py:6376`) does not consult `$ORA_HOME`, so a relocated install writes uploads to the un-relocated root. That is a portability drift, not a crash.

**Config surfaces.** The tracked launcher `start.sh` exports the runtime flags — `ORA_HOME`, `ORA_RAG_SELECTION=1`, `ORA_RAG_FIT_GATE_SLOT=sidebar`, `ORA_WEB_EXTRACTION=1`, `ORA_RUNTIME_ENGRAM_PROMOTION=1`, `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB=1` and more (`start.sh:12-58`) — then launches the server backgrounded (`"$PYTHON" server/app.py --oversight &`), selecting `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` with a system-`python3` fallback. Model routing is edited through `GET/POST /config/routing/slots` (`server.py:14042`/`14071`), which merges `{preferred, fallback}` into a single named slot within `routing-config.json` and rejects underscore-prefixed keys from client patches so annotations can't be clobbered. Named configurations live behind the `/api/configurations*` family (`server.py:13395`–`13596`): list, duplicate, new, per-slot edit, per-slot fallback edit, delete, active-get/set, and active-toggles. The active pipeline is chosen through these, not a config-file edit.

**Programming documentation-evidence forwarding (current scoped behavior).** `POST /api/programming/run` accepts an optional `documentation_review` object alongside the approved plan. The server rejects a non-object value and forwards the object unchanged to `orchestrator.programming.run_approved_programming`; it does not reinterpret, summarize, or certify the evidence. The browser sends that object only when it resumes a documentation-impacting task at the final evidence boundary, after sending the pasted raw JSON through the existing privacy screen. The HTTP layer preserves the coordinator's complete packet; Programming validates its final gate state and the independent reviewer owns the semantic verdict described in Chapter 18.

**The route surface (174 routes).** Grouped by family: chat (`/chat`, `/chat/multipart`, `/chat/queue-retry`); clarification (`/api/clarification`, `/skip`, `/pending`); conversations (list, load, fork, mark-read, close); configurations and routing slots (above); oversight (`/api/oversight/paused` at `server.py:14683`, `/api/oversight/operating` at `14716`, and per-entry `/name`, `/engagement`, `/discuss` at `14731`–`14761`); canvas/visual (`/api/canvas/save` at `6691`, `/api/canvas/load/<id>` at `6792`, visual-schema serving); media capability — nine wired image/video POST routes (`image_generates` at `server.py:11048`, then `image_edits` `11227`, `image_outpaints` `11403`, `image_upscales` `11615`, `image_styles` `11790`, `image_critique` `11987`, `image_varies` `12375`, `image_to_prompt` `12601`, and `video_generates` `12779`); themes; and the model registry (`/models` at `12932`, `/api/model-registry/picks` at `13652`). The capability image/video routes are live and wired — this contradicts the stale note elsewhere in the project docs that image generation is "locked but not implemented." That note was not superseded in place.

**Residual SSE.** Streaming survives on exactly three route bodies, all returning `text/event-stream`: `/api/document/stream` (`server.py:5084`), `/api/clarification` (`server.py:10714`), and `/api/clarification/skip` (`server.py:10805`); `/api/framework/analyze-inputs` (`server.py:5109`) sits in the same neighborhood. These are the minority path. The pipeline's own progress is not among them.

**The V3 static UI.** `/` and `/v3` both serve `index-v3.html` (`server.py:3859`, `3867`); the classic `/classic` and `/v2` routes were retired (per the `index()` comment, 2026-05-30). `index-v3.html` owns the stable Knowledge Library mount and the single mode-aware listener on the native O. `library-workspace.js` owns ordinary Library state, inventory and pinned-relationship requests, List/Visual rendering, measured geometry, preview layers, relationship disclosure, and the extension-action event; `library-workspace.css` owns its presentation. `sidebar.js` retains the launcher and existing Dialogue mutations but no longer creates a second Library overlay or calls the two-source search endpoint for Library results. It also runs the conversation-list reconciliation loop (12s, visibility-gated so a hidden tab stops polling). `sidebar-oversight.js` drives the three-supergroup accordion (Conversations / Paused / Operating) and polls `/api/oversight/paused` and `/api/oversight/operating` every 12s. `v3-conversation.js` remains the active Dialogue owner and renders assistant turns; Library pin preview does not call it.

Aside submission uses the existing privacy preparation screen. Choosing Private updates the current Dialogue’s privacy in place; it does not create another Dialogue. Its callback reads the resulting active Dialogue ID only after that process completes, preserving any main Inquiry draft. While a Dialogue is loading or a composition target is unresolved, Aside does not send the prompt. The browser sends that ID with the prompt to `/api/scratchpad` and accepts a reply only for the same ID. Each Dialogue has its own temporary display of up to ten query/answer pairs. Selection hides the previous display while loading; successful navigation restores the selected Dialogue's display, and failed loading restores the still-active one. A late reply stays with its original Dialogue's display. Successful Delete Forever discards that display and prevents an outstanding reply from recreating it.

**Partial and standalone components.** `/chat/queue-retry` (`server.py:9034`) is **partial**: it persists vision-retry requests both in-memory and to `~/ora/sessions/<conversation_id>/vision-retry-queue.json`, but its own docstring is explicit — "NO automatic retry here — a future daemon or user-triggered action will flush the queue." Persistence is built; the flush is not.

`server/review.py` remains a **separate** standalone Flask app (the Note Review interface) that binds `127.0.0.1:5005` in its own `__main__`; it is not imported by `server/app.py`. Its runtime-created review records are nevertheless part of the per-exchange privacy contract. Each carries the exact Dialogue owner, local turn, chunk, and privacy from the authenticated extraction source. Approve, edit, and reject take the same cross-process Dialogue lifecycle lock used by retag, reread the review record under that lock, and refuse if its owner changed before an approved Markdown derivative can be published.

**Failure behavior, summarized.** Every startup hook is best-effort. A pipeline crash mid-run leaves the pending file in place; the next boot surfaces an errored chunk when recovery can save the chunk and envelope, otherwise it logs the failure and retains the pending source. A save failure returns `status: errored` and marks a readable envelope without replacing unreadable authority. The oversight health surface prepends a warning to the next chat response when the daemon is down or a watcher is stale, so the user sees degradation in the conversation without a separate dashboard.

### Evidence & Verification

**What is tested.** The focused `server/static/tests/test-library-ui-quick-fixes.js` harness covers the production Library's independent mixed sources, Files-only Folder grouping and root label, stale-group repair, continuity, zero-source local state, authoritative totals/paging, complete-inventory metadata query, stable-ID append, separate inventory/relationship/render generations, native O semantics, inert-state restoration, lower-pane preservation, semantic relationship disclosure, summary-only no-edge behavior, explicit Related endpoint O-connectors, contextual action extension, and measured 1024/736/360 layouts. `orchestrator/tests/test_library_browser.py` separately covers shared-provider aggregation and the mixed-source paginated HTTP boundary. The visual-dispatch fence extraction has `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/tests/cases/test-v3-visual-dispatch.js` (12 assertions, per the project's test docs). The routing-slots endpoint has `orchestrator/tests/test_routing_slots_endpoint.py` (12 Python tests, verified by count in-file) and `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/tests/cases/test-visual-slots-pane.js` (24 JS assertions, per the project's test docs), part of a JS harness cited at 813/813.

**What is not tested — and this is the honest gap.** There is no server-route-level HTTP integration test inside `server/`. A grep of `server.py` for test or pytest references returns nothing in-file. Route correctness is inferred from orchestrator-side unit tests plus live probing, not from an automated suite that boots the app and exercises `POST /chat`. There is no CSP test and no auth test, because there is no CSP header and no auth. The full 174-route surface was mapped by family; the capability/media/theme sub-endpoints are asserted present by their route decorators but not each exercised by an automated request. And whether the three residual SSE endpoints are still exercised by the current V3 JS or are vestigial was not traced through the client.

**How to confirm it works live** (this is a manual path, not CI): launch via `start.sh`; `GET /health` (`server.py:3871`) returns `{"status": "ok", "endpoint": ...}`; `POST /chat` with a real prompt, then confirm a chunk `.md` lands under the conversations directory and the `conversation.json` envelope updates. For the durability guarantee specifically: `POST /chat`, kill the process mid-pipeline before the reply, restart, and confirm the submission reappears as an errored chunk with retry/dismiss (the orphan scan at `server.py:14984`).

### Expert Reading Notes

**Read these four spans first, in order.** (1) The `__main__` block (`server.py:14780`–`15040`) for startup ordering and every self-heal side effect — this is where the process's contract with its environment lives. (2) `_invoke_pipeline` (`server.py:5997`) and (3) the bypass guard inside `_pipeline_stream` (`server.py:3507`–`3541`) for the request lifecycle and the empty-response fix. (4) `_save_conversation` (`server.py:5626`) for the persistence contract — internalize that `conversation.json` is source of truth and chunks are a denormalized cache before you touch either.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift.** First, the doc-vs-code gap on streaming: any documentation that headlines "Flask + SSE" or "SSE streaming with `pipeline_stage` events" is describing the retired model; the installed `/chat` is synchronous JSON with disk persistence and browser polling. Second, the 174-route surface grows without a map, so any route-count or route-family reference goes stale quietly. Third, launcher drift: the tracked `start.sh` sets every runtime `ORA_*` flag (`start.sh:12-58`) and selects `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` with a system-`python3` fallback, oversight-on by default — this is the committed, reproducible launcher. The author's Mac additionally runs an **untracked** `run-ora-server.sh` launchd wrapper (not in commit 7a5e8f40; a fresh clone won't have it) whose flag set can diverge from `start.sh`. Because the wrapper is untracked and machine-local, anyone reasoning from the commit should treat `start.sh` as authoritative; the divergence is a live hazard only on a machine carrying the untracked wrapper. Fourth, the 12s poll cadence duplicated across `sidebar.js` and `sidebar-oversight.js`. Fifth, the `_save_conversation` embedder docstring naming nomic while the system runs bge-m3.

**One thing to verify before you rely on it.** `sidebar-oversight.js` hedges the Approve/Deny commit path as going through "`/api/oversight/resolve` (or fall back to slash commands)," and its own header comment says the v1 build uses the slash-command path. There is no `/api/oversight/resolve` route in `server.py` — the oversight route family stops at `/discuss` (`server.py:14761`). Approve/Deny commit through the chat resolution-chain / slash-command path, not a dedicated resolve endpoint. Do not build against a resolve endpoint that isn't there.

### Platform compatibility

The macOS+Windows portability retrofit (PR #181) is what this commit *is*, so the platform story here is better than "Mac-only" — but "retrofitted so it should work" is not "run and verified off-Mac," and I label the difference. Nothing below was booted on a non-Mac surface; every non-mac cell that is not a clean-degrade path is "untested."

- **First-free-port bind (5000–5010):** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = untested | Linux = untested. Plain `socket.bind` loop on `localhost` (`server.py:14789`); `localhost` resolves cross-platform, but full boot to a bound port was only observed on macOS. If all eleven ports are busy the loop falls through and `app.run` fails to bind — no dedicated error path.
- **Filesystem roots (`$ORA_HOME`, `$ORA_CONVERSATIONS`):** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = untested | Linux = untested. Resolved through `runtime_paths.py` from `expanduser("~")` (`runtime_paths.py:27`,`38`), which maps to `%USERPROFILE%` on Windows. The retrofit is the reason this is no longer "needs-porting," but no non-Mac boot was run, so it stays untested rather than "plausibly-works."
- **`VISUAL_UPLOADS_ROOT` hardcode:** macOS = works | Windows-native = wrong-root | WSL = untested | Linux = works-at-default-only. `os.path.expanduser("~/ora/sessions/")` (`server.py:6376`) ignores `$ORA_HOME`; a relocated install writes multipart uploads to the un-relocated path. Drift, not crash.
- **Cross-platform advisory lock:** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = works | Linux = works. `fcntl` on POSIX, `msvcrt` on Windows, guarded imports (`runtime_paths.py:88`,`92`). The Windows branch exists but was not exercised here.
- **Untracked launch artifact `run-ora-server.sh` (author's machine only — NOT in commit 7a5e8f40):** a `#!/bin/zsh -l` launchd wrapper hardcoding `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3`; macOS-only by construction and not part of the committed system (a fresh clone does not contain it). The tracked launcher at this commit is `start.sh`.
- **MLX heartbeat + `mlx_mutex` (Apple MLX/Metal):** macOS = works (Apple Silicon) | Windows-native = degrades | WSL = degrades | Linux = degrades. Wrapped in `try/except` (`server.py:14822`); off Apple Silicon the import fails, the hook is skipped, and the local-model path is simply absent — the server still boots.
- **Reveal-in-Finder (`POST /api/fs/reveal` → `open -R`):** macOS = works | Windows-native = clean-501 | WSL = clean-501 | Linux = clean-501. Guarded by `sys.platform != "darwin"` returning HTTP 501 "reveal-in-Finder is macOS-only" (`server.py:4948`) before it shells out to `open -R`. This one *was* ported — it degrades cleanly rather than erroring on a missing `open`.
- **Startup `bash` script calls (`generate-indexes.sh`, `compile-rag-manifest.sh`):** macOS = works | Windows-native = fails-soft | WSL = works | Linux = works. Invoked as `subprocess.run(["bash", ...])` (`server.py:14856`,`14893`); no `bash` on Windows-native means these fail, but they are inside the startup `try/except` and only skip index/manifest self-heal.
- **SIGINT/SIGTERM shutdown + threaded Flask:** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = works | Linux = works. `signal.signal` handlers (`server.py:14975`–`14976`); SIGTERM semantics differ on Windows-native.
- **Embedding-readiness probe (Ollama reachability):** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = untested | Linux = untested. `assert_embedding_ready` at `server.py:14833`; the module comment claims Win/Linux/Mac but only Mac was observed.
- **`index-v3.html` asset loading (absolute `/static` URLs, deferred scripts):** macOS = works | Windows-native = works | WSL = works | Linux = works. Browser-served absolute URLs are OS-agnostic and carry no filesystem-separator concern client-side.
- **CSP / auth / non-localhost bind:** all platforms = absent. No `Content-Security-Policy` header on the served page (0 matches in `index-v3.html`), no auth middleware, `host="localhost"` only (`server.py:15040`). This is a scope decision, uniform across platforms, and it is the blocker for any remote-access goal.

## 3. Settings, API Keys & Local-Model Handling

- **Current API-key store boundary.** Before approval, `POST /api/settings/api-key` requires a registered provider and one non-empty string value. Approval binds the exact raw string that the handler stores; malformed values cannot consume the approval.

### Problem

Three failure modes converge on one subsystem, and each is the kind that corrupts trust in an install rather than merely inconveniencing it.

The first is secret leakage. An orchestrator that talks to two dozen external providers holds a bearer credential for each, and each of those credentials bills the user's card. Store those keys in a JSON config file and they leak into git, into backups, into log lines, into any error message that echoes a request URL. The naive alternative — re-prompt for keys every session — trades leakage for friction so severe the product is unusable. The requirement is keys durable across restarts, absent from every file the user might share, and scrubbed from every error string that can round-trip to a browser.

The second is provider-metadata drift. The Settings UI must know each provider's display label and console URL. The boot sequence must know each provider's env-var name to bridge its key. The key-setup framework must know each provider's key prefix to validate format. The verify probe must know each provider's authentication scheme. If those four surfaces each carry their own copy of the provider table, they drift: a provider added to the UI never gets its key bridged to the env; a renamed env var silently drops a working key. This is the classic single-source-of-truth problem, and the punishment for getting it wrong is a key the user pasted correctly that nonetheless does nothing.

The third is local-model registry staleness. Ora runs MLX models — Apple's array framework for on-device inference on Apple Silicon — from `~/ora/models/`. When the user drops a new model into that directory or deletes an old one, nothing notices until a human hand-edits `config/models.json`. The V3 Models pane then shows models that are gone and hides models that are present. This was a live gap dated 2026-05-26, recorded in `local_model_discovery.py`'s own module notes: nothing in the system noticed the on-disk change until a human edited `models.json`.

A fourth, quieter need rides along: non-secret preferences — Whisper model size, default render preset, output style, capture frame rate — need a durable home that is neither an environment variable (wrong lifetime, wrong scope) nor per-conversation state (wrong persistence).

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

Two deliberate splits and one automation choice.

**Split one: secrets live in the OS keyring; everything else lives in JSON.** API keys are written only to the operating system's credential store via the `keyring` library — service name `ora`, username `<provider>-api-key`. On macOS that store is the Keychain. Non-secret preferences live in `config/user-settings.json`, which holds no secrets — its module docstring states "Secrets (API keys) are NOT stored here; they live in the system keyring" (`orchestrator/user_settings.py:5-7`). The rejected alternative was a single encrypted config blob. It reinvents a credential store the OS already ships, audits, and integrates with the user's login session; a homegrown blob is a smaller attack surface's illusion, not its reality.

**The single source of truth for provider metadata is `provider_registry.py`.** One list, `PROVIDERS`, declares every provider's id, label, category, keyring username, env var, signup and console URLs, key prefix, activation flags, dispatch style, and verify eligibility (`orchestrator/provider_registry.py:73-328`). Every consumer derives from it: `user_settings.py` builds its keyring-username map and its UI status rows from `keyring_username_map()` and `ui_rows()`; `boot.py` builds its keyring→env bridge from `env_bridge_pairs()`; the prefer-direct router builds its rewrite table from `or_prefix_map()`. The registry's own docstring states the invariant: adding a provider is a one-entry edit here; no consumer needs touching. The rejected alternative — duplicating provider tables across the JavaScript panel, the Python boot path, and the setup docs — is exactly the drift failure mode above. The tradeoff is a single point of failure inverted into a single point of correctness: a provider omitted from `PROVIDERS` silently vanishes from the env-bridge, the verify probe, and the Settings UI at once. That is a real hazard, and it recurs under Expert Reading Notes because it is the most likely way a future edit breaks this subsystem.

**Split two: desktop and server key handling diverge on purpose.** The Mac desktop uses the keyring. The headless Linux server does not. `scripts/install-server.sh` writes `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` (and optionally `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`) to a plaintext file at `~/.config/ora-server.env`, `chmod 600`, sourced into the process environment (`scripts/install-server.sh:190-217, 226-231`). This is a deliberate acceptance of plaintext-on-a-locked-box over the cost of wiring a headless credential daemon — the Secret Service backend `keyring` targets on Linux needs a running keyring daemon and a login session a headless VM does not have. What makes the divergence cheap is the bridge design below: the same `boot.py` code serves both hosts because it reads keys from the environment, and the environment is populated either by the keyring mirror (Mac) or by the sourced env-file (Linux). This is an open problem, not a solved one: there is no OS-backed credential store on the server path. The plaintext env-file is the current answer, and a headless-keyring option remains unbuilt. Nothing in `CLAUDE.md` documents the server plaintext path — its only keyring reference is a note about which Python interpreter ships the `keyring` package — so a reader who knows only the desktop model can wrongly assume keyring everywhere.

**Automation choice: local models are discovered, not curated.** `local_model_discovery.py` scans `~/ora/models/` at server startup and rewrites the `local_models` array in `config/models.json`. The rejected alternative — hand-curated entries — is the staleness failure mode by construction. The tradeoff is that discovery can only read what the model files declare, so a model with a malformed `config.json` is silently skipped rather than surfaced as an error. Against that, a no-shrink guard: `refresh()` refuses any write that would record fewer local models than `models.json` already holds, printing the refusal and the missing ids, so neither an unmounted models directory nor a readable-but-empty one can truncate the registry (`local_model_discovery.py::refresh`). Without that guard, a race between a mount and server startup would erase every local model from the UI; deliberate removal is the exception and passes `allow_shrink=True` (CLI `--write --allow-shrink`).

### Implementation

**Provider registry (`orchestrator/provider_registry.py`).** `PROVIDERS` is a list of 24 dicts. The derived helpers are pure functions over that list: `keyring_username_map()`, `labels_map()`, `env_bridge_pairs()` (every provider declaring an `env_var`), `direct_llm_providers()` (those with a `dispatch` value), `catalog_authoritative_providers()` (direct providers with a real pay-as-you-go path), `or_prefix_map()` (keyed by OpenRouter vendor segment for the prefer-direct rewrite), and `ui_rows()` (the no-secret payload the panel renders). `validate_key_format(provider_id, value)` is lenient by design: it returns a `(looks_ok, message)` tuple, and a key under 12 characters or missing its declared prefix returns `looks_ok=False` with a warning message that the UI surfaces but the store path never enforces (`provider_registry.py:384-399`) — the docstring ties this to the vault's [[Framework — API Key Setup]] rule that a surprising shape warns but never blocks a key the user insists on. Categories drive UI grouping through `GROUP_ORDER`; dispatch metadata (`native` vs `openai_compatible`, `base_url`, `or_prefix`, `native_service`) drives direct-vendor routing.

**Settings persistence (`orchestrator/user_settings.py`).** Load, save, and reset use `runtime_paths.CONFIG_DIR / "user-settings.json"`, under the effective `ORA_HOME` (default `~/ora`). A relocated installation does not fall back to the home installation's settings. `load_settings()` deep-merges the on-disk file over `DEFAULTS` and is forward-compatible: keys present on disk but absent from `DEFAULTS` are preserved on round-trip, so an older client cannot truncate a field a newer server wrote (`user_settings.py:158-181`). Writes go through `_write_raw()`, which writes a `.json.tmp` sibling and calls `Path.replace` — an atomic same-filesystem rename that prevents a torn write from a crash mid-save (`user_settings.py:151-155`). A `threading.Lock` serializes read-modify-write against concurrent requests. `save_settings()` type-validates known leaves before persisting (`capture.frame_rate` ∈ {24,25,30,50,60}, `whisper.model_size` ∈ {tiny,base,small,medium,large-v3}, render preset against `render.py`'s keys) and raises `SettingsError` rather than writing garbage.

The API-key surface delegates entirely to the keyring, guarded by a provider whitelist derived from the registry so the HTTP endpoints cannot write an arbitrary keyring account (`user_settings.py:274-277`). `set_api_key`, `delete_api_key`, and `api_key_present` import `keyring` lazily — the comment notes this lets tests stub it via `sys.modules`. `delete_api_key` swallows every exception because keyring backends raise different exception types for "not found," and "already absent" is success (`user_settings.py:288-296`). `list_api_key_status()` returns the registry's `ui_rows()` enriched with a live `present` boolean and nothing else — the value never leaves the keyring (`user_settings.py:310-324`).

**The keyring→env bridge (`orchestrator/boot.py:173-200`).** `_export_provider_keys_to_env()` runs unconditionally at module import (`boot.py:200`). It iterates `provider_registry.env_bridge_pairs()` and, for each `(env_var, keyring_username)`, writes the keyring value into `os.environ` only when the env var is not already set — the "pre-set env vars win" rule (comment at `boot.py:170`, enforced at `boot.py:189-190`). This one rule unifies the two hosts: on Mac the keyring populates the env; on Linux the sourced env-file has already populated it, so the bridge sees the var set and no-ops. The function is defensively wrapped: a missing `keyring` backend (`ImportError`) returns early, and a registry import failure falls back to the original web-search trio (Tavily/Brave/Exa) so the search cascade survives even a broken registry (`boot.py:176-188`). Without the bridge, every consumer that reads `os.environ` would see no key until a manual restart-time lookup — a cold-start credential outage.

At dispatch time the read path is env-first, keyring-second. `_provider_key(entry)` returns the env var if set and stripped, else falls back to `_keyring_lookup("ora", entry["keyring_username"])` (`boot.py:13713-13720`). The prefer-direct router (`_resolve_direct_endpoint`, `boot.py:13723`+) uses `or_prefix_map()` to rewrite an OpenRouter `vendor/model` id to the vendor's own API when that vendor's key is present, bypassing OpenRouter's markup. It is gated by `ORA_PREFER_DIRECT`, read at `boot.py:13701`: `_prefer_direct_enabled()` treats the value as on unless it is explicitly `0/false/no/off`. That flag is a kill switch, not a feature gate — it defaults on, so prefer-direct is installed and live, not flag-gated-off.

**Settings HTTP surface (`server/app.py`).** `GET /api/settings` returns settings, the no-secret `api_keys` rows, and provider groups (`server.py:1142-1160`). `POST /api/settings` deep-merges an `updates` dict and returns the merged state (`1163-1177`). `POST /api/settings/api-key` stores a key; `DELETE /api/settings/api-key/<provider>` removes one, validating the provider id against the registry before touching the keyring (`1810-1844`). `POST /api/settings/api-key/verify` runs `_verify_provider_key`, which makes the cheapest authenticated call that proves a key works — a free auth probe, a model-list GET, or a single search — using only stdlib `urllib` (`1847-1927`). Every branch is free or negligible; providers whose only auth proof costs a generation (image, TTS, transcription) are marked `verifiable: false` in the registry and short-circuit to "Verification isn't available" at the endpoint before any network call (`1945-1947`). The scrubbing discipline is the load-bearing detail: FRED authenticates via query string, so a malformed key can embed itself in a URL that an exception stringifies; the catch-all replaces the key with `***` before returning (`1922-1926`). The Gemini probe deliberately passes the key as a header, not a query param, for the same reason (`1884-1887`). This component closes the "secret round-trips to the browser in an error message" failure mode.

**Local-model discovery (`orchestrator/local_model_discovery.py`).** `probe_model_dir()` reads a subdirectory's `config.json` and `*.safetensors`, returning `None` for any directory lacking a config, lacking safetensors, or carrying malformed JSON — so `diffusers`, `whisper`, and `loras` subdirectories are rejected automatically (`local_model_discovery.py:238-284, 287-296`). It estimates total parameters from safetensors byte-size and quantization bits rather than parsing layers, which works across architectures and captures embeddings and vision towers for free (`111-127`). It detects MoE (mixture-of-experts — a model that routes each token through a subset of its parameters) and extracts active-params-per-token, first from the directory-name `-a<N>b` convention, then from the config's expert-routing ratio (`85-97, 129-164`). Vision capability is inferred from three signals: a `vision_config` block, an image-token field, or an architecture ending in `ConditionalGeneration` (`61-82`). `refresh(write=True)` rewrites `models.json`'s `local_models` array and stamps `_local_models_discovered_at`, preserving `commercial_models` and all other top-level keys, and refuses the write outright when discovery would record fewer models than the file already holds, unless `allow_shrink=True` (`local_model_discovery.py::refresh`). `server.py` calls it best-effort at startup, wrapped so a failure logs but does not abort boot (`server.py:15008-15024`). The CLI defaults to dry-run; `--write` persists.

**Install-side selection (`scripts/local_models.py`).** A separate concern: at install time, a RAM-tier classifier maps total system RAM to a deployment tier (`small_only` under 32 GB, `workhorse_choice` under 64, `full_local` under 128, `adversarial` at 128+) and a curated per-tier catalog, then wraps `huggingface_hub.snapshot_download` for the fetch, with `local_dir_use_symlinks=False` to avoid the Windows symlink-permission pitfall, and `HF_TOKEN` guidance for gated repos (`scripts/local_models.py:113-296, 329-362`). RAM detection branches on `sys.platform`: `sysctl` on macOS, `/proc/meminfo` on Linux, `wmic` on Windows (`92-120`). This path writes install state to `config/local-models.json`, distinct from the runtime `models.json`.

**Failure behavior, summarized.** Missing keyring backend → bridge no-ops, verify returns `None`. Malformed model `config.json` → that directory is silently skipped. Zero discovered models → `refresh` declines to write. Malformed `user-settings.json` → `load_settings` raises `SettingsError` rather than returning partial state.

### Evidence & Verification

Four test suites cover the subsystem, 110 tests total, and the counts hold against the files: `test_user_settings.py` (27 — persistence, deep-merge, forward-compat, validation, and keyring CRUD via a stubbed `keyring` in `sys.modules`); `test_provider_registry.py` (22 — derived maps, `env_bridge_pairs`, `validate_key_format`, `ui_rows`); `test_local_model_discovery.py` (37 — probe, scan, refresh, MoE active-param extraction, vision detection, and the zero-entry guard); `test_chunk8_local_models.py` (24 — RAM-tier classification, RAM estimation, and the download wrapper). Adjacent coverage lives in `test_aa_path_resolution.py` and `test_direct_catalog.py`.

Live confirmation: `config/models.json` on this machine carries 6 discovered `local_models` and a `_local_models_discovered_at` float stamp — four Qwen MLX builds (a 4B abliterated variant, a 9B, a 27B, and a 122B `-a10b` MoE), GLM-4.6V, and a 128B Mistral-Medium-3.5, matching the 2026-05-26 vision lineup. The server-path key store is checked by `scripts/install_server_smoke.py`, which asserts `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` is sourced from the env-file.

Explicitly untested surfaces. The `keyring` backend is exercised only on macOS Keychain; the Windows Credential Locker and Linux Secret Service paths are never run — the server path deliberately routes around them with the env-file. The verify probes call live provider endpoints; there is no offline mock, so the most failure-prone branch of `_verify_provider_key` — the network call itself — is uncovered by CI. `scripts/local_models.py`'s `snapshot_download` is not exercised against a real gated repo in CI. None of these paths is instrumented with success-rate or reliability metrics; there is no reliability budget measured here, and none is claimed. The named risks are qualitative failure modes, not measured rates.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `provider_registry.py`'s `PROVIDERS` list first. It is the spine; every other surface in this chapter derives from it, and the fastest way to understand a bug in the env-bridge, the verify probe, or the Settings UI is to check whether the provider's registry entry declares the field that surface consumes. Then read `boot.py::_export_provider_keys_to_env` — it is the Mac↔server unifier, and the "pre-set env wins" rule (`boot.py:189-190`) is what lets one code path serve two credential models. Then read `server.py::_verify_provider_key` for the key-scrubbing discipline (`1922-1926`) — the pattern to preserve is header-auth-over-query-auth and scrub-on-exception.

Drift will most likely appear in four places. First, `config/user-settings.json` on disk carries `capture.default_system_audio` and `whisper.provider`, both absent from `DEFAULTS` (the `default_system_audio` checkbox was wired to nothing and removed 2026-07-01, per the NOTE at `user_settings.py:64-67`; the disk file predates that). The forward-compatible merge preserves them harmlessly, which is exactly why the drift is invisible — a reader comparing `DEFAULTS` to the live file will find keys in the file that the code no longer knows about. Second, `scripts/local_models.py`'s `TIER_RECOMMENDATIONS` catalog still lists Qwen2.5-7B/27B and Meta-Llama-3.1-70B (`scripts/local_models.py:211-296`) — the Qwen2.5/Llama-3.1 era, not the deployed 2026-05-26 vision lineup in `models.json`. The two local-model code paths — install-side selection and runtime discovery — are not reconciled; there is no step that syncs the curated catalog to what is actually installed. Third, any new provider must land in `PROVIDERS` or it silently drops out of the bridge, the verify probe, and the UI at once. Fourth, `local_model_discovery.refresh` rewrites only `local_models` and the timestamp; the `_vision_capable_defaults`, `overhead_reservation_gb`, and `local_model_directory` keys in `models.json` are managed elsewhere and the module does not document where — a reader debugging vision routing should not assume discovery owns those fields.

### Platform compatibility

- **API-key storage backend (`keyring`, `user_settings.set_api_key`/`api_key_present`):** macOS — works (Keychain, exercised). Windows-native — untested (maps to Credential Locker in principle; never run here). WSL — untested. Linux — needs-porting/unknown-untested: `keyring`'s Secret Service backend requires a running keyring daemon and login session a headless server lacks; the server path deliberately uses the plaintext env-file instead, so libsecret is never exercised.
- **Server-side key file `~/.config/ora-server.env` (`chmod 600`, `source` into env; `install-server.sh`):** Linux — works (the deployment target). WSL — works in principle (bash + POSIX perms + `source` apply); not exercised on this machine. macOS — works (POSIX). Windows-native — needs-porting: no `chmod`/POSIX permission bits and no shell `source`; a different secret mechanism would be required.
- **Settings config via `runtime_paths.CONFIG_DIR` (effective `ORA_HOME/config`) and models dir `~/ora/models/`:** macOS — works. Linux — works. WSL — untested. Windows-native — unknown-untested: `Path.home()` is cross-platform, but the hardcoded `~/ora` layout and the absolute `/Users/oracle` paths baked into `models.json` are Mac-shaped. `models.json` is gitignored and machine-specific, so discovery regenerates the absolute paths per host — the paths do not need to be portable, only re-derivable, which the startup rewrite guarantees.
- **Local MLX model inference (running the `models/` contents):** macOS (Apple Silicon) — mac-only; MLX/Metal is Apple-Silicon-exclusive. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — not-applicable: the discovery code merely reads `config.json` + safetensors (platform-neutral), but the models only *run* on Apple Silicon; the Linux server path routes every model call to OpenRouter or direct vendor APIs and hosts no local models.
- **RAM detection `scripts/local_models.py::get_total_ram_gb`:** macOS — works (`sysctl`). Linux — works (`/proc/meminfo`). Windows-native — untested and at risk: uses `wmic`, deprecated on recent Windows. WSL — untested.
- **HF `snapshot_download(local_dir_use_symlinks=False)`:** macOS — works. Linux — works in principle; not exercised in CI. WSL — untested. Windows-native — untested, though disabling symlinks is the correct portability choice for the Windows symlink-permission pitfall; it is simply not run there.
- **`user-settings.json` atomic write (`.json.tmp` + `Path.replace`):** macOS / Linux / WSL / Windows-native — atomic same-filesystem rename on every platform; no `fcntl` locking is used (in-process `threading.Lock` only). Exercised on macOS; the mechanism is stdlib and platform-neutral, but only the macOS path is actually run here.
- **Verify probes (`_verify_provider_key`, live HTTP via `urllib`):** platform-neutral (stdlib `urllib`), so no OS-specific surface — but network-dependent and unmocked; the branch is uninstrumented and untested offline on every platform including macOS.


---

# Part II — The Analytical Core

## 4. Model / Provider Configuration & Routing

### Problem

A single hardcoded model choice cannot span Ora's install spectrum. At one end sits a free-tier-only user with no local hardware, routing every call through rate-limited OpenRouter free endpoints. At the other end sits a power user on a Mac Studio with 128 GB of RAM, running several local MLX models plus a wallet full of paid API keys. Between them are hybrid installs that mix a couple of local models with a Claude or GPT key. A choice that is correct for one of these is wrong — often unusably wrong — for the others.

The problem is worse than "one size doesn't fit all," because a single request is not homogeneous. Ora's adversarial pipeline runs eight steps, and the steps have opposite cost/capability profiles. Prompt cleanup and classification want a cheap 4B model that answers in two seconds. Depth analysis wants a 60B-plus model that can actually execute an adversarial protocol. The breadth analyst wants a model from a *different* training family than depth, so the two do not share the same blind spots. The consolidator and formatter want something in between. If every step draws from the same model, you either overpay for classification or underpower analysis, and you lose the cross-family diversity that makes the adversarial pass worth running.

Four concrete failure modes follow if you do not solve this:

1. **Every model choice becomes a code edit.** Changing which model authors an article, or which model classifies intent, means editing Python. Non-programmer users cannot tune the system at all.
2. **Adversarial diversity is impossible.** With no way to say "breadth must differ from depth," the pipeline silently pairs a model against itself and reports agreement as if it were independent confirmation.
3. **A rate-limited or down provider stalls the pipeline.** One 429 from OpenRouter, or one MLX model that fails to load, and the request hangs or dies with a raw transport error shipped to the user.
4. **Concurrent MLX calls segfault the process.** The MLX runtime on Apple Silicon crashes with SIGSEGV when two threads load or invoke models on the same machine at once. Gear 4 runs depth and breadth in parallel by design. Without serialization, an all-local Gear 4 run kills the server.

A fifth, subtler failure sits underneath all of these: capability metadata drifts. A model's `vision_capable` flag, hand-entered into a config file, goes stale when a provider changes the model. The proof case was `kimi-k2.6`: a wrong `vision_capable: true` in `routing-config.json` routed image turns to a model that silently dropped the image, and the analyst ran blind with no error. Whatever fills each slot, something authoritative has to decide what that model can actually do.

This chapter describes the subsystem that decides which concrete endpoint fills each named pipeline slot — for a given gear, execution context, and user-selected configuration — and dispatches the call while surviving all five failure modes. The eight-step pipeline that consumes these resolved endpoints is covered in the Gear Pipeline & Mode Dispatch chapter; the concurrency primitives are canonicalized in [[Reference — Ora Concurrency Architecture]]; the per-slot Models pane UI is described in [[Reference — Ora Runtime Configuration]].

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

**The decision: named slots decouple role from model; a user-selected named configuration maps each slot to an explicit primary-plus-fallback chain.**

A slot is a role in the pipeline — `depth`, `breadth`, `sidebar`, `consolidator`, `evaluator`, `step1_cleanup`, `classification`, `formatter`, `primary`. The mode file and the gear pipeline reference slots, never models. A named configuration (`config/configurations/<name>.json`) maps each slot's cell to an ordered chain: a `primary` endpoint id plus a `fallback` list of endpoint ids. The router walks that chain in order and returns the first endpoint that survives filtering. This is the Unix principle of indirection — the pipeline calls a name, and a lookup table binds the name to an implementation, so you re-point the binding without touching the caller.

The user picks a configuration. Four presets are defined — **Free**, **Budget**, **Speed**, **Premium** — baked from the model catalog on first Models-pane open (`active_configuration.bake_missing_presets`, `active_configuration.py:272`), plus any custom configurations the user builds. The active choice is a pointer file at `~/ora/data/active-configuration.json`, read on every interactive request (`active_configuration.get_active_name`, `orchestrator/active_configuration.py:52`). `PRESET_ORDER = ["free", "budget", "speed", "premium"]` (`active_configuration.py:615`) is the authoritative list.

At the reviewed commit, only three preset files are materialized on disk — `free.json`, `budget.json`, `premium.json`; `speed.json` is not yet baked. All four presets are defined in `config/configuration-presets.json` (keys `free / budget / speed / premium`), so `bake_missing_presets` will materialize `speed` the next time it runs against a present catalog; it is a defined-but-unbaked preset, not a missing one.

> Drift note, load-bearing: `CLAUDE.md` names the presets "Free / Budget / Optimum / Premium" and cites a "358-model" inventory. The code says `free / budget / speed / premium` and the shipped registry carries `model_count = 337` (`config/model-registry.json`). The Preset Speed Redesign renamed Optimum→Budget and old-Budget→Speed; the `CLAUDE.md` Models-pane paragraph was not updated. Trust the code. This chapter uses `speed`.

**The main alternative rejected: the six-bucket tier system.** The original design (`docs/design-model-configuration.md`, now marked Historical/superseded) sorted every endpoint into one of six tiers — `local-fast`, `local-mid`, `local-premium`, `free`, `mid`, `premium` — and resolved a slot by walking an ordered list of buckets. That abstraction was dissolved. Opaque tier ordering did not match how users reason. A user does not think "give me a local-premium model"; they think "use Qwen 122B for depth, and if that's busy fall back to GLM." The bucket walk hid the specific model behind a tier label, so the Models pane could not show, and the user could not choose, the actual endpoint. Named configurations invert this: the chain names concrete ids, the UI renders them, and the user edits them directly.

**The tradeoff.** Explicit chains are verbose — a `gear4.depth` cell can list six fallback ids — and they can reference an id that is absent from `routing-config.json::endpoints[]`. When a registry refresh reshapes endpoint ids, a configuration written against the old ids silently degrades: the router logs the unknown id and continues the cascade to the next fallback (`router.py:1044-1061`). This is config-vs-catalog drift, and it is the live system's sharpest edge (see Open problems). The bucket system had the opposite property — tiers were stable, so a refresh could not orphan a slot — but it paid for that stability by hiding the model. The team judged explicit-and-fragile better than opaque-and-stable, because the failure is loud in the logs and the fix (`sync_endpoints_from_catalog.py`) is mechanical, whereas the opacity was a permanent usability tax.

**Pre-execution downgrade and current-request recovery are distinct.** When a required slot cannot resolve, the router can try lower gears before model work starts (Gear 4 → 3 → 2 → 1); a `STOP` or exhausted cascade still refuses. Once invocation starts, bounded retries and the selected cell's configured fallback repair that current request. Neither mechanism guarantees an answer: exhausted invocation returns a typed failure, not an error string dressed as successful output.

**Curated registry authoritative for capability flags.** Rather than trust the hand-entered `vision_capable` in `routing-config.json`, an offline pipeline builds `config/model-registry.json` from OpenRouter, LiteLLM, Chatbot Arena, and Artificial Analysis, and `model_registry.overlay_routing_config` merges its capability values onto the endpoint dicts at load (`boot.py:1375`). The registry wins. This closes the `kimi-k2.6` class: a wrong flag in `routing-config.json` cannot propagate into routing because the overlay overwrites it. The tradeoff is a second source of truth that must be refreshed; when the registry is missing or malformed the overlay is a documented no-op and the routing-config values stand.

**Feature-owned provider rows do not create routing.** A loaded trusted feature
may atomically add an owner-bound provider declaration after collision checks.
This makes its no-secret row and keyring presence available to Settings and lets
that feature read only its declared named credential. It does not create a model
endpoint, catalogue entry, preset, or routing fallback; those remain explicit
configuration work in the mechanisms described below.

**Open problems acknowledged in the design:**

- The legacy bucket-walk code still exists in `router.py` (`resolve_endpoint`'s fallback branch at lines 338-392, and `_get_bucket_order` at 1097-1147). It is dead: `routing-config.json` has no `pipelines` block, so `_resolve_config_name` always resolves a configuration name and the bucket branch is never reached. It is a reader trap and a latent bug surface, pending removal (labelled Chunk 12).
- `vision_capable` lives in three places — `routing-config.json` endpoints, `config/models.json`, and the registry overlay — reconciled by `vision_capable_for_endpoint`. The source-of-truth consolidation is incomplete.
- Non-Mac support is untested end to end (see Platform compatibility).

### Implementation

**Entry points.** Two callers reach the router. Project tools call `model_dispatch.invoke_chat(system_prompt, user_prompt, slot=..., context=...)` (`orchestrator/model_dispatch.py:42`) — the stable, project-agnostic public API; MSI's `article_generator.py` is the reference consumer. The gear pipeline inside `boot.py` calls `get_slot_endpoint(config, slot, context, config_name)` (`boot.py:1512`) and, for the depth/breadth pair, `resolve_gear4_endpoints` (`boot.py:1593`). Both paths converge on the singleton `Router`.

**The singleton and its load.** `boot._get_router()` (`boot.py:1389`) builds one `Router` and caches it; a failed load is marked `False` and not retried until `reload_router` clears the marker. On construction the Router reads `routing-config.json`, then does three things that matter (`router.py:_build_lookup_tables`, 145):

1. Indexes endpoints by id.
2. Merges discovered local models from `config/models.json` into the in-memory endpoint table (`_merge_models_json_local_endpoints`, 165). This is the seam that lets a model you picked in the Models pane actually resolve at dispatch time even when `routing-config.json` has drifted behind the discovered set. It is runtime-only; discovery remains the source of truth for the installed local set. An entry's `status` becomes `active` and `enabled` becomes true only when its `model_path` exists on disk (`router.py:203-204`) — a model listed but not present cannot be dispatched.
3. Builds case-insensitive endpoint aliases (`_build_endpoint_aliases`, 218). The sharp edge is MiniMax: the catalog id is `minimax/minimax-m3` but the direct-provider endpoint registers as `minimax/MiniMax-M3`. Configurations get edited from both sides, so a case-equivalent id resolves instead of falling through the chain.

`load_routing_config` (`boot.py:1342`) is separate from the Router and is what `invoke_chat` calls; it reads the file and applies the registry overlay before handing the config to `get_slot_endpoint`.

**Active-configuration precedence.** `_resolve_config_name(config_name, context)` (`router.py:728`) derives the effective configuration with this precedence, most-specific first:

1. An explicit `config_name` passed by the caller (a per-run override) wins over everything.
2. For `interactive` context: the active project's `default_model_profile`, if set and resolvable, overrides the account-wide default — a project default is "closer" than the global one. Best-effort; a failure never breaks chat.
3. Otherwise the account-wide active pointer, `active_configuration.get_active_name()`.
4. For `agent` / `autonomous` context: the static map `DEFAULT_CONFIG_FOR_CONTEXT` (`router.py:45`) — both resolve to `background-default`. Automated processes are not user-steered through the active pointer.

The operative distinction here is foreground vs. background execution: interactive chat consults the user's live selection; a background pipeline run resolves against a fixed default. This is not "manual vs. agentic" — both run the same resolver; they differ only in whether a human is currently steering the pointer.

**The chain walk.** `_resolve_from_configuration` (`router.py:995`) is the live resolution path. It loads and caches the named configuration, maps the slot to a cell path via `_slot_to_cell_path` (`router.py:811`), and walks `primary` then each `fallback` id. For each candidate it applies, in order:

- **Unknown-id skip.** If the id has no matching endpoint, log and continue to the next fallback (the config-vs-catalog drift path, 1044-1061).
- **Enabled / active filter.** Skip endpoints that are disabled or not `status == "active"`.
- **Exclusion filter.** Skip ids already assigned this gear (used for diversity).
- **Same-machine block.** Skip a local endpoint on a machine already committed to another local model this gear — the MLX parallel constraint.
- **Mutex skip.** If a local endpoint's per-machine MLX mutex is currently held, skip it in favour of the next chain entry. If every remaining entry is ineligible and the only survivors are busy locals, return the first busy local so the caller's `call_model` queues at the mutex — the "no API fallback, so we wait" path (`first_busy_local`, 1041/1093).
- **Circuit-breaker skip.** If `endpoint_health.is_in_cooldown(id)` is true, skip (1088).

The first candidate that survives all filters is returned as a v2 endpoint dict, converted to the v1 shape `call_model` expects by `_to_v1_endpoint` (`router.py:1149`).

**Slot-to-cell mapping (current Model boundary).** Utility slots map to `cells.utility.<slot>`; `sidebar` aliases `utility.step1_cleanup`. Analysis runs use the exact `cells.analysis.gear3` or `gear4` depth/breadth cell. Consolidation, verification, and formatting use their separate `post_analysis` cells. The generic `primary` alias reads `gear3.depth`, but the direct Gear-2 runner does not use it: `resolve_single_pass_endpoint` returns an endpoint together with its actual cell identity. Gear 1 uses `classification`; Gear 2 first resolves `fast` to `utility.gear2_rag_lookup`, then may resolve `utility.step1_cleanup` within the same named configuration. Only an unnamed legacy request may finally use the active endpoint.

**Current-request Gear-1/2 fallback.** `run_single_pass_with_tools` preserves that actual cell, gear, configuration, and vision requirement. Each endpoint receives one same-model retry on transport failure, error output, or empty output; if still unhealthy, the exact cell chain advances with all attempted endpoint IDs excluded, so it cannot cycle back. The direct-call minimum is one character after cleanup: a substantive one-word answer is valid. Successful output carries the attempted and selected endpoint/model identities. Exhaustion raises `ModelInvocationFailure` before completed output routing or persistence.

**Gear cascade and diversity.** `execute` (`router.py:476`) tries the requested gear, calls `resolve_gear` for every slot the gear needs, and on any unfilled slot drops a gear and retries. `resolve_gear` (394) applies adversarial diversity: for the `breadth` slot it excludes the `depth` id so breadth lands on a different model. When diversity is enforced it deliberately does *not* fall back to the same model — it lets the cascade find a wider pool at a lower gear instead. A per-config `diversity_override: false` disables this, for single-model demo configurations like `qwen-9b-only` where every slot is intentionally the same 9B endpoint (proving Ora's pipeline lifts one small model); without the override, gear 4 would downgrade all the way to gear 2 because breadth could never differ from depth.

**Effective gear is separate from parallel safety (current Model boundary).** `Gear4EndpointResolution` carries both `effective_gear` and `parallel_safe`. A router-selected effective Gear 3 delegates to `run_gear3`; `parallel_safe=false` alone does not. Serialized Gear 4 still runs the complete two-lane Gear-4 contract. The per-machine local-model mutex remains the GPU serialization boundary.

**Current RAM and selected-endpoint cache protection.** The resilience probes return `None` for failed, malformed, or unsupported RAM readings; they never substitute 128 GB. The resource assessment reports that uncertainty. Unknown RAM or model-memory facts serialize the local analyst pair without changing Gear 4 or authorizing eviction. Before each analyst attempt, including retries, fallback, and supplemental calls, the admission guard checks the actual endpoint against active calls and earlier local attempts, using `ram_resident_gb` plus `ram_overhead_gb` and current available RAM, not legacy slot estimates. It waits for conflicting active calls and requires release of an earlier local model only when two distinct local identities have a known combined requirement above 80% of available RAM. Failed local attempts remain possible cache residents even if that lane later recovers on an API. MLX removes cached entries matching the model path; an already-absent entry is a successful release. Ollama sends that model's unload request to its selected server. Failed required release produces a typed `resource_release_failed` refusal before the unsafe attempt; existing configured recovery may still use a safe alternative, such as an API. Unrecovered release failure cannot be bypassed by downgrading to Gear 3, and the admission guard remains active for later Gear-4 stages and retries after a release failure. API pairs, same-model pairs, unknown facts, and below-threshold pairs do not trigger cache release.

**Dispatch.** `call_model(messages, endpoint, images)` (`boot.py:13043`) routes on `endpoint["type"]`:

- **api** — wrapped in `mlx_mutex.track_api_call(id)`, a non-blocking in-flight counter for observability plus an optional sized concurrency cap. It does not block on contention; API providers handle their own concurrency.
- **local** — wrapped in the blocking `mlx_mutex.acquire(machine_id)` (`boot.py:13077`), so only one local call per machine runs at a time. This is the SIGSEGV guard. `call_local_endpoint` (`boot.py:14178`) then resolves the engine: the endpoint's `engine` field defaults to `ollama`, an explicit `engine` is honoured, and `engine == "auto"` resolves to `mlx` only when `platform.system() == "Darwin"` and `platform.machine() == "arm64"`, otherwise `ollama` (14185-14189). MLX runs in-process via `mlx_lm` with a module-level model cache; Ollama posts to `localhost:11434`.
- **anything else** — returns `"[Error] Unknown endpoint type"`.

**Failure behavior feeds the circuit breaker.** `call_model` reads the return string and records the outcome to `endpoint_health` (`boot.py:13093-13105`). A response starting with `"[Error"` is a failure; crucially, an *empty* response is also a failure (13099) — the `kimi-k2.6` proof case returned `content=None` with no exception, and treating empty as success meant the breaker never tripped and the router kept dispatching to the broken model. `endpoint_health` (`orchestrator/endpoint_health.py`) is a per-endpoint circuit breaker: **3 failures within a 60-second rolling window trips a 300-second cooldown** (`_failure_threshold = 3`, `_failure_window_seconds = 60.0`, `_cooldown_seconds = 300.0`, lines 47-49). During cooldown the router's chain walk skips the endpoint. After cooldown the next call is allowed through as a half-open probe; success clears the breaker, failure re-extends the cooldown (`is_in_cooldown`, 110). These three numbers are the trip mechanism, not a reliability budget — nothing in the code measures achieved success rates; the failure counts and cooldown are the only instrumented health signal, and all three are tunable via `configure()`.

**When resolution or invocation fails, the caller refuses honestly.** `get_slot_endpoint` returns `None` for an exhausted named chain rather than selecting the first active catalog entry. Required Gear-3/4 endpoint failures and exhausted invocation attempts are typed failures. `ModelInvocationOutcome` remains text-compatible but carries attempted/selected endpoint and model identities, substitution state, and any typed failure; a failed outcome contains no candidate prose. Provider exceptions and error/empty responses therefore cannot become analysis, critique, consolidated text, or a completed answer just because they were wrapped in a string. The low-level provider/circuit-breaker convention above remains internal to these call boundaries.

A successful identity-changing recovery records one `model_substitution` warning per request context, with attempted and selected identities; same-model retries record none, even if the provider channel changed. Gear-4 outcomes are aggregated on the request thread after worker completion so the ordinary response collector receives the warning. Failure of the health surface logs loudly and does not discard a usable result.

**Startup rewrite of the local set.** At server boot (`server/app.py:15009`), `local_model_discovery.refresh(write=True)` scans `~/ora/models/` for subdirectories with a valid MLX `config.json` and `.safetensors` files, derives id / ram_gb / type / vision_capable / recommended_roles, and rewrites `config/models.json`'s `local_models` array — preserving `commercial_models` and every other key. A no-shrink guard refuses to write whenever discovery finds fewer models than the file already records (`local_model_discovery.py::refresh`), protecting against a momentarily unmounted or half-populated models directory; deliberate removal passes `allow_shrink=True`. `active_configuration.bake_missing_presets()` creates any missing preset configs on Models-pane open.

**The offline registry pipeline.** Separate from per-request routing, a refresh pipeline builds the capability data. `scripts/sync_model_registry.py` (the 124KB primary builder) pulls OpenRouter + LiteLLM + Arena + Artificial Analysis and writes `model-registry.json`; `refresh-catalog.py`, `refresh-openrouter.py`, and `refresh-direct-apis.py` refresh the catalog and provider listings; `sync_endpoints_from_catalog.py` keeps `routing-config.json::endpoints[]` in step with the ids configurations reference. These are triggered from the server (`/api/model-registry/refresh`, `/reach/start|status`) or run as CLI. `check-model-refresh-drift.py` is a CI guard that fails if a refresh run dirtied tracked seed files.

**Live config edits reach the singleton without restart.** The V3 Models pane POSTs to `server/app.py` (`/api/configurations/*`, `/config/routing/slots`), which write the configuration/routing files and call `boot.reload_router()` (`boot.py:1406`) plus the `active_configuration` setters. `router.reload()` (`router.py:265`) re-reads the file and rebuilds the lookup tables, clearing the configuration cache so edits take effect on the next call; on a read/parse failure it keeps the prior in-memory config so the running pipeline does not degrade.

**Protected Model Profile deletion (current boundary).** The four presets and the reserved `user-pipeline`/`background-default` profiles are refused. A custom deletion first requires a regular non-symlink target and a readable valid active pointer. Deleting the active custom additionally binds the existing regular Free profile and validates its current allocation shape; this is not a live reachability or complete-fallback qualification. The route approves those exact target/pointer/Free identities. The sink repeats the full preflight under the existing process lock and target, pointer, and applicable Free file locks before unlinking the target and atomically writing the pointer to `free`. Removal and pointer fallback share one protected receipt scope. A failure records actual post-state; there is no rollback if deletion succeeded before a later pointer write failed.

**Config surfaces.** `config/routing-config.json` (endpoints, machines, capability slots including `slots.vision_input`, diversity, and the legacy `slot_assignments`/`gear4_overrides`/`buckets` fields); `config/configurations/<name>.json`; `config/models.json` (gitignored, machine-specific; auto-managed `local_models` + hand-kept `commercial_models`); `config/model-registry.json` and `config/model-catalog.json`; `config/configuration-presets.json` (preset algorithm params); `config/family-classification.json` (same-family warning). Environment: `ORA_HOME`, `ORA_API_POOL_SIZE`, and `install-state.json::profile` for API-pool sizing (Solo `0` = uncapped, Hybrid 8, Organization 32; `mlx_mutex.PROFILE_API_POOL_SIZES`, 62).

**Vision fallback.** `resolve_vision_fallback` (`router.py:913`) is the resolution path for an image-bearing turn where the slot must be able to read the image. It checks a global `slots.vision_input` chain (`_vision_input_chain`) first; when that chain is configured it *is* the vision fallback and the method refuses to advance into the slot's generic chain, because a `vision_capable` flag is not sufficient to trust an entry — image *delivery* is per-service, and the 2026-06-01 evaluation surfaced a breadth entry that was flagged vision-capable but whose service path never attached the image. When no global chain is present it falls back to the cell's per-cell `vision_substitute` field (the legacy path, still in the code). The two coexist; the global slot takes precedence but has not fully replaced `vision_substitute`.

### Evidence & Verification

**Unit tests** live under `orchestrator/tests/` and run with:

```
cd ~/ora && /opt/homebrew/bin/python3 -m unittest discover -s orchestrator/tests
```

The routing-relevant suites, all present in the tree: `test_model_router` (core slot resolution + gear cascade), `test_router_config_name` (named-config resolution + precedence), `test_router_chain_walk` (fallback-chain reporting + circuit-breaker state in the refusal path), `test_router_vision_input` (global vision-input slot), `test_router_reload` (reload keeps prior config on failure), `test_slot_inheritance` (cell inheritance), `test_active_configuration` (63 test methods: presets, active pointer, slot setters, lifecycle), `test_model_registry` + `test_runtime_configuration_overlay` (registry overlay + `compute_picks`), `test_model_dispatch` (`invoke_chat` typed errors), `test_mlx_mutex` + `test_call_model_mutex` (per-machine mutex, API pool, `call_model` integration), `test_endpoint_health` (cooldown/probe breaker), `test_local_model_discovery` + `test_chunk8_local_models` (MLX scan + rewrite safety guard), `test_provider_registry`, `test_direct_catalog`, `test_vendor_catalog_registry`, `test_sync_endpoints_vendor_auth`, `test_refresh_catalog`, `test_refresh_openrouter`, `test_visual_routing` + `test_routing_slots_endpoint` (capability-slot routing + Models-pane endpoints).

**How to confirm resolution by hand.** `model_dispatch.get_slot_info(slot, context=...)` (`model_dispatch.py:135`) returns the endpoint a slot would resolve to without spending a call — the fastest way to see what a configuration actually binds. The circuit-breaker state for any endpoint is readable via `endpoint_health`. To confirm the local set, `local_model_discovery.refresh()` runs dry (no `write=True`) and prints what it found.

**Instrumented facts, verified against the installed tree at 7a5e8f40.** `config/model-registry.json`: `model_count = 337`, `generated_at = 2026-06-15`, `last_probe_at = null`. `config/routing-config.json`: 547 endpoints, one machine (`studio-128`), no `pipelines` block (confirming the bucket-walk path is dead), `buckets` key still present (the dead data). `config/configurations/`: 8 files — `free`, `budget`, `premium` (the three baked presets; `speed` was defined in `configuration-presets.json` but not yet baked — since then the installer bakes all four during step 7, so a fresh install has no unbaked preset), plus `user-pipeline`, `background-default`, `background-speed`, `qwen-9b-only`, and one `experiment-optimum-premium-consolidator`. (There are no `campaign-*` configuration files in the committed tree.) `active_configuration.PRESET_ORDER = ["free", "budget", "speed", "premium"]`.

**Explicitly untested / unverified surfaces:**
