- **End-to-end resolution off macOS.** The Ollama fallback and the pure-API path have no non-Mac test evidence. Every "works" claim in the Platform matrix that is not macOS is "untested," not "broken."
- **Keyring off-Mac.** `user_settings` stores keys via the `keyring` library under service `"ora"`; only the macOS Keychain backend has been exercised.
- **The empirical reach-probe has not run on the shipped registry.** `last_probe_at = null` means the probe that would populate per-endpoint measured latency/reachability has not been run against this registry; the circuit breaker is the only runtime health signal.
- **`direct_catalog` and `vendor_catalog_registry` gates default ON but their live consumption is unverified.** `direct_catalog._enabled()` reads `ORA_DIRECT_CATALOG` defaulting `"1"` (ON), and `vendor_catalog_registry.enabled()` reads its flag defaulting `"1"` (ON). Both are labelled *partial* here not because a flag defaults off, but because they feed the *offline* registry-build pipeline and their runtime effect on live routing was not traced line-by-line; the AA/Arena/LiteLLM fetch logic inside the 124KB `sync_model_registry.py` was verified only at the wiring/subcommand level.
- **`call_api_endpoint` provider-specific request building** (`boot.py:13404`) was not traced per service; the per-service image-delivery claim in `resolve_vision_fallback`'s docstring is taken at its word.
- **The committed config JSONs are authoritative here; the live working tree drifts from them.** A clean checkout of 7a5e8f40 is clean — the committed `model-registry.json` / `model-catalog.json` / `routing-config.json` are what the figures above are read from. On the author's running machine those same files show as `M` (modified) because the catalog/registry refresh rewrites them at runtime; that auto-managed working-tree drift is machine-local and not part of the commit, so this document reports the committed values, not the dirty working copy.

### Expert Reading Notes

**Read in this order.** Start with `router._resolve_from_configuration` (`router.py:995`) and `_slot_to_cell_path` (811) — this is the entire live resolution path in two functions. Then `_resolve_config_name` (728) for the active-config precedence. Then `boot.get_slot_endpoint` (1512) for the v1-slot → v2 mapping, including the `formatter` branch and the single-slot depth/breadth lookup that tries the gear-4 cell before gear-3. Then `boot.call_model` (13043) and `call_local_endpoint` (14178) for dispatch, the mutex acquisition, and the `engine == "auto"` platform gate.

**Ignore the bucket-walk path.** `router.py` lines 338-392 (`resolve_endpoint`'s fallback branch) and 1097-1147 (`_get_bucket_order`) reference `self._buckets` and `pipelines[context]`, which no longer exist in the config. In production these return empty/None and are never reached, because `_resolve_config_name` always resolves a name. Do not mistake them for live logic. The module docstring still describes the router as "bucket-based" — that docstring is stale; the live engine is named-configuration cell chains.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift:**

1. **Config-vs-catalog id drift.** A configuration primary or fallback that references an id absent from `routing-config.json::endpoints[]` is silently skipped and logged (`router.py:1044-1061`). A registry/endpoint refresh that reshapes ids can quietly degrade a preset's chain. `sync_endpoints_from_catalog.py` is the coupling that must hold; if you change how ids are minted, run it and re-check the presets.
2. **Three `vision_capable` sources.** `routing-config.json` endpoints, `config/models.json`, and the registry overlay all carry it, reconciled by `vision_capable_for_endpoint` (`router.py:688`, `boot.py:1443`). Change one, check the other two. `models.json` is the intended source of truth; the migration is incomplete.
3. **The slot-to-cell contract is duplicated.** `router._slot_to_cell_path` and `boot.get_slot_endpoint`'s slot dispatch must agree on the slot vocabulary. Add a slot in one place only and it silently resolves to the wrong cell.
4. **Single-machine assumption.** `DEFAULT_MACHINE_ID = "studio-128"` is hardcoded in `router.py:41`. Multi-machine Bonjour discovery from the design doc was never built. Any local endpoint without an explicit `machine` is assumed to be on the one Studio.
5. **`CLAUDE.md` is stale on preset names and model count** (Optimum vs Speed; 358 vs 337) and still frames routing as bucket-based ("the classification slot resolves from the local-fast bucket"). Trust `active_configuration.PRESET_ORDER`, `config/model-registry.json::model_count`, and the named-configuration code, not that prose.

### Platform compatibility

Ora is developed and run on a single Apple Silicon Mac Studio. Every "works" below means "exercised on macOS." Non-Mac surfaces are marked from the code's platform gates and the absence of off-Mac test evidence — not from having been run.

| Subsystem | macOS | Windows-native | WSL | Linux |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **MLX local inference** (`call_local_endpoint` engine=`mlx`/`auto`) | works | n/a | n/a | n/a |
| **Ollama local fallback** (`localhost:11434`) | works | untested | untested | untested |
| **`mlx_mutex` SIGSEGV serialization** | works | n/a | n/a | n/a |
| **API-only / cloud routing** (OpenRouter + direct APIs) | works | untested | untested | untested |
| **API key storage** (`keyring`, service `"ora"`) | works | untested | needs-porting | untested |
| **Config/model paths** (`~/ora`, `~/ora/models` scan) | works | needs-porting | untested | untested |
| **Local-model discovery scan** (`.safetensors` glob) | works | untested | untested | untested |
| **Server startup + refresh subprocesses** | works | untested | untested | untested |
| **MLX heartbeat + machine id `studio-128`** | works | n/a | n/a | n/a |

Per-item notes:

- **MLX** runs in-process via `mlx_lm` against Apple Metal and is Apple-Silicon-only. `engine == "auto"` resolves to `mlx` only under `Darwin` + `arm64` (`boot.py:14185-14189`); everywhere else `auto` resolves to `ollama`. Marked **n/a** off-Mac because MLX cannot run there at all.
- **Ollama** is the only local path on non-Apple hardware. It is plain HTTP to `localhost:11434` (`call_local_endpoint`) and cross-platform in principle, but has no off-Mac test evidence. Marked **untested**, not "works."
- **`mlx_mutex`** guards the MLX Metal segfault. It is irrelevant where MLX is not the engine — an API-only install never takes the blocking `acquire`. Marked **n/a** off-Mac because there is no MLX to serialize.
- **API-only routing** is pure HTTP plus keyring — the zero-local-hardware path, and the most portable in principle. It is nonetheless **untested** off macOS.
- **Keyring** uses the `keyring` library: macOS Keychain on Mac; Secret Service (Linux) or Windows Credential Locker elsewhere in principle. WSL headless typically lacks a Secret Service backend, so it is marked **needs-porting**; native Windows and Linux are **untested**.
- **Paths** use POSIX `~` and forward slashes throughout (`Path.home()/'ora'/'models'`, `ORA_HOME` defaulting `~/ora`), with no `%USERPROFILE%` handling. Native Windows is **needs-porting**.
- **Local-model discovery** is a filesystem glob with no Apple calls, so it is portable in principle — but only MLX models are useful output, and MLX is Mac-only, so the scan finding anything usable off-Mac is moot. Marked **untested**.
- **Refresh subprocesses** are documented against `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3`, a Homebrew-specific interpreter path. **Untested** off-Mac.
- **`studio-128`** is `DEFAULT_MACHINE_ID`, a single hardcoded Mac Studio. Multi-machine discovery was not built, so the machine layer is **n/a** off the one supported machine.

## 5. Gear Pipeline & Mode Dispatch

### Problem

A single language model cannot reliably catch its own errors. The model that fabricates a citation, misframes a decision, or quietly answers a different question than the one asked will not flag that mistake on self-review. The blind spots are correlated: the same weights that produced the error also grade it, so "check your work" returns the same wrong answer with more confidence. This is the failure mode the whole subsystem exists to prevent — an analysis that is wrong in a way its own author cannot see.

There is a second, cheaper failure. If every prompt runs the same pipeline, a greeting, a capital-city lookup, and a wicked-problem analysis all pay the same latency and cost. A five-to-fifteen-minute adversarial cascade on "hello" is not thoroughness; it is waste. And routing that guesses wrong in the other direction is worse: an ambiguous prompt either interrogates the user with clarifying questions they already answered, or silently commits to the wrong analytical frame and produces a confident answer to the wrong problem.

So the subsystem has two jobs that pull against each other. It must spend heavily where judgment is required and independent review actually breaks correlated errors, and it must spend nothing where the prompt is trivial or already unambiguous. The routing decision that separates those cases has to be predictable — a routing layer that is itself a non-deterministic model call reintroduces exactly the unreliability it is meant to contain.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

Two coupled moves answer those two problems.

**Deterministic mode-and-gear routing.** A four-stage pre-routing pipeline classifies each prompt to one of 64 analytical modes across 21 territories (T1–T21, verified in `architecture/territories.md`), and the selected mode file's `## DEFAULT GEAR` heading — not a model's opinion — fixes the pipeline shape. "Gear" is the pipeline's cost/quality tier: Gear 1 is a direct single-model pass with no retrieval; Gear 2 is a single pass with RAG for factual lookups; Gear 3 is a sequential adversarial cascade on two models; Gear 4 is the parallel adversarial cascade. The distribution across the installed 64 modes is concrete and lopsided: 1 mode declares Gear 1, 2 declare Gear 2, 3 declare Gear 3, and 58 declare Gear 4 (confirmed by grep over `modes/*.md`). Judgment-heavy work is the common case; the cheap gears are the exceptions that must opt out explicitly.

The rejected alternative was the retired Mode Classification Directory, which ran an LLM intent-classification pass over a flat mode list (`CLAUDE.md` records it archived 2026-05-02, Phase 9). It was brittle and non-deterministic: the same prompt could route two ways on two runs, and a misclassification was invisible. The pre-routing pipeline replaces that with substring matching against a hand-maintained signal-vocabulary registry. The tradeoff is honest and named in the spec: substring matching is fast and reproducible, but it needs a vocabulary registry someone has to keep current. A signal the registry does not know about does not route. This buys a predictable, reproducible mechanism at the cost of a maintenance burden — the deliberate trade of a dumb transparent mechanism over a clever opaque one.

**Independent-model adversarial review.** For the 58 Gear-4 modes, two analysts — a depth stream and a breadth stream, ideally on different model families for genuine independence — analyze in parallel, then cross-evaluate, revise against each other's critique, and verify each other's revised drafts before a consolidator and formatter produce the deliverable. The reason is the Problem above: independent review catches what self-review cannot, because a second model does not share the first's correlated blind spot.

The rejected alternative was single-model-plus-self-critique — one model that drafts and then critiques its own draft. It is roughly half the cost. It was rejected because it does not break correlated errors; the self-critique inherits the same blind spot that produced the error. The tradeoff for choosing real adversarial independence is steep and stated plainly in `architecture/pre-routing-pipeline.md`: five to fifteen minutes of wall-clock latency and roughly 2x model cost for a Gear-4 turn.

**Open problems.** Bounded retries, configured endpoint advancement, and the circuit breaker can recover transient model failures and provider outages, but do not guarantee availability when the permitted chain is exhausted. The verifier is a gate, not an editor: a correction it computes (a fixed date, a fixed citation) is captured in the trace but never reaches the user, because the consolidator and formatter read the reviser's draft directly — an acknowledged architectural gap, not a bug (detailed below). And the routing layer's determinism is only as good as the signal-vocabulary registry; an unrecognized phrasing falls through to a T0 catch-all rather than the specific mode its author intended.

### Implementation

The runtime entrypoint is `boot.py::run_pipeline` (line 9053). The flow, in order: a risk gate, then slash-command and runtime short-circuits (the meta-layer `/instance`, `/render`, `/framework` commands intercept here before any model call), then Phase A prompt cleanup, then `run_pre_routing_pipeline` (line 5513), then the gear-dispatch switch (lines 9338–9394).

**The four stages.** `run_pre_routing_pipeline` runs Stages 1–3; Stage 4 is the gear execution that follows.

- *Stage 1 — pre-analysis filter* (`stage1_pre_analysis_filter`, line 3760). Substring-matches the prompt against the signal vocabulary and a short bypass list. Greetings and prior-conversation references set `bypass_to_direct_response` and the pipeline returns immediately with no mode (line 5553). A distinct branch handles Gear-2 RAG dispatch: an information request carrying a `GEAR2_RAG_TRIGGERS` substring (the trigger list is defined at line 2428) and no judgment marker skips Stage 2 entirely and dispatches straight to the `factual-lookup` mode (the `gear2_rag_dispatch` branch, line 5572). The mode file's own `## DEFAULT GEAR` supplies Gear 2; the dispatcher honours it.
- *Stage 2 — sufficiency analyzer* (`stage2_sufficiency_analyzer`, line 4241). Classifies signals strong versus weak, AND-composes them, checks cross-territory adjacency and within-territory disambiguation, and either dispatches a `mode_id` or surfaces exactly one plain-language disambiguation question. When Stage 2 finds no specific mode *and* no disambiguation conflict, it does not interrogate the user; it routes to a T0 catch-all — `subjective-inquiry` if `_has_subjective_marker` fires, else `general-inquiry`, both Gear 3 (fallback branch, lines 5599–5624). The universal adversarial scaffolding carries the discipline; the mode-specific layer is deliberately thin.
- *Stage 3 — input completeness check* (`stage3_input_completeness_check`, line 5355). Loads the dispatched mode's input contract, picks expert versus accessible framing, and either elicits a missing required field or degrades to a lighter sibling mode.
- *Stage 4 — mode execution.* `extract_default_gear` reads `## DEFAULT GEAR`; a missing heading defaults to **Gear 3**, not Gear 2. Gear 1 resolves classification, and Gear 2 resolves the dedicated RAG-lookup cell or its same-configuration cleanup fallback, carrying the actual cell through current-request retries (Chapter 4). Gear 3 calls `run_gear3`; Gear 4 calls `run_gear4`, which distinguishes a real router-selected effective Gear 3 from merely serialized Gear 4. There is no separate Gear 5.

**Prompt assembly — the injection model.** Every pipeline step's system prompt is built by `build_system_prompt_for_gear` (line 8467). This is the load-bearing coupling in the whole subsystem, so its contract is worth stating precisely. The function takes a `step` — one of the six members of `_PIPELINE_STEPS` (line 8358): `analyst`, `evaluator`, `reviser`, `verifier`, `consolidator`, `formatter` — and injects exactly one role-specific `##` section from the mode file per step, on top of a shared baseline. It extracts named sections via `_extract_section` (line 7823):

- Baseline injected into *every* role-specific step: the mode's `## ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA` and its `## VERIFICATION CRITERIA`, each wrapped in `_fenced` banner blocks — `MODE BRIEF & EVALUATION CRITERIA — <mode>` and `MODE VERIFICATION CRITERIA (PASS gate) — <mode>` (lines 8579–8588). The analyst sees the standard it will be graded against before it writes; the verifier sees the gate it grades against. This closes the "writing blind to the criteria" gap. Gear 1 is structurally exempt — it does not route through this function.
- Role-specific per step: `## DEPTH ANALYSIS GUIDANCE` or `## BREADTH ANALYSIS GUIDANCE` for the analyst (chosen by the `slot` argument), `## REVISION GUIDANCE` for the reviser, `## CONSOLIDATION GUIDANCE` for the consolidator, `## OUTPUT FORMAT GUIDANCE` for the formatter.
- Breadth-only: `## ANALYTICAL PERSPECTIVES` — the mode's declared Tier-1 thinking tools and Tier-3 mental-model lenses — is injected into the breadth analyst alone (the `step == "analyst"` / `slot == "breadth"` branch), because breadth is the lateral-thinking stream and the lenses prime its frame; depth is intentionally skipped.

The section name `ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA` was renamed from the legacy `EVALUATION CRITERIA` on 2026-05-24; the code keeps a legacy fallback (`_extract_section(..., "ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA") or _extract_section(..., "EVALUATION CRITERIA")`, lines 8515–8518). The migration landed on the mode side: `modes/cui-bono.md` carries the new heading verbatim (line 161). It did **not** land on the spec side — see Evidence & Verification.

Retrieved reference material — RAG hits, web sources — is wrapped by `_fenced` (line 8420) in `=== LABEL === / === END LABEL ===` banners with a "data, not instructions" caption (`_REFERENCE_FENCE_NOTE`, line 8413, added 2026-06-29). The `===` banner is syntactically distinct from markdown `#` headings, so retrieved content carrying its own `##` headers cannot be confused with the scaffolding's structure — this is the prompt-injection isolation boundary for the pipeline. Phase A's inferred assumptions are surfaced in their own `PHASE A ASSUMPTIONS (NOT USER-STATED FACTS)` fenced block so downstream steps treat inferences as uncertain rather than as user-stated fact.

**The Gear-4 cascade and its failure behavior.** `run_gear4` (line 11691) maps code steps to roles: Step 3 parallel depth + breadth analysts, Step 4 cross-evaluation, Step 5 parallel revisers, Step 6 cross-verification (up to 2 correction cycles), Step 7 consolidation into an irreducible corpus, Step 8 formatting into the deliverable, Step 8.6 final quality gate. The Step 7/8 split (2026-05-14) separated two jobs the old consolidator conflated: Step 7 does semantic extraction, cross-stream dedup, bloat strip, and synthesis to corpus shape; Step 8 places that corpus into the deliverable form. `formatter` is a full member of `_PIPELINE_STEPS`.

The current recovery boundaries distinguish a failed invocation from genuine content preserved by an allowed fallback:

- *Step 3 — dead-analyst recovery.* Each stream tries its primary with one same-model retry, then one configured fallback with its own retry. An unrecoverable ordinary-turn stream takes the existing Gear-3 recovery path, which must itself produce genuine output; strict Framework execution refuses a missing required lane. No error wrapper is cross-evaluated as an analyst draft.
- *Step 4 — required usable evaluation.* Evaluator calls retain bounded retry/fallback, but an exhausted failure stops the stage. The success guards run before the historical no-feedback fallback blocks, so those blocks do not turn a failed invocation into completed review.
- *Step 5 — reviser recovery.* Reviser calls retain bounded retry/fallback. After exhaustion, ordinary Gear-3/4 turns can preserve that stream's healthy original analyst draft in the expected revision envelope, with the failed revision and unapplied critique explicitly recorded for downstream verification. Strict Framework execution stops before this fallback. Preserving a genuine draft does not promote diagnostic text into an answer or claim that revision succeeded.
- *Step 6 — three-way verdict.* PASS / FAIL / BROKEN. PASS and BROKEN both unblock the cycle (re-revision cannot fix a verifier that itself errored); only FAIL triggers re-revision, capped at 2 cycles. BROKEN never counts as real verification — a `step6-cycleN-<slot>-verifier-BROKEN-not-verified` contingency lands in the health record so trend data reflects how often verification actually happened. This replaced a retired auto-PASS-on-exception path (the docstring labels it "silent failure #9").
- *Step 7 — substantive consolidation.* The consolidator uses its configured post-analysis cell, bounded recovery, and a 30-character stage floor. Exhausted failure raises before the old longer-draft fallback block; it does not relabel one stream as completed consolidation.
- *Step 8 / 8.5 — formatting and leak scrub.* Required formatter calls and corrective formatter calls must return usable outcomes; exhaustion stops them before failed text can enter delivery. The structural-leak gate retries a process-meta leak and Step 8.5 deterministically strips residual pipeline-leak lines.
- *Step 8.6 (Gear 4) / 6.5 (Gear 3) — final quality gate.* The verification-slot judge grades a usable candidate against the mode and output contracts. Gear 4 permits one redo per implicated producer kind (ANALYSIS reconsolidates then reformats; FORMATTING reformats), with reinspection; Gear 3 has its bounded revision redo. PASS confirms the inspected candidate. A broken judge is a typed failed call, not verification success. Ordinary turns preserve a substantive candidate in full on unresolved FAIL/BROKEN and mark it unverified with a warning where its format permits; structured openings remain byte-identical, and status still records shipped-with-warning. Strict Framework execution instead withholds the candidate. Neither path promotes the judge's failure text into the deliverable.

`run_pipeline` can prepend the resource-degradation signal. Model substitution is separately collected once on the request thread after worker outcomes return (Chapter 4). Parallel execution uses `ThreadPoolExecutor`, while the per-machine mutex and the selected-endpoint cache decision may serialize local work without changing Gear 4. Only a true effective-gear selection or the explicit unrecoverable-analyst recovery path invokes Gear 3; a scheduling hint alone cannot do so.

**Config surfaces.** `config/routing-config.json` is the single source of truth for endpoints, capability routing, `slot_assignments`, and gear-4 overrides. The `slot_assignments` block names eight gear-independent model slots — `depth`, `breadth`, `evaluator`, `consolidator`, `classification`, `step1_cleanup`, `sidebar`, and `rag_planner` — and any model can fill any slot. Note that there is no `verifier` slot and no `formatter` slot in `slot_assignments`: the verifier and the quality-gate judge resolve to the post-analysis `verification` slot through the router (`resolve_post_analysis_slot("verification", ...)`, lines 1539–1540), and the formatter/consolidator draw on the analysis slots. Env flags read at runtime: `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB` (Step 8.5), `ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION` (the model-driven tool escape hatch), `ORA_RAG_SELECTION` (RAG contamination gate), `ORA_PIPELINE_TRACE` (forensic traces). Both `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB=1` (line 47) and `ORA_RAG_SELECTION=1` (line 18) are exported by `start.sh`, so those are on in the installed system.

**Status precision on two features.** The **Step 8.5 deliverable scrub** is not flag-gated-off: the scrub is guarded by `_env_flag("ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB")` at its gear-4 call site (line 11648; `_env_flag` is defined at line 6973 and is true when the var is `1`), and `start.sh` exports `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB=1`, so the scrub runs by default in the installed system and is disabled only by explicitly setting it to `0` for debugging (implemented, default-on). The **model-driven tool escape hatch** is genuinely partial: `_model_tool_selection_enabled` (line 8363) defaults `ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION` to `"0"`, and `start.sh` does not export it — flag-gated-off, so partial. The current Gear-2 `fast` resolution is installed through the named configuration's dedicated `utility.gear2_rag_lookup` cell; it is not dependent on a legacy `slot_assignments.fast` entry. If cleanup supplied the endpoint instead, that actual cell owns its retries.

### Evidence & Verification

**Tests.** `orchestrator/tests/test_pre_routing_pipeline.py` covers all four stages plus the dispatch announcement and the `run_pre_routing_pipeline` composition. Gear-4 reliability has dedicated suites — `test_gear4_analyst_recovery.py`, `test_gear4_degradation.py`, `test_gear4_no_short_circuit.py` — alongside `test_quality_gate.py`, `test_boot_prompt_assembly.py`, `test_perspective_loader.py`, `test_deliverable_scrub.py`, and nine `test_style_*.py` files exercising the Output Style composition path. I confirmed the test files exist by directory listing and confirmed the code paths they name against the source; I did **not** execute the suite in this pass, so pass/fail is asserted from wiring, not from a run.

**Forensic traces.** When `ORA_PIPELINE_TRACE` is active, every step writes a health payload and any fired contingency under `data/pipeline-traces/<conv>/<turn>/`, including a `step-health.json` recording each step's ok/degraded state and the named contingency (for example `step8_6-quality-gate-*`, `step6-cycleN-<slot>-verifier-BROKEN-not-verified`). This is the primary way to confirm a Gear-4 turn actually ran the adversarial pair rather than silently degrading — the trace distinguishes "verified" from "verifier BROKEN" so a turn that shipped without real verification is visible after the fact. Reliability here is qualitative: the traces record *whether* each step degraded, but there is no instrumented success-rate or per-step reliability budget in the code. Any percentage would be invented; none is measured.

**Trace-completeness currency note (2026-07-16, post-pin).** PR #269 (implementation commit `99638ef3`, merge commit `241a31c0`) extends the forensic layer well beyond the state described at this document's `7a5e8f40` pin. This is a scoped current-state note for that landed subsystem, not a re-audit or re-pin of the rest of the chapter.

The current trace boundary covers the real server and CLI entrypoints, explicit direct-entry calls, framework milestones, and the production paths for Gears 1–4. It records pre-routing, context/RAG and web consultation, model-call configuration and usage, claim-evidence assembly, verification, fallback/retry paths, lifecycle outcomes, and the terminal value returned after output routing or server delivery. `step-terminal-output.json` is deliberately written after routing/persistence and includes route, output target, and `persisted`; a failed persistence response is therefore evidence with `persisted=false`, not a falsely successful terminal record. Gear 4→3 fallback markers and the last `step-health.json` correct the manifest to the Gear that actually executed.

`trace-manifest.json` preserves history instead of normalizing it away. `expected_steps` is the normally required path; `actual_steps` is the literal on-disk `step*.json` inventory; `derived_artifacts` holds summaries such as step health and cost; `missing_steps` is restricted to required work absent from a completed turn; `skipped_steps` covers optional non-execution and not-reached work on exceptional exits; `replaced_steps` names normal stages displaced by a fallback; `contingency_steps` names real exceptional stages that ran; and `unexpected_steps` contains real stages belonging to none of those declared sets. `stage_replacements` records which contingency displaced which normal stages. Direct and short-circuit paths have their own truthful expected sets rather than inheriting an impossible full-pipeline template.

Tracing remains observational and fail-open: trace start, step writes, terminal capture, inventory refresh, and manifest finalization catch their own failures and cannot change the production return value. Raw turn directories intentionally contain exact forensic inputs and outputs. The browser and HTML-export trust boundary is different: recursive safe projections replace every raw string/byte sequence with type, length, and SHA-256 metadata, hash unrecognized keys, refuse unsafe paths/symlinks/oversized files, and emit fixed error codes. Private Dialogues retain local evidence but are labeled `redaction_level=private`; browser/export surfaces omit their raw content and diagnostic turns retain the private routing tag. Stealth suppresses trace creation, and protected Delete Forever purges any Ora-managed residue. Trace Walk inspection and export are read-only with respect to production behavior and original evidence; pin/unpin changes only retention metadata, and **Investigate** creates a separate same-Dialogue P-Debug turn rather than replaying or modifying the traced execution.

**Confirmed drift (trust the code).** Three documentation-versus-code divergences are real and load-bearing enough to name:

1. `frameworks/book/f-evaluate.md` and `f-revise.md` still reference the mode section `## EVALUATION CRITERIA` (f-evaluate lines 3, 11, 206, 211). The modes and `boot.py` (lines 8515–8518) renamed it to `## ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA` on 2026-05-24. The code keeps a legacy fallback, so this is functional but doc-stale — a reader following the F-stage spec will look for a section name the mode files no longer use.
2. `architecture/pre-routing-pipeline.md` says "56 deep-analysis modes" (line 23) and its Stage 4 still describes loading runtime config from `runtime-configuration.md` (line 235) with error-on-missing per Decision C. Both are stale: 58 modes declare Gear 4, and `runtime-configuration.md` was retired 2026-05-12 — runtime fields now live in each mode's `## DEFAULT GEAR` / `## RAG PROFILE`.
3. `boot/boot.md` line 59 reads "Seventeen modes, four gears." The installed system has 64 modes. The gear count is right; the mode count is stale.

Additionally, `f-quality-gate.md` is wired (Gear-3 step 6.5, Gear-4 step 8.6) but is absent from `pre-routing-pipeline.md`'s stage description and from the F-stage inventory — an undocumented pipeline step relative to the architecture spec. And `architecture/` holds 9 files (`trusted-web-sources.md` is the ninth), while `CLAUDE.md` Decision K enumerates only 8.

**Explicitly untested surfaces at the chapter baseline.** The model-driven tool escape hatch (`ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION` default off, unexercised in normal operation) and every non-macOS platform (see below) retain their stated limitations. The current Model notes describe source-level Gear-1/2 and Gear-3/4 behavior, not a new live-provider or platform qualification. The broader `manual_mode_selection` and `/chat` bypass claims remain outside this scoped update.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read in this order. Start with `architecture/pre-routing-pipeline.md` for the *why* of the four stages (discount its "56 modes" and `runtime-configuration.md` language as stale). Then read `build_system_prompt_for_gear` (`boot.py:8467`) to see exactly what each step's prompt contains — this is where the whole subsystem's behavior is actually decided. Then `run_gear4` (line 11691) for the cascade and its contingency table, and `run_gear3` (line 11046) for the sequential variant and the step-6.5 gate.

The single highest drift risk is the coupling between mode-file `## SECTION` names and the `_extract_section` calls at lines 8509–8523. A renamed section does not error; it silently returns empty and drops that step's guidance. The `EVALUATION CRITERIA` → `ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA` rename already left `f-evaluate.md` and `f-revise.md` stale, and the code only survives it because someone added a legacy fallback. If you rename or add a mode section, grep every `_extract_section` call and every `f-*.md` spec before assuming it took effect. This is the place future changes most likely cause silent regressions.

Second: the F-stage spec files in `frameworks/book/` are documentation that must track `boot.py`'s step wiring, and they have drifted before (`f-verify.md` was corrected 2026-05-22 after the Step 7/8 split left it describing a post-consolidation verifier that no longer exists). Treat the specs as intent and the boot.py step map as truth. Note in particular the verifier's known gap: `f-verify.md` states plainly (lines 7, 26) that the verifier's `### Verified Final Output` is captured in the trace but **not propagated** — the consolidator and formatter read the reviser's `revised_depth` / `revised_breadth` directly. A correction the verifier computes never reaches the user. That is by design, but it surprises readers who assume verification edits the output.

Third: the pipeline's "8 steps" and its 6 named pipeline roles are different countings. `_PIPELINE_STEPS` has six roles (analyst, evaluator, reviser, verifier, consolidator, formatter); the "8 steps" language counts Phase A cleanup as step 1, context assembly as step 2, and the quality gate at 6.5/8.6. Do not try to reconcile them into one sequence.

Fourth: `orchestrator/dispatcher.py` is named like a mode/gear dispatcher but is the *tool* dispatcher (permission gating, path validation, audit logging). The actual gear dispatch is the switch inside `boot.py::run_pipeline` at lines 9338–9394. This mis-maps easily from the file list.

Finally, treat `CLAUDE.md`'s mode and file counts as unreliable: 56, 58, 60, 64, and 65 all appear across the docs. The verified numbers at 7a5e8f40 are 64 modes (65 files in `modes/`, one being `INDEX.md`) with a 1/2/3/58 Gear-1/2/3/4 distribution, and 9 files in `architecture/`.

### Platform compatibility

The installed system runs on macOS on Apple Silicon and is verified only there. Per-subsystem checks:

- **Inference engine (depth/breadth analyst calls, Gears 3/4).** `platform_check.py::detect_platform` routes `Darwin` + `arm64` to MLX and everything else to Ollama (the `recommended_engine` branch at line 31). MLX is Apple-Silicon-only. **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: n/a for MLX** — those platforms are routed to Ollama by design, but that Ollama path for the gear analysts was **not run off-Mac and is untested.**
- **Parallel-analyst execution (`run_gear4`).** Uses stdlib `concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor` (portable) plus a Mac-specific MLX mutex that serializes same-machine local calls. **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — the ThreadPoolExecutor is portable in principle; the MLX serialization is the Mac-specific part and does not apply elsewhere.
- **RAM detection (`platform_check.get_system_ram_gb`).** Has explicit branches for Darwin (`sysctl`), Linux (`/proc/meminfo`), and Windows (`wmic`), with a 16 GB fallback (lines 40–59). **macOS: works. Linux / Windows-native: code path exists but untested. WSL: untested** (would take the Linux branch).
- **Root paths (`runtime_paths.py`).** Built from `expanduser('~')`, env-overridable via `ORA_HOME` / `ORA_VAULT`, with `norm_key` using `os.path.normcase` + `realpath` for Windows-safe prefix checks. **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — cross-platform in construction, POSIX-shaped in its defaults, never actually run off-Mac.
- **Interpreter and launcher.** `start.sh` is a bash launcher and retains Mac-oriented interpreter preferences and explicit feature exports. The separate current `start.bat` honors `ORA_HOME`, prefers the checkout virtual environment, falls back to `py -3`/`python`, validates the port, enables oversight by default, consumes `--no-oversight`, forwards other arguments, and binds process/health handling to the current checkout. **macOS: works. Windows-native: implemented but untested** — `start.bat` enables the Execution Review loop but omits the POSIX launcher's additional feature-default exports, so Windows can still run a reduced or differently configured pipeline; no clean-room Windows run supports a “works” label. **WSL / Linux: untested** — the POSIX launcher is plausible there but not verified.
- **Server startup (`server.py` → `boot.py` pipeline, localhost:5000, Flask).** **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — localhost binding is portable in principle, but the server has not been started off-Mac. (The `/chat` pipeline is synchronous JSON with browser polling, not SSE — see the Server chapter; SSE survives only on three residual endpoints.)
- **Trace writes (`data/pipeline-traces/` via `pipeline_trace`).** Path-based writes through `runtime_paths`. **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — filesystem case-sensitivity differs on Windows; `normcase` handles the prefix checks but the write path itself was not exercised off-Mac.

No non-macOS surface is claimed to work; every one above is labeled untested or needs-porting where it was not actually run off-Mac.

## 6. Framework Loading & Execution

- **Current standalone document-review boundary.** The Note Review app at `server/review.py` remains a separate localhost process, but its app-wide pre-view hook calls the same server-package browser-origin decision as the main host.
- A hostile form POST to `/action/<filename>` returns `403` before the action handler reads `request.form` or touches review, staging, or rejected files; headerless local and same-origin requests remain compatible.

### Problem

An Ora framework is a long adversarial-analysis specification — Process Formalization Framework v2.1, for one, runs to thousands of words of layered instructions. If I hand that whole document to a model as a single prompt, three failure modes follow. The model truncates: it reads the first layers, produces something plausible, and never reaches the verification criteria at the bottom. The model wanders: with no enforced checkpoints, milestone three's output drifts from what the user actually asked in milestone one, and nothing catches the drift until a human reads the final artifact. And the model conflates: it collapses a five-stage procedure into one undifferentiated pass, losing the reproducibility that made the framework worth writing down. A framework that is only prose is a framework the model is free to ignore.

There is a second, orthogonal risk. The directory `~/ora/frameworks/book/` holds 39 markdown files. Most are not user-facing frameworks. Eight are internal Gear-4 pipeline-stage specs — `f-analysis-depth.md`, `f-evaluate.md`, `f-consolidate.md`, and their siblings — that the gear pipeline loads automatically and that make no sense invoked standalone: running `/framework f-verify "…"` would ask the model to perform a per-stream verification pass against nothing. If file existence in `frameworks/book/` were treated as permission to invoke, every one of those internal stages, plus support specs like `phase-a-prompt-cleanup.md` and `supplemental-rag-protocol.md`, would be exposed through the same `/framework` command that runs Problem Evolution. That is a namespace-hygiene failure: the invocation surface must be a curated subset of the file surface, not its mirror.

This subsystem solves both problems. It parses spec prose into an executable milestone graph, runs that graph as milestone-bounded gear passes with a drift check at each boundary, and gates which files a user may invoke through a hand-maintained allow-list.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

**The milestone is the unit of execution.** The parser (`framework_parser.py`) reads a framework's `## MILESTONES DELIVERED` section and extracts each `### Milestone N:` (single-mode) or `#### Milestone N:` (multi-mode) block into a `Milestone` dataclass, binding its properties inline: the endpoint it produces, the verification criterion that defines success, the layers it covers, the gear it runs at, the milestones it requires first, and a drift-check question. The executor (`milestone_executor.py`) then runs the framework as a sequence: for each milestone, assemble a structured handoff packet, run it through one gear pass, persist the deliverable, and check it for drift before advancing.

The decision I want to defend here is reuse over parallel machinery. A framework execution *is* adversarial analysis — depth pass, breadth evaluation, revision, consolidation, verification. Ora already has that machinery in `run_gear4`. So one milestone equals one `run_gear4` call over a structured handoff packet (`_run_through_gear_pipeline`, `milestone_executor.py:565-607`). I rejected the alternative of building a separate framework-specific review path. A parallel path would have duplicated the entire gear pipeline's adversarial logic and then drifted out of sync with it the first time someone tuned the gear-4 consolidation step. Instead the framework layer is thin: it decomposes the spec into milestones and lets the existing pipeline do the analytical work. The tradeoff is that a framework can only be as adversarial as the gear it declares; a milestone marked `gear: 1` gets a single-pass model call with no cross-evaluation (`milestone_executor.py:586-607`).

**Multi-mode routing is deterministic-first, LLM-last.** Frameworks like Problem Evolution declare several operating modes (PE-Init, PE-Iterate, PE-Review, PE-Spawn). Picking the right one for a given user request is a routing problem, and I did not want to pay a model call for it in the common case. `select_mode` is a four-tier cascade (`milestone_executor.py:734-793`): an explicit mode prefix as the first token of the input (consumed, cheap, exact); an in-input mention of a mode name anywhere in the text; only then a small-model routing classifier on the sidebar slot; and finally a default to the first declared mode. The first two tiers are pure string matching. The classifier — the only tier that costs a model call — fires only when no textual signal is present, and it degrades to the first-mode default when no endpoint is available (`_llm_select_mode` returning `None`). This is the same design principle as a fast-path/slow-path cache: spend the expensive resource only on the inputs that actually need it.

**Invocability is a curated allow-list, not file existence.** `framework_invocability.py` loads `config/framework-invocability.json`, which declares three disjoint sets: `invocable_frameworks` (11 entries, runnable through `/framework`), `pickable_frameworks` (11 entries, the same public set surfaced in the picker UI), and `internal_only_frameworks` (10 entries). The loader unconditionally folds the eight Gear-4 F-* stage IDs into the internal set regardless of the JSON (`framework_invocability.py:86-91`), so even a mis-edited config cannot accidentally expose a pipeline stage. `resolve_user_invocable_framework` maps a user token through aliases (`cff` → `corpus-formalization.md`, plus `deep-research`, `knowledge-artifact-coaching`, `off`, and `pff`) and raises `FrameworkInvocabilityError` with a specific message when the token is internal-only or unregistered (`framework_invocability.py:124-149`). The rejected alternative — glob `frameworks/book/` and expose everything — would have made the F-* internal stages invocable and coupled the user-facing surface to an implementation-detail directory.

**Config binding was designed as opt-in, and remains unwired.** `framework_config.py` implements `${config.<key>}` substitution and `<!-- ora-project-extension: <id> -->` overlay splicing, so a project-bound spec can carry project-specific values (a ChromaDB collection name, say) without hardcoding them. The design decision was to keep this a separate opt-in layer — `compose_framework_spec` — so the default execution path stays project-neutral and the executor keeps parsing raw spec text. That decision is sound. But the honest status is that `compose_framework_spec` has no production call site: grep finds it only in `framework_config.py` itself and its test file, and `framework_config` is imported by nothing in production (verified against `orchestrator/` and `server/` at 7a5e8f40). The module's own docstring says Chunk 4 — the migration of `document-processing.md` — "switches that one framework's call sites over" (`framework_config.py:43-46`); that migration did not land. This is partial: coded, unit-tested, and orphaned. The runtime path in `execute_framework` calls `parse_framework_file` on raw text and never touches the config binder (`milestone_executor.py:254`).

**The strict parser is a deliberate tradeoff that leaves DCA non-executable.** The parser fails loudly: a framework whose `## MILESTONES DELIVERED` section is absent raises `FrameworkParseError` rather than silently producing an empty milestone list (`framework_parser.py:100-101`). I prefer loud failure — a spec that doesn't conform should not run half-formed. The cost is that Decision Clarity Analysis (`decision-clarity-analysis.md`), named as one of the six canonical frameworks, has no `## MILESTONES DELIVERED` section. Parsing it raises `FrameworkParseError: decision-clarity-analysis: no '## MILESTONES DELIVERED' section found` (verified by direct invocation at 7a5e8f40), and it is absent from all three lists in `framework-invocability.json` (verified: `grep -c` returns 0). So the operative status is precise: PEF, PIF, PFF, CFF, and OFF parse and route as multi-mode frameworks (all five verified to yield their declared mode lists), but DCA is an analytical specification only — it cannot run through the milestone executor. The canonical framework suite is closed at six members; five are executable, DCA is not.

**Open problems.** Three are load-bearing. First, `DRIFT_DETECTED` is log-and-continue: when the drift check flags that a milestone wandered, the executor records the reasoning and proceeds anyway (`milestone_executor.py:321-324`); pause-and-surface-to-user is deferred. Second, per-framework default configuration via `framework-routing.json` is coded but inert — the file contains only an underscore-prefixed `_example_pef` stub that the loader ignores, so the lookup always falls through to the Router's auto-derivation (`config/framework-routing.json`; lookup at `_lookup_framework_default_configuration`, `milestone_executor.py:141`). Third, the orphaned config-binding layer above.

### Implementation

**Dispatch chain.** Both `orchestrator/boot.py` and `server/app.py` detect the `/framework ` prefix and route to `run_framework_command` (`boot.py:9158-9194`, `server.py:3253-3290`). Each call site is gated by an Execution Review Phase 2 check before dispatch (the comment reads "Execution Review Phase 2 (condition 4)" at both sites). `run_framework_command` (`milestone_executor.py:994`) is the top-level entrypoint: it calls `parse_framework_command` to split `/framework <name> [--config <ConfigName>] [<query>]` into a resolved filename, a query, and an optional config name. The filename is resolved through `resolve_user_invocable_framework`, so an internal or unregistered token is rejected here with a bracketed error string. Every failure mode in this function is caught and returned as a bracketed string rather than raised (`FileNotFoundError`, `FrameworkParseError`, `NotImplementedError`, catch-all `Exception`), because the chat UI must always receive a renderable response. A framework that fails to parse produces `[Framework parse error: …]` in the transcript, not a stack trace.

An empty query is a deliberate fork, not an error: `run_framework_command` treats it as a usage message, while the chat handlers route empty-query invocations to interactive multi-turn elicitation (documented at `parse_framework_command`, `milestone_executor.py:890`).

**Execution.** `execute_framework` (`milestone_executor.py:205-411`) parses the file, resolves the effective config name (explicit arg → `framework-routing.json` default → `None`, at which point the Router auto-derives from execution context), then branches on `is_multi_mode`. Multi-mode frameworks call `select_mode`; the chosen mode's milestones are pulled from `milestones_by_mode`. If the selected mode is mechanical (C-Instance, C-Validate, O-Render), the executor short-circuits before creating scratch or calling any model and returns a redirect (`milestone_executor.py:272`, `_build_mechanical_redirect` at 693). This prevents a specific failure: a mechanical mode's real job is to create a file on disk via a runtime function; routed through the gear pipeline, a model would instead hallucinate an artifact. The redirect points the user at the matching slash command (`/instance`, `/validate`, `/render`).

**Mechanical corpus validation.** The corpus parser treats malformed YAML in either frontmatter or a fenced `sections:` block as an invalid corpus. `/instance`, `/validate`, and `/render` surface that parse failure and stop before creating an instance or rendered artifact or emitting `CorpusInstanceCreated`, `CorpusValidated`, or `OFFRendered`; metadata-free legacy corpus templates and valid YAML continue through the existing paths.

For a live mode, the executor creates a `ScratchSession` (`scratch.py`), emits a `FrameworkStarted` oversight event, and iterates the milestones. Each milestone runs through `_run_milestone` (`milestone_executor.py:416-456`), which builds the handoff packet once and retries the gear pass up to `MAX_RETRIES = 3` times with `2**(attempt-1)` backoff (`milestone_executor.py:449-451`). On success it writes the deliverable to scratch and runs the drift check; on the third failure it raises `MilestoneExecutionError`, which `execute_framework` catches to mark the scratch session failed — preserving it for inspection — and emit `MilestoneBlocked` plus a failed `FrameworkComplete` (`milestone_executor.py:369-411`). On overall success, scratch is cleaned up and the last milestone's deliverable becomes the final output.

**Handoff packet.** `_build_handoff_packet` (`milestone_executor.py:461-527`) assembles the user message for the gear pass from: the original user input; prior milestone deliverables read from scratch, keyed on the milestone's `required_prior` list; the current milestone header; the layer instruction bodies for the layers this milestone covers (looked up through `_collect_layer_bodies` at `milestone_executor.py:530`, which tries exact label, punctuation-stripped label, then integer match); the output specification; the verification criterion; and any conditional layers. When a milestone references layers whose `## LAYER` blocks are absent, the packet does not silently proceed — it inlines a visible note recording the gap. This matters because the packet, not the raw spec, is what the model sees; a missing layer body would otherwise be an invisible omission.

**Gear invocation.** `_run_through_gear_pipeline` (`milestone_executor.py:565-607`) wraps the packet in a `context_pkg` and dispatches by declared gear: `run_gear4` at gear ≥ 4 (default), `run_gear3` at gear 3, single-model pass below. It deliberately bypasses Phase A cleanup and mode classification — the handoff is framework-generated structured content, not raw human input, so re-running input hygiene on it would be wrong. The `config_name` threads through to the gear functions so a named configuration routes the whole pass.

**Runtime document-processing ownership.** Dialogue-closeout extraction is not another framework mode, but it reuses the document-processing path after `server/app.py` has authenticated one current complete non-Stealth exchange. Every approved staging note and every human-review record created from that run carries the Ora-managed derivative kind plus the exact source Dialogue, source chunk, local turn, and Standard or Private authority. A Private derivative also retains its controlled Private tag. Stealth is refused before extraction, and the extractor never receives preceding history as substitute evidence for a missing current owner.

The standalone review app preserves runtime ownership through disposition. Approve, edit, and reject take the source Dialogue's cross-process lock and reread the record. They compare only immutable Dialogue/chunk/turn coordinates; when those still match, disposition uses the reread record's current privacy, so a retag completed before lock acquisition is safe. Ordinary non-turn records with none of the runtime markers—derivative kind, manager, turn privacy, source chunk, or source turn—remain compatible. Any marker makes an ownership claim; partial or malformed runtime ownership, and conflicting immutable coordinates, are refused.

**Drift check.** `_run_drift_check` (`milestone_executor.py:627-669`) runs on the sidebar slot (`DRIFT_CHECK_SLOT = "sidebar"`, a small cheap model). It prompts a drift-detection auditor with the user's original input, the milestone deliverable, and the milestone's drift question, demanding a structured `STATUS:` reply parsed by `_parse_drift_response` (`milestone_executor.py:672`). Three outcomes: `IN_SCOPE`, `DRIFT_DETECTED`, or `DRIFT_CHECK_SKIPPED` — the last when no drift question is declared, no endpoint is available, or the model call raises. The check names the failure mode it prevents: silent scope creep across a multi-milestone procedure.

**Oversight events.** Every boundary emits through the in-process bus (`FrameworkStarted`, `MilestoneComplete`, `FrameworkComplete`, `MilestoneBlocked`), each carrying the selected mode. The emit function is lazily imported and stubbed to a no-op when the oversight infrastructure isn't loaded (`milestone_executor.py:243-249`), so the executor runs standalone. Standalone invocations (no `project_nexus`) still emit but are filtered out downstream by the oversight router.

**Config surfaces.** Four: `config/framework-invocability.json` (the allow-list); `config/framework-routing.json` (per-framework default configuration — stub-only today); the `--config <ConfigName>` flag on the command; and `ORA_HOME`, honored only by `framework_config.py:60`. Note the workspace-root divergence: `framework_parser.py:20` hardcodes `~/ora/frameworks/book` and `scratch.py:22` hardcodes `~/ora/scratch`, both via `os.path.expanduser` and both ignoring `ORA_HOME`, while `framework_config.py:60` respects it. A relocated install would find the config binder and the parser disagreeing about where the workspace lives.

**A one-shot self-persistence side effect.** `_maybe_persist_self_mindspec` (`milestone_executor.py:155-202`) fires only when the framework is `mindspec-interview` and the mode is `MSI-Self`: it archives the full self-portrait to `~/ora/mindspec/self-spec.md` and writes a projected assistant-directives block into `~/ora/mind.md` (via `mind_projection`, falling back to the verbatim spec on projection failure). It is best-effort and never raises. This is the one place the executor writes outside scratch, and it is narrowly gated so an agent or character spec can never clobber the user's values.

### Evidence & Verification

**Passing test suites** covering this subsystem: `test_multi_mode_dispatch.py` (28 tests) covers the four-tier priority chain, the LLM-classifier response parsing, catalog construction, end-to-end `execute_framework` with a mocked gear pipeline, mode-tagged oversight events, and the mechanical-mode redirect, plus live-parse smoke tests against the canonical CFF and OFF files. `test_framework_invocability.py` (4 tests) covers the allow-list resolution and alias handling. `test_framework_config.py` (35 tests) covers `parse_config_interface`, `substitute_config_refs`, `splice_extension_overlays`, and `compose_framework_spec` end-to-end against synthetic frameworks. `test_chunk3_per_process_config.py` (10 tests) and `test_router_config_name.py` (26 tests) cover named-configuration threading. These counts are the `def test_` totals in each file at 7a5e8f40; the subsystem carries no instrumented aggregate suite number.

**Live parser proof.** Running `parse_framework_file` against each canonical framework at 7a5e8f40 confirms: `problem-evolution` → `['PE-Init', 'PE-Iterate', 'PE-Review', 'PE-Spawn']`; `process-inference` → `['P-Infer', 'P-Debug', 'P-Decompose', 'P-Formalize', 'P-Feasibility']`; `process-formalization` → `['F-Design', 'F-Convert', 'F-Render', 'F-Audit']`; `corpus-formalization` → `['C-Design', 'C-Modify', 'C-Instance', 'C-Validate']`; `output-formalization` → `['O-Design', 'O-Modify', 'O-Render', 'O-Audit']`. The negative case is equally confirmed: `decision-clarity-analysis.md` raises `FrameworkParseError: decision-clarity-analysis: no '## MILESTONES DELIVERED' section found`, and `grep -c decision-clarity config/framework-invocability.json` returns 0.

**How to confirm end-to-end.** Invoke `/framework problem-evolution PE-Init walk through the new project` in chat: the explicit prefix consumes `PE-Init`, the remaining text becomes the effective input, and `format_execution_result` surfaces the selected mode with a mode-selection line in the header. A `/framework f-verify …` invocation returns a bracketed `FrameworkInvocabilityError` message — proof the internal-stage boundary holds. A `/framework cff "create instance for May"` invocation returns the mechanical-mode redirect rather than running the gear pipeline.

**Explicitly untested surfaces.** `framework_input_gap.py` has no dedicated unit-test file in `orchestrator/tests/`; its two analysis paths (structured `## Setup Questions` and LLM-fallback `## INPUT CONTRACT`) are verified only indirectly through its `server/app.py` call site at `/api/framework/analyze-inputs`. `compose_framework_spec` is unit-tested but has zero production call sites — its behavior in situ is unverified because nothing in production invokes it. The `framework-routing.json` per-framework default path is exercised by no real mapping — only the ignored `_example_pef` stub exists, so the non-trivial branch of `_lookup_framework_default_configuration` never fires in production. There is no instrumented reliability metric on milestone execution: `MAX_RETRIES = 3` is a fixed retry count, and the fraction of milestones that pass drift, retry, or fail is not measured. Non-macOS platforms are entirely untested.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `framework_parser._parse_properties` and `_extract_milestones_section` first. The entire executor depends on that regex schema — properties are parsed from `- **PropertyName:** value` bullets, and a spec that deviates from that exact shape silently loses the property (the value defaults to empty string, and the milestone runs with a missing endpoint or verification criterion). If you are debugging a milestone that runs but produces the wrong deliverable, suspect a malformed property bullet before anything else. Then read `milestone_executor.select_mode` (734-793) and `_build_handoff_packet` (461-527) — those two functions determine which milestones run and what the model actually sees.

Four drift hotspots, where future changes are most likely to cause silent breakage:

1. **Stale docstrings.** `milestone_executor.py:19-24` still claims single-mode is "the MVP target" and multi-mode routing is "not yet wired in (an executor TODO)." That is false at 7a5e8f40 — `select_mode` and full multi-mode dispatch are implemented and tested. Trust the code, not the docstring.

2. **Orphaned config binding.** `framework_config.py`'s docstring describes a Chunk-4 migration of `document-processing.md` that never landed. `compose_framework_spec` is dead in the runtime path. If a new framework adds a `## CONFIGURATION INTERFACE` section expecting `${config.*}` substitution, the executor will not perform it — it parses raw text. This is the single most likely place for a future contributor to build on an assumption the runtime doesn't honor.

3. **DCA in the canonical six.** Anyone reasoning about "the six frameworks" as a uniform executable set is wrong: DCA is a spec-only analytical framework. Check the invocability JSON before assuming a framework runs.

4. **Workspace-root divergence.** `framework_parser.py:20` and `scratch.py:22` hardcode `~/ora` paths; `framework_config.py:60` honors `ORA_HOME`. Any relocation work must reconcile these three, or the parser and the (currently dormant) config binder will read from different roots.

### Platform compatibility

- **`FRAMEWORKS_DIR` (`framework_parser.py:20`, `os.path.expanduser("~/ora/frameworks/book")`)** — macOS: works. Linux: untested; `expanduser` resolves `~` on POSIX, but the hardcoded `~/ora` layout has not been run off Mac. WSL: untested, same basis. Windows-native: needs-porting — the hardcoded `~/ora` layout and forward-slash path have not been exercised off POSIX.
- **`framework_config.FRAMEWORKS_DIR` / `ORA_HOME` override (`framework_config.py:60-61`)** — macOS: works. Linux / WSL: untested. Windows-native: untested. Note the intra-subsystem inconsistency: this module honors `ORA_HOME`; `framework_parser.py` and `scratch.py` do not. On any platform where the install is relocated via `ORA_HOME`, the parser and config binder resolve different roots — though since `compose_framework_spec` is unwired in production, this is latent, not active, today.
- **`framework_invocability.CONFIG_PATH` (`framework_invocability.py:16-20`, `Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent / "config" / …`)** — macOS: works. `pathlib` relative resolution is cross-platform in principle, but not actually run off Mac. Linux / WSL / Windows-native: unknown-untested.
- **Scratch writes (`scratch.py:22`, `SCRATCH_ROOT = ~/ora/scratch/<execution_id>/`) and the failure-message path (`milestone_executor.py:968`, hardcoded `~/ora/scratch/…`)** — macOS: works. Linux / WSL: untested. Windows-native: needs-porting — same hardcoded POSIX layout as the parser.
- **Local model dispatch for drift check and LLM mode-select (sidebar slot)** — macOS: works. Linux / WSL / Windows-native: the *local* slots are Apple-silicon-only. Ora's local models run on MLX/Metal. Off-Mac, only cloud slots are available; when no endpoint resolves, the drift check returns `DRIFT_CHECK_SKIPPED` and `_llm_select_mode` returns `None` (the cascade then defaults to the first declared mode). Framework execution itself does not require a local model — it degrades to reduced routing precision and skipped drift checks, not failure. This degradation path is coded but has not been exercised off Mac.
- **`compose_framework_spec` overlay reads (`framework_config.py`, utf-8, `OSError`-guarded)** — macOS: works in unit tests. Linux / WSL / Windows-native: untested. Effectively n/a in production today: the entire config-binding path has no production caller, so no platform actually exercises it at runtime.
- **Untested off-Mac overall:** Windows-native, WSL, and Linux have not been run for this subsystem end-to-end. Any "works" above is scoped to macOS; POSIX `expanduser`/`pathlib` semantics are the *reason to expect* Linux/WSL portability, not evidence of an executed run.

## 7. Output Styles & Personal Values

- **Current Style/Theme deletion boundary.** Style deletion requires its callable store/settings handlers, a regular style store, a regular-or-absent settings file, the exact profile, and valid settings shape before review. Theme deletion similarly requires one valid regular index entry and its non-symlink directory. Their existing protection receipts still bind the affected files and terminal state.
- Project-theme asset serving, list previews, duplication, and export share the project-theme opener described in Chapter 17; it refuses outward-symlink redirection before content is read.

### Problem

An analytical pipeline that produces the right answer in the wrong voice has still failed the user. But voice is not one knob. A user wants two independent controls: *how* the assistant talks — tone, arrangement, diction, the genre it writes in — and *what values* it holds — its directness, its willingness to disagree, its ethical red lines. Bolt these together into a single "personality" and you get a system where changing the writing style silently changes the ethics, and where a request for a warmer tone leaks into a request for softer verdicts.

Two failure modes must be prevented specifically.

The first is granularity collapse: injecting the same voice instruction into a two-line conversational reply and a 3,000-word deliverable. A fast reply at gear 1 needs a disposition — is this assistant blunt or diplomatic, warm or cool. A produced artifact at gear 4 needs that *plus* an arrangement schema, a craft floor, and a diction glossary. One undifferentiated blob is either too heavy for the quick reply or too thin for the deliverable.

The second, and the more dangerous, is style contaminating substance. If the same voice layer that shapes prose also reaches the evaluator or the verifier — the adversarial steps whose entire job is to catch errors — then a style mismatch can lower a verdict. A user who asked for a "challenging" demeanor could, through that alone, get findings marked down. Style is secondary to substance. The system has to encode that ordering mechanically, not merely hope for it.

The values layer has its own failure mode: clobbering. A user might spend two minutes in a guided wizard, then months later run a multi-hour structured interview, then hand-edit the result. If those three authoring paths write the same file without knowing about each other, each overwrites the last and the user loses work silently.

This chapter covers the subsystem that answers all four: the prompt-injection voice/values layer, distinct from and secondary to the gear pipeline described in the pipeline-architecture chapters. It is authoritative for what the vault documents as the Output Styles pane; see [[Reference — Ora Output Styles Pane]] and [[Specification — Output Style Registry]].

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The concern splits into two orthogonal, independently-toggled layers with an explicit precedence chain baked into every injected block. In the module's own words (`orchestrator/style_assembly.py:13`) the ordering is:

```
values floor > completeness floor > craft floor > substance/findings > style
```

That ordering is not decorative — a condensed form of it ships verbatim inside the injected text, so the model reads its own precedence constraint. The gear-2 demeanor block emits `PRECEDENCE: values floor > completeness > craft > substance > style` (`style_assembly.py:164`); the gear-3/4 style block emits `PRECEDENCE: values floor > completeness > craft > findings > style` (`:175`). The precedence is *instructed*, not mechanically gated — a prompt line, not a runtime guard — and that is a weaker guarantee than a hard gate. What *is* mechanical is where the block gets injected and which steps see it.

Output Styles are gear-conditional. At gears 1-2 (fast conversational replies) the system injects a compact `## DEMEANOR` block, framed *high* in the prompt, before substance — because at that granularity the disposition *is* the instruction. At gears 3-4 (produced output) it injects the full `## STYLE` block, appended *after* the step's substance, and only on the producing/shaping steps: analyst, reviser, consolidator, formatter. The evaluator and verifier are excluded outright (`orchestrator/boot.py:8711`). This is the mechanical answer to the substance-contamination failure mode: a style mismatch physically cannot reach the step that renders a verdict, because the injection code never runs there.

Personal values live in a single user-authored `mind.md`, appended by the boot loader as an authoritative block, gated on one setting. It is one file with one save destination that equals its load source — no parallel copies to drift.

Persona, Output Styles, and `mind.md` are separate layers rather than one unified persona blob. Persona establishes the speaking identity; Output Styles shape delivery at the gear and pipeline stages described above; `mind.md` supplies the user's authoritative personal values. For interactive work, Persona resolution keeps its ordinary precedence: a project Persona, then the global default, then packaged Ora. The Scheduled/Oversight email-draft composer is the deliberate exception: it disables project precedence and uses the global default unless the user explicitly selects another available Persona. Each saved draft records and displays the resolved Persona identity. If an explicitly requested Persona is unavailable, draft creation refuses the request instead of silently substituting another sender. Creating the draft changes no authority: it neither activates nor approves a Trigger, contacts an email provider, nor permits sending. Keeping these layers separate prevents a writing-style edit from changing the user's values or a project context from silently changing the identity attached to scheduled correspondence.

The tradeoff on the values side is three authoring paths for one file: a two-minute guided wizard, a raw editor, and a projection from a multi-hour structured interview. Three writers on one file is exactly the clobbering hazard. It is paid for with a provenance-marker state machine: a composed `mind.md` carries an HTML-comment marker declaring who wrote it — `<!-- ora-mind-guided: … -->` (`orchestrator/mind_guided.py:36`) or `<!-- ora-mind-projected: … -->` (`orchestrator/mind_projection.py:55`) — while the stock default file carries only a plain text marker line (`*Default configuration…*`, `mind.md:3`) that reads as un-customized. The write endpoints read the marker before overwriting and refuse to clobber a hand-edited or interview-authored file without explicit `confirm_overwrite` (`server/app.py:1339`). This lets a 2-minute wizard and a multi-hour interview coexist on the same file without either silently eating the other.

**Open problems.** Two are load-bearing and are flagged plainly here.

First, the situational-delta and register-switching chain is designed and coded but unwired at runtime — status *partial*. `style_assembly.py` fully implements one-rung demeanor swaps (`resolve_demeanor`, `:98`) and a conversational-register overlay (inside `compose`, `:218`), and both are unit-tested in isolation. But the runtime context package hardcodes `style_register: "written"` and `style_deltas: None` (`orchestrator/boot.py:7767-7768`), and no code path sets them otherwise. The register-derivation is explicitly deferred in the module's own docstring ("once the resolution chain is wired", `style_assembly.py:206`). In production this surface is dead: every injected style resolves as written-register with no deltas. It is not a bug; it is an unbuilt half of a designed feature, and the documentation must not present it as working.

Second, the values floor is instructed, not enforced. The precedence line tells the model to hold values above style, but nothing measures whether it did. There is no instrumentation on this — no counter, no per-step budget. A guarantee stronger than "the prompt says so" does not exist yet.

### Implementation

The subsystem has a clean seam: a standalone, side-effect-free assembly core (`style_assembly.py`, `style_store.py`) that only reads files and returns strings, and the boot-layer wiring that decides *when* and *where* those strings get injected. This mirrors the Unix discipline of a pure function plus a caller that owns the side effects.

**Style assembly (the pure core).** `orchestrator/style_assembly.py` parses four read-only markdown source files under `frameworks/book/`:

- `style-registry.md` — 12 built-in genre profiles (explainer, technical, how-to, journalism, business, white-paper, academic, legal, marketing, reference, narrative, conversational), each a `## id` heading plus a `yaml` block (verified: `grep -c '^## '` = 12).
- `style-demeanor-axes.md` — 7 demeanor axes, each with 3 rungs low-to-high: warmth (cool/even/warm), force (gentle/measured/forceful), energy (calm/steady/lively), outlook (skeptical/balanced/affirming), playfulness (straight/dry/playful), directness (diplomatic/plain/blunt), agreeableness (accommodating/candid/challenging) — plus a device-overlays section (sarcasm, irony, hyperbole, understatement) toggled independently. The file carries 8 `## ` headings: the 7 axes plus the device-overlays block.
- `style-arrangement-schemas.md` — 12 ordered slot-list schemas (answer-first, motivation-first, inverted-pyramid, and so on).
- `style-craft-floor.md` — 5 craft-floor bullet lines that outrank style inside the full block.

The parsing uses PyYAML to read the registry's yaml blocks. The import is guarded: if PyYAML is absent, `load_registry` raises `RuntimeError` (`:57`) — and the callers swallow that (below), so a PyYAML-less environment silently *disables* Output Styles rather than crashing the pipeline. That is a deliberate degradation, and it is exactly the failure mode "missing dependency takes down the whole assistant" that the guard prevents.

`compose(style_id, register, gear, deltas, base, custom_entries)` (`:201`) is the entrypoint. It merges any custom profiles over the built-in registry, then branches on gear: `gear <= 2` returns `compose_demeanor_block` (disposition lines + the values-floor line + the completeness line, no arrangement); `gear >= 3` returns `compose_style_block` (demeanor + craft floor + arrangement schema + diction glossary + completeness). The gear-2 branch is where the conversational-register overlay *would* apply — it reads `entry["conversational"]` and merges the alternate demeanor per-axis — but since no caller sets a non-written register at runtime, the overlay is inert today.

**Style store (custom forks).** `orchestrator/style_store.py` persists user-forked profiles to `data/custom-styles.json` (STORE_PATH at style_store.py:25; the file is created on first custom-profile write and is absent on a fresh checkout). It mirrors the model-configuration persistence idiom deliberately: read-only framework files stay canonical, user edits live in the data dir. Writes are atomic — write to `STORE_PATH + ".tmp"`, then `os.replace` (`:95-98`) — so a crashed write never leaves a half-written store. Every reader degrades to an empty dict on a missing or corrupt file (`load_custom_profiles`, `:74`): a bad custom profile can never break the picker or the pipeline. A `PATCHABLE` whitelist (`:48`) means an endpoint can never inject an arbitrary key into a stored entry; a patch to `demeanor`/`devices`/`glossary`/`format` deep-merges key-by-key so one axis can be changed without resending the block.

**The two injection sites (the wiring).** `orchestrator/boot.py::_compose_output_style` (`:8432`) is the single funnel. It reads `style_id` off the context package, merges custom profiles, and calls `style_assembly.compose`. Critically, the *whole function is wrapped in a bare except that returns `""`* (`:8464`) — style is secondary to substance and must never break the pipeline, so any failure (missing file, malformed yaml, absent PyYAML) yields a no-op, not an exception.

It is called from exactly two places:

1. Gears 1-2, framed high (`:8539`): `if 0 < _osf_gear <= 2:` — the compact demeanor block goes near the top of the prompt.
2. Gears 3-4, after substance (`:8711`): `if _osf_gear >= 3 and step in ("analyst", "reviser", "consolidator", "formatter"):` — the full style block is appended after the step's substantive content. The step tuple is the mechanical exclusion of evaluator and verifier.

**Resolution chain.** `_resolve_effective_style_id(config)` (`:7088`) computes the turn's default style before any one-off, in precedence order: active project's `default_style_id` → project container-record `output_style` (read via `project_meta` for a Projects-feature project with no plugin manifest) → the `styles.default_id` account setting → legacy engine-config default → `None`. It is best-effort throughout — every lookup is in a try/except that falls through, so a project-lookup failure never breaks step-2 assembly.

**Config surfaces.** `orchestrator/user_settings.py` defines two account-wide controls (`:105-115`): `styles.default_id` (string; `""` = no style) and `styles.use_custom_values` (bool). Against the upstream inventory this is worth stating exactly: `default_id` ships defaulting to `"explainer"`, not empty — Output Styles are on out of the box, styled with the explainer genre — and `use_custom_values` ships `False`, so values injection is off by default. The settings docstring also states the register is determined by the pipeline path, not a stored toggle; that intent is consistent with the unwired-register open problem above, and the runtime hardcode is why the pipeline-path derivation does not yet happen.

**Per-turn overrides.** `/style <id>` and `/style off` are parsed in `parse_user_command` (`:14509`). `_is_known_style_id` (`:14485`) validates the id against the registry and custom store, and — matching the degradation philosophy — *accepts* (returns `True`) when the registry can't be read, so a one-off isn't silently eaten in a PyYAML-less environment. An unknown id leaves the input intact, so a `/style` typo passes through as ordinary text rather than being consumed.

**Honne/tatemae audience toggle.** `server/app.py::_apply_style_audience` (`:5970`) implements a separate axis: an input-toolbar toggle (`server/static/js/style-audience-toggle.js`) that marks a turn `internal`, folding the active project's `interaction_style` onto `style_id` for that turn (how Ora talks *to* you, versus the default output style). An explicit `/style` one-off already on the context wins over it. This swaps the *whole* style_id; it is **not** the written-vs-conversational register axis — two different knobs a reader could easily conflate.

**Personal values injection.** `load_boot_md` (`orchestrator/boot.py:1316-1324`) appends `mind.md` as an authoritative block only when `styles.use_custom_values` is on and the file exists. Before appending, `_filter_private_values` (`:95`) strips any `## Private Context` section unless the conversation is tagged `private` or `stealth` — so private life-context directives never reach an ordinary conversation. The heading constant `PRIVATE_VALUES_HEADING` (`:87`) is pinned to the projection module's whitelist by a unit test (`test_mind_projection.py:164-165`), so the two can't drift apart. One implementation seam worth flagging: `boot.py` derives its `WORKSPACE` from `os.path.expanduser("~/ora/")` (`:110`) and builds `MIND_MD` from it (`:112`), while `style_store.py` and `style_assembly.py` derive their `WORKSPACE` from `__file__` (`style_store.py:24`, `style_assembly.py:25`). The `__file__`-derived paths let a git worktree's tests write the worktree's own `data/`; the `expanduser` path in boot always points at the live install regardless of checkout.

**Three authoring paths for `mind.md`.**

- Guided wizard — `orchestrator/mind_guided.py` (595 lines). A deterministic, no-model question registry: `QUESTIONS` (`:51`) holds 12 questions across 5 sections (Communication Preferences, Intellectual Posture, Ethical Boundaries, Work Patterns, Standing Principles). `compose(answers, free_text)` (`:530`) builds a complete `mind.md`, splicing in fixed non-configurable lines from `_FIXED` (`:411`) — anti-sycophancy pushback calibration and source discipline — that the user cannot toggle off. The design constraint is explicit in the docstring: the wizard covers only the value dimensions that measurably change model output when injected; the MindSpec library's psychometric variables are prompt-inert and deliberately excluded. Backs `GET/POST /api/mind/guided`. 31 tests.
- Raw editor + create-from-template — `POST /api/mind` (`server.py:1237`), 128 KB cap (`_MIND_MAX_BYTES`, `:1200`), atomic tmp+`os.replace` write. Create-from-template refuses to overwrite a customized file (`:1251`).
