Its behavioral cases invoke real single-analyst, external-consolidation, fallback/retry, server/CLI direct-entry, lifecycle/disconnect, flagged-claim evidence, supplemental-RAG, web-consultation, terminal-routing, private-projection/export, and writer-failure paths. This statement is limited to the trace-completeness landing; it does not revise the other untested surfaces named in this chapter.

A note on Execution Review status, because it is easy to overstate. Phases 1–4 are landed in `~/ora` main at 7a5e8f40 (the commit is titled "Execution Review Phase 1-4: macOS+Windows portability retrofit"). Phase 5 — the evidence-runner — is not. It sits in an unmerged worktree (`ora-worktrees/phase5-impl` on branch `execution-review-phase5-impl`) at the code-review gate; it is not merged to `~/ora` main, not installed, not landed. The installed system's Execution-Review coverage ends at Phase 4. The evidence-runner vocabulary you see in `tool_events.py` is the Phase-4 declaration of what Phase 5 will validate — declared now precisely so an undeclared check cannot later slip past a runner that does not yet exist.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read these five things first, in this order. First, `tool_events.gate()` and the `FAIL_CLOSED` default — this is the release-blocking security boundary, and everything else is scaffolding around it. Second, `_redact_for_record`, `_SCRUB_PATTERNS`, and `resolve_path_sensitivity` — the leak-prevention layer that keeps the audit log from becoming a secret store. Third, the dispatcher seam — the single place policy is resolved and the gate and recorder fire; its `PreparedCommand` and `mcp_policy` paths are the current command/MCP authority boundary. Fourth, `capability_registry.invoke`, `resolve_provider_chain`, and `load_registry`'s routing-config overlay resolution — the slot dispatch and fallback machinery. Fifth, `mcp_client`'s pinned-registry validation, fresh-environment construction, exact-ID request handling, discovery intersection, and cleanup — together these are the load-bearing MCP boundary; the reader thread is only one part of it.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift.** First, the `capability_registry.py` module docstring (lines 30-33) still declares "Stub-only in WP-7.0.1 ... ships with the registry, the dispatcher, and an empty provider table." That is stale: six integration modules now register real handlers. Trust the code — the provider table is populated at load. Second, `CLAUDE.md` gives this subsystem essentially no coverage: the security/telemetry substrate (the gate, the recorder, the dispatcher wiring, the MCP client, the web modules, the trace/health surfaces, hooks) is absent from the architecture map, which mentions only `capability_slots` and only in the plugin-convention and Visual-tab sections. The doc-code gap here is a whole-subsystem omission, not a line-level lag — do not read `CLAUDE.md`'s silence as absence of the subsystem. Third, the `ACTION_MANIFEST`, the exact MCP tool maps in `mcp-servers.json`, the package lock, and the `_PROTECTED_*` path lists must be kept in sync with new actions, MCP package versions, tools, and protection-worthy files; a discovered MCP tool absent from policy is not exposed and a new unclassified action fails closed, but a newly protection-worthy file left out of `_PROTECTED_FILES` remains ungated. Fourth, the secret-path patterns are POSIX-dotfile-shaped and will under-cover a Windows port. Fifth, the two default-off flags are partial-by-policy, not unbuilt — do not delete them as dead code; they are wired and tested, waiting on a deliberate operator enable.

For the framework contracts these modules implement, see [[Reference — Capability Invocation Contracts]] (slot input/output contracts), [[Reference — AI Agent Security Architecture]] (the gate's threat model), and the F-stage specs [[Specification — F-Consult]], [[Specification — F-Revise]], and [[Specification — F-Verify]] for the web-reach injection points at Step 2 (consultation), Step 5 (claim-verification reviser pre-flight), and Step 8 (claim-verification verifier pre-flight).

### Platform compatibility

The commit at 7a5e8f40 is titled a "macOS+Windows portability retrofit," and the code reflects genuine portability *intent* — but intent is not evidence. No test at this commit simulates Windows or Linux path semantics — no test exercises `sys.platform`, `normcase`, or backslash-separated paths against the classifiers — so every non-macOS surface below is labeled **untested**, never "works." macOS Apple Silicon is the sole verified target.

- **Path handling — `_matchable` / `_cmp_key` / protected-path and secret-path matching** (`tool_events.py:61-77`, 254-282): macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. The code forward-slashes and normcase-folds keys explicitly so that `/`-anchored patterns and `startswith` prefix checks hold on backslash-separated, case-insensitive Windows paths; this is written for portability but has no off-Mac test evidence.
- **Secret/sensitive path patterns** (`tool_events.py:289-313`): macOS / WSL / Linux — works (POSIX dotfile conventions: `.ssh`, `.aws`, `.env`, `id_rsa`, `.pem`). Windows-native — untested and known to under-cover: Windows credential conventions (`%APPDATA%` credential stores, DPAPI blobs) are not in the pattern set, so a Windows port would under-classify sensitive paths at the gate. This is a correctness gap, not just an untested one.
- **Tool-event sink write — `os.open(..., O_WRONLY|O_APPEND|O_CREAT, 0o644)`** (`tool_events.py:563`): macOS / WSL / Linux — works. Windows-native — untested; `os.open` ignores most mode bits on Windows but the append write still functions.
- **Approvals file locking — `runtime_paths.locked_file` + atomic `os.replace`** (`tool_events.py:601-607`): macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. Portability depends on the `runtime_paths.locked_file` cross-platform lock abstraction, which is out of scope for this chapter and unverified off-Mac.
- **MCP stdio reader threads + binary UTF-8 protocol bounds** (`mcp_client.py`): macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. The pump avoids `select.select` on Windows pipes, while byte limits are enforced before decoding; this is design-intent for Windows correctness, not verified behavior.
- **Pinned repository-local MCP packages and Chromium** (`mcp-runtime/package-lock.json`, `config/mcp-servers.json`; vault-fs/playwright/github): macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. Startup uses exact local Node entrypoints rather than `npx`; installation requires a compatible Node runtime and provisions the locked Chromium, but neither install nor launch has been exercised off-Mac. Individual servers fail soft when their pinned local runtime or required credential is unavailable.
- **`local_diffusers` capability provider (MLX/Metal)** (auto-registered in `capability_registry.load_registry`): macOS Apple Silicon — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — n/a: MLX/Metal is Apple-Silicon-only; on other platforms the import fails and the registry silently falls through to API providers.
- **`web_fetch` browser tier (local Playwright/Chrome) used by web_consultation/web_extraction**: macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested; depends on a browser install and was not exercised off-Mac.
- **Pipeline-trace / tool-event sink directory under `ORA_HOME`** (`pipeline_trace.py:35-56`): macOS / WSL / Linux — works; `_resolve_ora_home` derives from `__file__` to avoid a literal-`~` directory when `HOME` is unset and refuses `~` paths. Windows-native — untested but filesystem-agnostic in principle.
- **`yt-dlp` URL import binary resolution** (`url_import.py:48`, 149): macOS — works. Windows-native — needs porting: the fallback path `/opt/homebrew/bin/yt-dlp` and the `brew install yt-dlp` guidance are Homebrew-specific. WSL / Linux — untested: `shutil.which` would find a system-installed binary, but the fallback and error message are Mac-shaped.

## 10. Visual Intelligence

### Problem

Ora's substantive analytical output has two coupled forms: prose carries the full reasoning, and a visual companion makes its relationships inspectable. For systems dynamics, decision structure, causal attribution, and argument mapping, prose alone hides structure that a diagram exposes immediately. A causal loop with three reinforcing and two balancing links is a five-line drawing and an unreadable page of English. Three failure modes stand between "the model reasoned well" and "the user saw a correct, readable, accessible diagram," and each one, left unaddressed, silently degrades the product.

First, **producer unreliability and revision drift.** The renderable contract is a strict, typed JSON envelope (`ora-visual`), but a producer can omit it, emit malformed JSON, or draw against prose that a later pipeline stage replaces. Gear 4 therefore contributes prose only; a branch diagram cannot survive consolidation merely because its labels still look plausible. Gear 2 and Gear 3 may contribute a candidate only when it remains paired with the exact prose revision selected for delivery. The terminal authority must recover or construct the visual from that final prose rather than trusting any diagram found earlier in the turn.

Second, **dishonest charts.** An LLM asked to draw a chart defaults to the dishonest form: truncated y-axes that exaggerate a trend, chartjunk, low-contrast palettes, unlabeled series. These are not rendering bugs; they are the model's learned priors. A chart that renders cleanly and lies is worse than no chart, because it launders a distortion through an authoritative-looking artifact.

Third, **inaccessibility.** A rendered diagram is, by default, a pixel blob to a screen-reader user and a dead end to a keyboard user. Without a deliberate accessibility layer, every visual the system produces is invisible to that audience.

Without this subsystem, visuals either fail silently, disagree with the delivered reasoning, erase canvas work, or appear misleading and inaccessible. The Visual Intelligence subsystem exists to prevent those failures while leaving the prose answer intact.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The central move is one **terminal visual authority over a strict semantic envelope**. Producers may offer candidates, but the final accepted prose is canonical. The authority validates candidates against that prose, holds producer envelopes outside text sent to later models, and uses a bounded selection and recovery sequence:

1. Honor an explicit user-selected image provider or visual type.
2. Accept a schema-valid candidate only when it is paired with the selected prose revision; deterministic Mermaid, DAGitty, and Structurizr recovery is limited to that same revision.
3. If no specialized candidate survives, synthesize from the final prose and then build the existing clause-grounded `concept_map` as the universal fallback. Numeric grounding and structural integrity remain hard blocks; redundancy receives one improvement attempt and then remains a nonblocking warning.
4. If bounded recovery still produces no valid envelope, persist a visible `failed` outcome with stage and reason. The prose remains delivered and unchanged.

The alternative I rejected is **making the analytical model emit render-ready SVG directly.** It is tempting: one round-trip, no schema, no compiler. I rejected it because SVG from a model is non-deterministic (the same reasoning yields a different drawing each time), unauditable (there is no typed structure for an honesty gate to inspect — you would be reduced to regexing pixels), and it forecloses the accessibility layer (alt-text and keyboard navigation need to know that *this* mark is a stock and *that* one is a flow). The strict-envelope-plus-swappable-producer design keeps a single auditable contract at the center and lets the messy, model-dependent part live at the edges where it can fail open.

Honesty is enforced by an **adversarial Tufte gate**, `visual_adversarial.py`, which runs 15 `_t*` rules plus LLM-prior-inversion, grounding, and rendered-artifact checks. Critical findings reject the visual candidate; they do not replace it with a table or pretend the visual succeeded. The authority continues through its bounded grounded recovery, then persists a conspicuous failure if nothing valid survives. This is the component that prevents truncated axes and chartjunk from being laundered through an authoritative-looking artifact.

Rendering has two explicit environments over one vendored compiler. Interactive V3 compiles in the browser and installs native Excalidraw objects or compiler-rendered artifacts. Agent and autonomous producers have no client, so the Node/jsdom CLI renders the first valid surviving envelope to a persisted SVG. The normal visual path launches no server-side browser; Playwright use elsewhere in Ora is unrelated to visual rendering.

The tradeoff is real and worth naming. This design pays for model-independence, honesty, accessibility, editable native output, and ownership-safe regeneration with bounded repair calls, a substantial vendored library set (Vega, Vega-Lite, Mermaid, D3, dagre, Ajv, viz-js, and a hand-written Structurizr parser), and a Node runtime for headless producers. A single-round direct-SVG design would be lighter and faster. I traded latency and disk for auditability and correctness, because for analytical diagrams a wrong-but-fast answer is a liability, not a feature.

**Open problem.** Emission telemetry makes candidate validity observable, but there is still no live reliability budget or success-rate threshold. Provider and model quality therefore remain environment-dependent even though malformed, ungrounded, and unrenderable outcomes now fail visibly rather than disappearing.

### Implementation

**Runtime entry.** `_run_visual_hook(response, context_pkg)` is the terminal authority for direct pipeline output, framework one-shots, and milestone execution. The assistant-message record begins in `building`; the hook either leaves valid envelopes for the client, materializes a headless artifact, establishes a narrow `not_applicable` exception, or records `failed`. Visual-hook availability is still guarded, but absence of the optional hook cannot remove the prose response.

**Data flow inside the hook** (braiding why with how):

- **Candidate isolation and revision pairing.** Gear 4 branch envelopes are discarded because consolidation follows them. Gear 2 and Gear 3 candidates are held outside downstream prose and reattached only when paired with the selected final revision. `_visual_recovery_texts` mines that selected prose only; it does not search superseded pipeline steps.
- **Validation and selection.** `_visual_process_response` validates every fenced block against the final prose, including schema, structural, numeric-grounding, and adversarial checks. Numeric grounding recognizes conventional three-digit comma, horizontal-space, and apostrophe groups in integers, signed decimals, and percentages in both prose and numeric visual labels. It compares equivalent values, including explicitly marked percentages, without joining separate numbers. Unsupported or mismatched numeric claims reject the visual block while preserving the prose. Every valid survivor remains available to interactive dispatch. Critical failures are trace evidence, not permission to alter the prose.
- **Bounded recovery.** The authority first attempts deterministic recovery from the selected revision, then interactive synthesis for configured modes, then the deterministic clause-grounded concept-map builder. Each successful candidate re-enters the same validation path. If all stages fail, the outcome is `failed` unless the turn positively qualifies for a narrow `not_applicable` reason.
- **Diagnostics and durable outcome.** Per-envelope diagnostics land in `step-visual-hook.json`. An exception from the primary visual processor never releases its input as a fallback: it strips complete unreviewed `ora-visual` blocks, visual-suppression markers, and any residual unterminated `ora-visual` opener while preserving the remaining prose and ordinary fenced code in source order. It stores a blocked diagnostic and a `failed` `visual_hook` outcome, and records `processor_exception` in the turn trace when one exists. If only the optional redundancy-improvement processor raises after the original visual passed review, the unreviewed replacement is discarded, the approved original remains, and the response plus its diagnostics, outcome, and, when tracing exists, trace disclose the failed attempt. The browser changes `building` to `ready` only after actual insertion; unsupported operations and dispatch errors use the Exhibits pane's nonblocking error surface and persist `failed`. Reopening a stalled `building` turn retries its durable envelope or records failure.

**Headless render.** `_materialize_noninteractive_visual` selects the first valid surviving envelope, calls `_render_visual_svg_cli`, and persists `visual-artifact.svg` plus `visual-artifact.json` beside the turn trace. `_render_visual_svg` delegates directly to the same Node/jsdom CLI. This is the load-bearing renderer for agent and autonomous producers and the SVG path used by vault export; it launches no browser.

**Dormant legacy helpers.** `_render_visual_svg_browser`, `_rasterize_svg_light`, and `_maybe_review_and_refine_visual` remain defined, but `_maybe_review_and_refine_visual` has no production caller. The old Playwright screenshot-critique loop is therefore not part of normal turn processing or export, and its mere presence must not be read as an active fallback.

**Client compiler.** `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/index.js` exposes `compileWithNav`: validate against the Ajv 2020 schema → dispatch through `dispatcher.js` → apply the accessibility layer (`alt-text-generator.js`, `aria-annotator.js`, and `keyboard-nav.js`) → run the rendered-artifact honesty pass for overlap, truncation, and contrast. A blocking artifact finding clears the artifact and becomes a dispatch failure; the client does not substitute a table or prose representation. The Findings prose remains intact, and the assistant message records the failure. The 15 renderer files cover 22 diagram kinds because Mermaid and Vega-Lite are multi-kind; `annotated_image` remains the separate Konva image-overlay path.

**Canvas and V3 dispatch.** `server/static/js/v3-visual-dispatch.js` extracts every parseable surviving envelope and hands the complete ordered set to `OraPanels.visual.onBridgeUpdate`; `v3-canvas-mount.js` serializes installation so every block lands in source order. Native structural types become editable Excalidraw objects, while position/value and deferred DSL types become assistant-owned rendered artifacts; Konva remains the fallback editor and SVG consumer. Four ownership fields — assistant visual identity, generation revision, semantic element identity, and original-generation fingerprint — distinguish untouched assistant output from user edits. `replace`, `update`, and assistant-issued `clear` remove only untouched assistant-owned material. User-created elements, uploads, viewport, and history survive; a directly modified native generation survives as a complete generation, and a modified assistant annotation group survives atomically with its attached generation. The dispatch key prevents a rerender of the same conversation turn from applying the set twice.

**Canvas file format.** `orchestrator/canvas_file_format.py` is the server-side reader/writer for `.ora-canvas` files (gzip-wrapped canonical JSON), a byte-identical mirror of the client `canvas-serializer.js`, used for vault export/autosave and server previews.

**Vision extraction.** `orchestrator/visual_extraction.py` (WP-4.3) invokes a vision-capable model on a user image, parses the result into `spatial_representation` JSON, validates with a single repair pass, and scores confidence — the inbound direction (image → structure) complementing the outbound envelope→SVG.

**Media adjuncts** (a related, Mac-bound cluster, not the diagram path):
- `plugins/video/backend/render.py` is the optional feature's **FFmpeg video** render engine. It remains separate from envelope→SVG visual rendering.
- `plugins/video/backend/media_capture.py` wraps FFmpeg for screen, microphone, and camera capture; AVFoundation on macOS is the working host path.
- `orchestrator/transcription.py` wraps `whisper-cli` (whisper.cpp) — ffmpeg 16 kHz mono extract → whisper-cli JSON → hallucination filter.
- `plugins/video/backend/waveform.py` shells `ffmpeg showwavespic` to a small PNG. Chapter 11 owns the plugin boundary and current portability detail.

**Library inventory with licenses** (versions and license text read from each `vendor/*/VERSION` and `LICENSE`): Vega 6.2.0 (BSD-3-Clause), Vega-Lite 6.4.2 (BSD-3-Clause), Mermaid 11.14.0 (MIT), D3 7.9.0 (ISC), dagre 0.8.5 (MIT — header "Permission is hereby granted, free of charge…", Copyright 2012-2014 Chris Pettitt), Ajv 8.18.0 2020-12 bundle (MIT), viz-js 3.26.0 (ships a Third-Party Notices file covering its Graphviz/Emscripten sublicenses — confirm before redistribution), structurizr-mini (hand-written parser + renderer, Context/Container only), and Konva (MIT, Anton Lavrenov) for the canvas. All vendored locally; no CDN; CSP-safe.

**Config surfaces.** `config/visual-schemas/specs/` holds the 23 JSON-Schema-2020-12 specs (verified count) plus example envelopes and guides. `config/mode-to-visual.json` (`_schema_version` 0.2, `_last_updated` 2026-06-13) sets per-mode `visual_types`, `relation_to_prose_default`, and `adversarial_strictness`; its `_comment` states it is synced manually to the vault reference "until generation is automated" (`config/mode-to-visual.json:2`). Environment flags: `ORA_VISUAL_RECOVER_SIBLINGS` (restore accept-any-sibling recovery), `ORA_VISUAL_RECOVER_ANY` (recover an unrequested diagram in interactive contexts; default off).

**Failure behavior separates prose continuity from visual integrity.** Prose delivery fails open: a hook, recovery, compiler, insertion, or persistence error does not remove the text answer. Visual integrity remains fail-closed: an invalid or dishonest artifact is not marked ready. Bounded recovery ends in a persisted, visible `failed` outcome with stage and reason; no table/prose substitution hides the missing visual.

### Evidence & Verification

**Python suites** (`orchestrator/tests/`): `test_visual_validator`, `test_visual_adversarial`, `test_visual_phase2_rules`, `test_visual_synthesis`, `test_visual_fallback`, `test_visual_extraction`, `test_visual_routing`, `test_visual_e2e`, `test_visual_merged_input`, `test_canvas_file_format`, `test_media_library_transcript`, `test_render_two_pass`, `test_waveform`. Recovery is exercised through the fallback and e2e suites.

**JS harness**: `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/tests/` with 39 case directories (verified: `ls tests/cases/` = 39), run via `node run.js`; plus a V3-dispatch case (`tests/cases/test-v3-visual-dispatch.js`, cited at 12 assertions in the module's audit note). Exact aggregate pass counts were not re-run in this pass — CLAUDE.md cites figures at various dates; treat those as stale until re-run.

**Live-fire**: a Tier A run across the visual-bearing modes was executed during development on 2026-04-18.

**How to confirm it works.** In an interactive turn with more than one valid envelope, confirm that every surviving block is installed in source order and that the assistant message moves from `building` to `ready` only after insertion. In an agent or autonomous turn, confirm that only the first valid envelope becomes `visual-artifact.svg` plus `visual-artifact.json`, with `renderer: node-jsdom-cli` and no browser launch. Force bounded recovery to exhaust and confirm that the prose remains present while the assistant message and Exhibits pane show the persisted `failed` stage and reason.

**Explicitly untested surfaces.** Live provider/model interpretation remains environment-dependent. The retained Playwright raster/critique helpers have no production caller and therefore provide no runtime coverage. **All non-macOS server platforms are untested** (see below). Video suggestion and preview evidence belongs to the optional feature in Chapter 11, not to the visual compiler. Reliability remains qualitative: there is no instrumented reliability budget, success-rate metric, or per-step percentage.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `orchestrator/boot.py::_run_visual_hook` first for terminal selection, bounded recovery, durable outcome assignment, and noninteractive handoff. Then read `server/static/js/v3-visual-dispatch.js` and `server/static/js/v3-canvas-mount.js` for all-block interactive dispatch and ownership-safe installation; `orchestrator/visual_recovery.py` for selected-revision recovery; `orchestrator/visual_adversarial.py` for honesty and grounding; and `tools/render-envelope.js` for the Node/jsdom headless renderer.

**Drift hotspots, in priority order:**

1. **Terminal authority must remain terminal.** Every output path, including framework one-shots and milestone execution, must pass the final selected prose through `_run_visual_hook`; Gear 4 branch envelopes must not leak back in after consolidation.
2. **Interactive and noninteractive multiplicity intentionally differ.** V3 installs every valid surviving block; `_materialize_noninteractive_visual` selects only the first. A shared helper that silently collapses both paths would change user-visible behavior.
3. **Ownership metadata is one safety mechanism, not decoration.** Assistant visual identity, generation revision, semantic element identity, and original-generation fingerprint jointly decide whether regeneration may replace material. Dropping any field can erase or strand user-modified work.
4. **`config/mode-to-visual.json` is manually synced** to the vault reference. Canonical-versus-operational drift remains possible.
5. **The 23 schemas ↔ 16 renderer files ↔ 22 synthesizable kinds mapping is non-obvious** because Mermaid and Vega-Lite are multi-kind and `annotated_image` is a separate Konva path. Hold the mapping, not the raw file counts.
6. **The Playwright renderer and screenshot-critique helpers are dormant.** They have no production caller; do not infer active behavior from their definitions. `render.py` is video, not diagrams. The noninteractive diagram renderer is the Node/jsdom `render-envelope.js` path.

### Platform compatibility

The interactive compiler, Excalidraw emitter, and retained Konva canvas are browser JavaScript with vendored, CSP-safe libraries. Noninteractive rendering is the Node/jsdom CLI and launches no browser. Server-side evidence remains macOS-only; every off-Mac label below stays conservative.

| Subsystem | macOS | Windows-native | WSL | Linux | Evidence / note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Client compiler + native Excalidraw emitter + accessibility review | works | works | n/a | works | Browser JS, vendored libraries, no CDN. `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/`. OS-independent by construction. |
| Excalidraw/Konva canvas dispatch and ownership preservation | works | works | n/a | works | Browser JS in `v3-visual-dispatch.js`, `v3-canvas-mount.js`, and `visual-panel.js`. |
| Noninteractive envelope→SVG render (Node + jsdom CLI) | works | untested | untested | untested | `_render_visual_svg_cli` and `render-envelope.js`; relies on `node` on PATH and the bundled jsdom/compiler dependencies. This is the normal agent/autonomous and vault-export path, not a Playwright fallback. |
| FFmpeg video render (`render.py`) | works | untested | untested | untested | `FFMPEG_BINARY = shutil.which("ffmpeg") or "/opt/homebrew/bin/ffmpeg"` (`render.py:71`). PATH lookup is portable; the fallback is a Homebrew-on-Apple-Silicon path. `drawtext` requires the homebrew-ffmpeg tap on Mac (per project memory). |
| Video-render font resolution (`_resolve_font_file`) | works | needs-porting | needs-porting | needs-porting | `render.py:247` candidates are all `/System/Library/Fonts` and `/Library/Fonts`; the final fallback is a hardcoded Mac path that will not exist elsewhere. macOS-only. |
| Screen/mic capture (`media_capture.py`) | works | not-implemented | n/a | not-implemented | macOS `avfoundation` only; the Windows branch raises `NotImplementedError("Windows capture lands in Phase 10")` (`media_capture.py:159, 259`). No Linux `x11grab` path exists. |
| Transcription (`transcription.py`, whisper.cpp) | works | untested | untested | untested | `whisper-cli` via PATH with `/opt/homebrew/bin/whisper-cli` fallback. whisper.cpp builds cross-platform, but binary path and models layout assume Mac. |
| Waveform (`waveform.py`) | works | untested | untested | untested | ffmpeg `showwavespic`; same ffmpeg-binary assumptions as above. |
| `WORKSPACE` / path form (`~/ora`, vault `~/Documents/vault`) | works | needs-porting | untested | untested | `os.path.expanduser("~/ora")` — tilde expands cross-platform, but a `%USERPROFILE%\ora` layout is untested and the vault path is Mac-specific. |
| JS test harness (`tests/run.js`, jsdom) | works | untested | untested | untested | `cd tests && node run.js`; node/jsdom are cross-platform but run only on macOS. |

No non-macOS server surface is claimed to work without evidence. The one surface with a *definite* off-Mac status is `media_capture.py`: its Windows path is coded-but-stubbed (raises `NotImplementedError`) and Linux is absent, so it is "not-implemented," not merely "untested." Cross-reference the Audio/Video Media & Timeline Editing chapter for the adjacent Mac-bound media-capture and render/export tooling.

## 11. Audio/Video Media & Timeline Editing

### Problem

A user may need ordinary audio/video transcription, or a complete editing surface
for capture, media organization, timeline work, preview, suggestions, and render.
Those are related experiences but different ownership boundaries. Transcription
is general Dialogue input. The editor is substantial and removable; making the
core import it everywhere would prevent Ora from starting cleanly without video.

The editing layer must also avoid destructive edits and unsafe deletion. A
timeline should refer to original media rather than rewrite it, and Delete Forever
must stop every video worker which could still write into the Dialogue before the
managed purge begins.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

Three decisions define the current layer.

**Video is the only configured production source in a plural first-party host.**
`server/feature_plugins.py` receives an explicit ordered source list and loads
the built-in Python implementation `plugins.video` only when the configured
`video` directory exists. By
default that checked descriptor-and-assets root is `<ora>/plugins/video`.
`ORA_FEATURE_PLUGINS_DIR` changes the parent of that checked root, but it does
not change the Python import: implementation code still comes from
`plugins.video`. The override is the removal-test seam, not alternate-package
discovery. The feature runs in the Ora process because its workers must share
the conversation lifecycle lock and deleted-state boundary. A separate service
would need a new cross-process safety protocol before it could preserve the same
purge guarantee. The accepted cost is that trusted first-party plugin code
shares the server's process.

**Registration is complete before installation.** Each package returns a validated
descriptor containing its source-matched id, static root, ordered scripts and
styles, routes, optional owner-bound providers, and Close/Delete lifecycle
callbacks. It receives a fixed
`FeaturePluginContext`, not the Flask application. Validation accepts only the
expected source id and static root, confines assets below that root,
requires declared files to exist, and rejects duplicate endpoint names. Only
then are routes, the static asset route, and page tags installed. An absent or
broken package is logged and returns no active descriptor; the core keeps
running. Validation precedes route installation, although Flask routes already
added before an unexpected mid-loop failure are not transactionally rolled back.

**Editing is non-destructive.** The plugin's media library stores protected
references and metadata rather than modifying a user's source file. Timeline
edits are normalized JSON state. Render resolves a snapshot of timeline and media
references into an FFmpeg command, leaving sources unchanged. This keeps undo and
re-edit cheap, at the cost of a dangling reference when an external source is
moved or removed. FFmpeg filter and codec availability remains the principal host
compatibility risk.

This is not generalized discovery, a public SDK, a marketplace, an installer,
version negotiation, dependency resolution, sandboxing, hot loading, or an
injection point into the analytical pipeline. Project Plugins remain the separate
external-project subprocess convention described in Chapter 17.

### Implementation

**Loader and contract.** `server/feature_plugins.py` defines `FeaturePlugin`,
`PluginRoute`, `FeaturePluginContext`, descriptor validation, route installation,
the plugin asset route, and asset-tag injection into `server/index-v3.html`.
The explicit context supplies two paths plus seventeen lifecycle, safe-file,
mutation-policy, settings, declared-credential, metadata, telemetry, and
async-capability helpers. It
is the current contract; the earlier seven-helper proposal is obsolete.

`plugins/video/__init__.py` registers the descriptor and three lifecycle
callbacks. `plugins/video/routes.py` declares all 31 plugin routes. They cover
capture; media library, URL import, protected-reference removal, transcript,
waveform and suggestions; timeline; preview; render and watermark upload; and
video capability/state calls. `plugins/video/runtime.py` holds the provided
context so package modules do not import `server.app`.

**Plugin-owned server modules.** The package owns its capture, media-library,
preview, render, timeline, URL-import, video-suggestion, and waveform backends
below `plugins/video/backend/`. The suggestion framework and schema are also
plugin-local. Removing the package therefore removes both the suggestion
implementation and the instructions which define its output.

**Core-retained services.** `orchestrator/transcription.py` and
`orchestrator/vault_transcript.py` remain general Dialogue services.
`orchestrator/job_queue.py` and its core routes/lifecycle remain shared by
non-video work. `server/static/transcribe-input.js` retains Inquiry-pane A/V
drop. `server/static/js/v3-capability-async.js` remains the shared browser
bootstrap; video consumes it through the context's async-capability registry.

**Paid async-provider contract.** Replicate `video_generates` and `style_trains` require an existing live Dialogue. Before provider contact, the shared queue durably records the `submitting` transition.
Before polling, it records the exact prediction ID with the same Dialogue and local job binding. Restart reattaches to that ID without resubmission.
Submission and queued cancellation are lock-ordered, so a queued cancellation either wins before any provider call or becomes durable intent on the active job.
Polling, terminal changes, cancellation, and artifact materialization authenticate the surviving Dialogue/job owner; artifacts are written only while that exact owner remains live.

**Browser installation.** After complete validation, the host replaces the
single `<!-- ORA_FEATURE_PLUGIN_ASSETS -->` placeholder with every loaded
descriptor's ordered styles and scripts. `plugins/video/static/video-plugin.js` registers four host
seams: the Exhibits `video` pane through `OraPanes`; Video editor, Browse Dialogue
media, and Send canvas image controls through `OraMounts`; the media browser
through `OraBrowseOverlays`; and **Settings → Video** through
`OraSettingsSections`. It also registers `/video` and releases pane ownership on
new-Dialogue or Dialogue-selection events. With no descriptor, the placeholder
is empty and none of those registrations execute.

**Media and render flow.** The plugin library records conversation-owned
references and metadata. Protected media-reference removal compares the approved entry with the current cached entry under the existing media-library lock and returns `409` without removing a substituted entry, while the route retains core state-file reauthentication immediately before the protected effect. Timeline state is normalized on read and write, and
render receives a snapshot of the timeline and library rather than reading a
moving edit. FFmpeg performs trimming, padding, overlays, watermarking, mixing,
encoding, preview frames/proxies, waveform work, and capture where the host
supports it. `plugins.video.backend.render.RenderManager` starts FFmpeg in its
own daemon thread and exposes render state to the plugin routes. The core shared
job queue remains available for non-video work; it does not execute this render.

**Lifecycle.** Close invokes `on_release`: finished capture, import, and render
records plus timeline/library caches are released, while active work and owned
media remain. Delete Forever invokes `on_quiesce` before core purge to stop or
tombstone capture, import, preview, render, and library work; after the purge,
`on_clear` removes timeline state, owned upload staging, and render lookup state.
Lifecycle errors are logged and collected without falsely claiming that an
optional callback succeeded.

Transcript and suggestion routes hold the existing conversation read scope from entry lookup through transcript file I/O, JSON parsing and normalization, optional suggestion derivation, and final response construction; Delete Forever therefore waits for an admitted read to finish, while a Dialogue it has made unavailable returns `410` without transcript or suggestion content.

**Failure and removal.** A missing source or an import, registration,
descriptor, provider, or route-install failure logs a specific error, skips that
source, and continues loading later explicit sources. An unexpected mid-route-loop
failure can leave routes Flask already accepted until restart; the loader does
not claim transactional rollback. Removing or renaming `plugins/video` and
restarting removes its 31 routes, assets, settings, controls, pane, overlay, and
slash command while leaving transcription, Inquiry A/V drop, and the shared job
queue operational. Removing code does not itself delete previously written media.

### Evidence & Verification

The landed implementation proves the boundary through focused behavior: startup
with the package present, startup with its root absent, rejection of invalid
descriptors and routes, plugin-only route and asset ownership, all four browser
registries, removal of the core `/video` registration, and the distinction
between Close release and Delete Forever quiesce/clear.

The accepted extraction also keeps shared services live when the plugin is
absent. The non-destructive removal test points `ORA_FEATURE_PLUGINS_DIR` at a
parent with no `video` child and restarts Ora; this changes only the checked
descriptor/assets root, so the built-in `plugins.video` import is never attempted.
Removing or renaming the bundled directory exercises the same absent state.
Confirm Ora boots and video routes/assets/UI are absent, then confirm Inquiry A/V
drop, transcription, and the shared job queue remain. A real FFmpeg render is
still the appropriate end-to-end check for host codecs, filters, fonts, device
capture, and output media; documentation or route tests cannot prove those host
capabilities.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read the boundary before the backend: `server/feature_plugins.py`, then
`plugins/video/__init__.py`, `routes.py`, and `runtime.py`; then the four browser
registries and `video-plugin.js`; finally the backend module relevant to the
behavior under review.

The highest drift risks are a plugin importing `server.app`, a new route or asset
bypassing descriptor validation, browser UI added directly to the core page,
Close killing active work, Delete Forever purging before quiesce, video-only
schemas moving back into core, or shared transcription/job infrastructure moving
into the plugin. Treat the 31-route descriptor, explicit context fields, browser
seams, and three lifecycle callbacks as one contract when changing any part.

### Platform compatibility

The feature boundary is platform-neutral Python and browser code, but the work it
exposes retains host dependencies:

- **Plugin loading and browser registration** — standard Python import, Flask,
  and browser JavaScript. Exercised on macOS; untested on Windows-native, WSL,
  and Linux. An unavailable package is skipped on every platform.
- **FFmpeg / ffprobe** — the plugin backends prefer binaries on `PATH` and retain
  Homebrew fallbacks. Software codecs are portable once present, but filters,
  fonts, and installed codec sets vary. macOS is exercised; other platforms are
  untested or need porting where a Mac font path is assumed.
- **Screen, microphone, and webcam capture** — macOS uses AVFoundation and is the
  working path. Windows-native and Linux capture remain unimplemented or
  unverified; WSL has no claimed desktop capture surface.
- **Local Whisper transcription** — core transcription works on macOS with
  `whisper-cli` and locally installed models. Other hosts are untested; the cloud
  transcription alternatives are network-dependent rather than OS-specific.
- **Atomic timeline/library state and subprocess cancellation** — use portable
  Python primitives, but only the macOS concurrency and process behavior is
  exercised. Windows handle and termination semantics remain unverified.
- **Removal behavior** — does not depend on FFmpeg: no valid descriptor means no
  video routes or assets. Core startup, transcription, Inquiry A/V drop, and the
  shared job queue remain the required cross-platform fallback.

# Part IV — Supervision & Governance

## 12. Meta-Layer Observation and Explicit Project Oversight

### Problem

Long-lived projects still need reality-contact signals: a milestone checkbox can change without evidence, a corpus reference can go stale, and a child project's progress may matter to its parent. Those facts justify observation and durable event records. They do not justify an autonomous judgment or transition engine.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The installed project-oversight router is observational. File events and exact time-caused deadlines feed the existing event bus. `oversight_router.process_event` records an event, suppresses Stealth persistence, prevents fan-out recursion, and may surface a child event to its parent. It never invokes Process Coherence, parses a framework verdict, mutates project state, or dispatches Programming work.

Process Coherence v5.0 is instead an explicitly invoked project/framework judgment. When a caller chooses it at a boundary configured by Oversight Configuration v3.0, it returns exactly `PROCEED`, `REVISE`, or `ESCALATE`. The caller retains responsibility for any permitted next action. These labels are unrelated to Programming's `CONTINUE`, `FIX`, `DONE`, and `ASK USER` outcomes.

This removes the former `ORA_OVERSIGHT_LIVE` fork, automatic context-to-model route, seven-directive parser, REVISE counter, and verdict-action dispatcher. There is no dormant live mode to enable and no simulated-supervision warning.

That observational boundary does not mean Ora has no Trigger facility. **Scheduled Triggers are separate, explicitly authored activation objects** built on the same exact-event and persisted-deadline substrate. A Trigger binds an already registered project tool, a user-invocable framework, or the narrow manual-only email action; it does not turn the oversight router into a judgment engine, create a Programming Run, or restore the retired governed-Process stack.

### Implementation

- **Event/deadline daemon.** `oversight_daemon.py` runs the OS file-event lane, persisted exact-deadline lane, and bounded watchdog introduced by G1.10. Time is used only where time is the actual cause; file-caused work runs from its event.
- **Event bus.** `oversight_events.emit` normalizes and records non-Stealth events, then invokes registered observational handlers without allowing one handler failure to break the bus.
- **Router.** `oversight_router.py` produces `logged_only`, `fan_out_audit_only`, or `stealth_suppressed`. Fan-out records carry the existing recursion sentinel and cannot fan out again.
- **Parent visibility.** `oversight_relationships.py` may append one managed child-update derivative to a parent's Decision Log. The parent is informed; no parent-side judgment is invoked.
- **Durable utilities.** `oversight_actions.py` retains lock-protected managed Decision Log derivatives, reversible privacy/delete support, the existing human queue, and action-log utilities used by active callers. Every Paused-queue addition, metadata rewrite, and entry removal—including lifecycle purge—is serialized by the queue's shared cross-process sibling lock and committed through a unique same-directory temporary that is flushed, fsynced, and atomically replaced. An interruption before replacement preserves the prior bytes. It contains no Process Coherence verdict parser or dispatcher.
- **Health.** `oversight_health.py` reports actual daemon/thread or telemetry degradation. It no longer reports a configuration-disabled judgment path.
- **Explicit frameworks.** The canonical behavior of project-level judgment lives in `Framework — Oversight Configuration.md` v3.0 and `Framework — Process Coherence.md` v5.0, not in the event router.

### Trigger routes, UI, and execution contract

`orchestrator/triggers.py` exposes four causes: explicit manual request, an OS file change under a live watched root, a named-timezone calendar occurrence, and successful completion of another Trigger. The corresponding actions are a registered project tool, a user-invocable framework, or `email_send`; email remains manual-only. Literal shell commands are refused. A calendar Trigger also requires a written reason that time itself is the cause and discloses the local-app boundary: Ora acts only while it is running, with no cron, launchd, interval scan, sweep, or clock fallback.

The browser places this facility in the **Scheduled** group inside Oversight rather than restoring the old Trigger Manager. Its default `/api/triggers` request lists drafts and active or paused Triggers; retired entries are excluded unless a caller explicitly requests them. `sidebar-oversight.js` shows the active count, lane-availability or repeated-restart warnings, Ora's separate internal-deadline count, recent firing history, and the next due time. The `+` form declares a cause and one registered action. The email-draft composer resolves its visible sender identity from the global default Persona with project precedence disabled, unless the author explicitly chooses another available Persona. The saved draft displays that resolved Persona; an unavailable explicit choice is rejected rather than silently substituted. Draft creation alone neither activates nor approves the Trigger, contacts the provider, nor grants send authority. Before activation, **Review and activate…** renders the material action—including exact project-tool arguments or stdin, framework input and its resolved Project nexus/profile when present, or the email action—along with the specification digest, bound program identity, calendar justification when present, and intermittency notice; activation succeeds only for that reviewed version. The same visible card can inspect its bound message, run once, pause, resume, retire, or—before provider contact—cancel and retire an approved email action.

For a project-tool action, activation is revocable standing authority for that one exact Trigger specification. The approved binding covers the Project nexus, canonical registered root, manifest, registered command, declared interface, resolved executable and script identities, and arguments/stdin digest. The exact authenticated absolute executable path becomes the child argv's first element at both the project-tool and project-slash subprocess boundaries; Ora never asks the subprocess launcher to resolve the manifest spelling again. The root is also the child working directory and `ORA_PROJECT_ROOT`, so re-registering identical bytes elsewhere is a binding change. Every event carries the specification and admission identity it observed. An active firing compares both under the Trigger-store lock before authority or claim, then passes immutable action, binding, specification, and admission snapshots to the worker and supplies the binding to `invoke_project_tool(expected_binding=...)` for its final pre-spawn check. Pausing, retiring, or editing prevents a new claim; resuming mints a new admission identity, and an edit after claim cannot substitute later draft work. A manual draft or paused project-tool run is outside activation and instead uses the existing Paused one-shot approval path. Both forms create authenticated System Protection start and terminal receipts without adding a generic Trigger entry to `tool_events` standing allows.

The server exposes the complete surface directly: `GET /api/triggers` and `/api/triggers/actions`; `POST /api/triggers`; `GET` and `PATCH /api/triggers/<trigger_id>`; `GET .../review` and `.../inspect`; and `POST .../activate`, `.../lifecycle`, `.../rollback`, and `.../run`. These are ordinary localhost APIs backed by the Trigger service, not aliases for Programming or Process Coherence.

**Local routine descriptor (current scoped foundation).** Existing Trigger create, read, list, and activation-review views, plus `/api/triggers/actions`, carry a `routine` object. Expanded Scheduled cards and **Review and activate…** render those server-supplied facts rather than browser-owned promises: execution target `local`; authority bound to the exact action binding (and action kind on a Trigger-specific view); firing-receipt and error-debug availability; remote execution unavailable; idle-sleep protection advertised only when the host supports it (currently when `/usr/bin/caffeinate` is executable on macOS), applying only while an action is actively running and ending with that action; and no ability for Ora to wake a sleeping Mac. The browser adds no execution capability, and the existing Trigger lifecycle, firing-receipt, and error controls are unchanged. This remains response metadata on the existing service; it does not create a remote runner, wake adapter, new service, store, schema, or daemon.

**macOS active-run sleep boundary.** Inside the spawned action process, `_active_run_sleep_protection` begins immediately before `_execute_action` and starts `/usr/bin/caffeinate -i -w <action-process-pid>`. If the helper is unavailable, cannot launch, or exits before the assertion is established, the action refuses to start unprotected. Normal success, action error, provider-callback error, and cancellation leave the action scope and terminate/reap the helper before a result or error crosses back to the parent. Timeout, a parent callback failure, or action-process death terminates and reaps the action boundary; `-w` also binds assertion release to that process's death. Other platforms continue the action without the macOS helper and make no sleep-protection claim. This prevents ordinary idle system sleep only while real Trigger action work is executing; it neither keeps the machine awake merely because Ora is open nor wakes a sleeping machine.

Durability is ordered around the point where work becomes externally observable:

- A watched-file publication carries a stable `publication_id`. Under the existing emitter lock and the event log's cross-process file lock, `oversight_events.emit` removes any unterminated append fragment and scans the complete records first. A durable delivery marker is authoritative: the emitter fsyncs the log, does not prepare or recreate a router plan, invokes no handler or sink, and performs only the same narrow idempotent router acknowledgment before allowing the watcher checkpoint to advance. That acknowledgment treats an already-completed or retention-pruned claim as success; a malformed or identity-mismatched surviving claim still fails. When no marker exists, the installed production router claims the delivery before a new event row is appended and freezes the complete original publication payload plus the first resolved parent nexus, canonical PED destination, rendered child-update content, and synthesized event/action payloads. This preparation writes only the internal claim; it performs no router-log, parent-PED, or fan-out audit effect. Only after that complete plan is durable does the emitter fsync one complete event line. Every retry reauthenticates and reuses the exact claimed payload and parent plan: a crash before the event row or changed child routing metadata cannot retarget it, and an unavailable original PED leaves the same delivery claimed and retryable. A handler or sink failure propagates while that frozen claim remains nonterminal, and startup recovery exempts only this claim type so the watcher rescan can resume it. After every router, parent-PED, and fan-out audit effect succeeds, the router durably records an effects receipt inside the still-claimed row; a retry that sees the receipt returns success without replaying those idempotent effects. A separate delivery marker in the event log is then fsynced, and only afterward does the emitter acknowledge the matching claim as completed.
- Every firing first validates its exact admission authority and claims one idempotent `EventLedger` identity. The claimed worker input is immutable even if the editable Trigger later changes. A calendar deadline persisted before admission identities existed is reported once as stale after upgrade: its action does not run, and the active Trigger arms its successor with the current authenticated payload. On startup, the daemon restores interrupted event claims and replays unfinished Trigger-completion deliveries before arming active calendar Triggers.
- Before a successful source firing is marked complete, the Trigger service persists every active dependent delivery with that dependent's exact specification and admission identity. After source completion, one exact proof must authenticate the same source event id, firing type, Trigger, specification, admission, and non-empty completion time. The service durably binds that proof to every matching staged delivery before attempting any child. A proof-save failure therefore runs no child and the proof-less internal row is omitted from list/detail projection rather than falsely reporting source success. On startup, replay takes its pending snapshot under the Trigger-store lock and releases it, reads any still-live completed source row under the separate ledger lock, derives the same proof, and then atomically backfills all matching deliveries under only the Trigger-store lock before child attempts or later source pruning. The same proof predicate governs replay and projection. Once proof is durable, an unfinished delivery survives restart even after the source ledger row is pruned. Trigger list/detail responses then project it separately from firing history as `pending_completions`, exposing only its source identities and times plus `pending_retry` or `blocked_by_trigger_change`; private proof and admission digests remain internal. The Scheduled card shows a collapsed pending/blocked badge and an expanded explanation, while `/trigger list` and `/trigger show` render a separate **Pending completions** section. A changed or reactivated dependent is identified as targeting an earlier approved version: Ora does not run the current action, does not synthesize a firing, and leaves the existing delivery visibly pending.
- Immediately before provider I/O, the action child requests contact permission and blocks until the parent durably records `provider_contacted` and acknowledges it. A callback failure or parent loss therefore permits no provider work while the ledger still claims pre-contact cancellation.
- Real action work crosses a spawned process boundary with one whole-firing deadline (`ORA_TRIGGER_FIRING_TIMEOUT_SEC`, default one hour). On POSIX, Ora sends termination signals to the known action process group and verifies that group and its wrapper are gone. A descendant can create a new session and leave that group, so a deadline reached after the action-start barrier remains durably claimed as `termination_unacknowledged`: Ora writes no terminal protected receipt, releases no overlap guard, and restart recovery keeps later firings blocked even when the recorded root and group are absent. On Windows, the held wrapper is assigned to a uniquely and globally named, non-inheritable Job Object with kill-on-close and die-on-unhandled-exception enabled and breakaway disabled before the parent durably records the Job identity and acknowledges action start; global-name creation failure or any pre-existing same-name object refuses before assignment and starts no action work. The persisted identity binds the exact root PID and Windows creation time, current active count and PID/creation identities, accumulated observed membership, assignment and containment flags, and the noninherited sole-owner handle facts. The existing duplex connection keeps the wrapper held after success, error, result-transfer failure, and during parent unwind. Ora persists actual Job membership before releasing a completed wrapper, then terminates the Job; timeout and cleanup terminate the still-held Job directly. Kernel Job membership continues to contain a late descendant after its intermediate parent exits, so no point-in-time parent graph is called complete. Terminal evidence and the in-process running-claim release wait for a zero `ActiveProcesses` snapshot captured through the original owner handle, durably saved through the existing ledger callback, and a dead wrapper. Restart may trust that persisted owner-zero fact; otherwise, any successfully reopened same-named Job remains ambiguous—even when empty or apparently matching—because a name can identify a recreated object, so Ora neither terminates it nor releases the claim. Authenticated not-found releases only when the validated global name and assignment, containment, kill-on-close, non-breakaway, noninheritance, and sole-owner facts prove that loss of the original handle killed every member. Any other setup, query, termination, persistence, close, open, or recovery ambiguity leaves the protected start receipt and firing claim nonterminal and blocks another firing.
- General Oversight health evaluates telemetry completeness before returning a clean result. The Scheduled panel itself warns only when an active Trigger's deadline lane is down or when a relevant event lane is repeatedly restarting; firing-evidence or telemetry degradation belongs to the general Oversight health surface. Health warnings do not themselves block the rest of Ora.

### Evidence & Verification

The active tests exercise Stealth suppression, observational routing, fan-out recursion prevention, managed Decision Log derivative preservation/removal, event/deadline liveness, system-protection boundaries, Trigger authoring/lifecycle, exact-event and deadline firing, process-bound timeout cleanup, watcher-publication de-duplication, completion-delivery replay, and health degradation. Runtime behavior—not old routing tables or historical papers—is authoritative.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `oversight_router.py::process_event` for the complete router authority boundary, `oversight_events.py` and `oversight_daemon.py` for event/deadline observation, `triggers.py` for the separate activation/firing contract, and `oversight_actions.py` only for its remaining durable utilities. Historical documents that describe automatic Process Coherence invocation, seven directives, governed Process Runs, or `ORA_OVERSIGHT_LIVE` do not describe the installed system. Historical claims that all Trigger facilities were removed are also too broad: only the old process-bound Trigger surface is gone.
## 13. Execution-Review Architecture

### Problem

An orchestrator that can touch reality — spawn a shell, `git push`, fetch a URL, call an MCP server, run a project tool, write a file — has two failure modes that no amount of model self-narration can catch, because the model is the thing being trusted.

The first is the auto-approve escape hatch. Ora's dispatcher has a permission mode. When a user (or a background configuration) sets it to auto-approve, every tool call the model emits runs without a human in the loop. That is the intended ergonomics for a long analytical turn — you do not want to approve forty file reads by hand. But the same setting will carry an irreversible action (`rm -rf`, a force-push, a `curl | sh`) or a secret-sensitivity read (a credential file, a private key) straight through to execution. The permission mode is a single dial, and turning it to "fast" turns off the brake on the one action class where the brake matters most. This is a privilege-escalation-by-convenience race: the dangerous call and the harmless call travel the same path, gated by the same dial.

The second is the reviewer grading the wrong artifact. When a downstream verifier — human or model — reads a turn's output and sees "I verified the tests pass and the migration is safe," it is reading a *producer claim*, a sentence the producing model wrote about its own work. A fluent claim out-argues a silent failure. If the model never actually ran the tests, the claim is indistinguishable in the text from the case where it did. The reviewer grades the model's homework using the model's own answer key. Without an independent record of what the turn *mechanically did* — what it mutated, what sources it read, what left the machine, how sensitive any of it was — there is no ground truth to check the narration against. The model grades itself, and confident prose wins.

Execution Review is the always-on instrumentation-and-gating layer that makes what a turn did to reality mechanically observable, so that neither failure mode depends on trusting the narration. I will define the load-bearing terms as they arrive; the first is *turn*, meaning one user-input-to-final-output cycle of the pipeline, which is the unit everything here is scoped to.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The architectural move is to split what-was-declared from what-was-observed onto two clocks, and to make the before-clock deterministic and fail-closed.

**Two clocks.** The *before-clock* runs before the turn executes: a deterministic risk classifier assigns a *risk tier* (one of `light` / `standard` / `high-risk` / `irreversible`, an ordered severity estimate computed with no model call), and a fail-closed capability gate stands at the single point where the model's tool calls enter execution. The *after-clock* runs after the turn: it folds the mechanical tool-event log — one JSON line recorded per action at the invocation boundary, never from narration — into a small set of observed signals, and compares them against what the before-clock declared. Declaration and observation never share a code path, so a divergence between "I said this was a read-only turn" and "the log shows an external write" is structurally recordable rather than something the model can paper over.

**Fail-closed at the single chokepoint, independent of permission mode.** The gate lives at `dispatcher.py:735`, the one model-facing call site through which every dispatched tool passes. It runs *before* the existing permission check and *independently of the permission mode*. An action classified as irreversible, secret-sensitivity, or — critically — *unknown* is blocked regardless of whether the dispatcher is set to auto-approve. The rationale is the Unix principle of a chokepoint: you enforce an invariant at the one place all traffic crosses, not at every caller. The manifest's default for an unclassified action is fail-closed: unknown means irreversible-and-secret means blocked (`FAIL_CLOSED`, `tool_events.py:101-106`). The tradeoff is real and I will not soften it — a novel tool with no declared axes is refused until someone classifies it, which trades convenience for the guarantee that auto-approve can never carry an unaudited capability. That is the correct trade for this class of risk; a system that fails open on the unknown case has no gate at all.

**Structural separation of evidence from judgment.** The after-clock's output is a packet whose lanes come in two kinds, and the separation is enforced by the type system rather than by discipline. An `EvidenceLane` is a mechanical, verifiable check that yields a real verdict. A `JudgmentLane` is interpretive — a taste call — and *by construction has no `verdict` or `sufficient` field to hold a verdict* (`execution_packet.py:81-88`). Taste cannot masquerade as a passing check because there is no slot for it to occupy. The renderer then orders the packet evidence-first and producer-claim-last, and labels the producer claim as unverified text (`render_for_review`, `execution_packet.py:236`). The reviewer sees what the turn mechanically did before it sees what the turn said about itself.

**The rejected alternative: a parallel review pipeline.** The obvious design is a second Gear-3/Gear-4 adversarial pass that reviews the first turn's work — a separate reviewer stage with its own model calls. I rejected it. It doubles the pipeline surface, it invites the reviewer to re-derive the producer's claims from the same text (grading the homework again, one layer up), and it is expensive. Instead the packet is a *richer form of the existing terminal deliverable*, built at the same `run_pipeline` terminal the deliverable already exits through — same machinery, richer output. There is one construction site per gear surface, not a parallel apparatus.

**The rejected alternative: model-driven risk classification.** I also rejected asking a model to rate the turn's risk. A model-scored tier reintroduces the original problem — the thing being gated writes its own gate input — and adds latency and nondeterminism to the before-clock, which must run on every turn. The before-clock is therefore a deterministic pattern classifier: turn-head regex floors (Stage A) plus an optional mode-declared `## RISK TIER` heading (Stage B), no model call (`classify_tier`, `risk_gate.py:293`). The tradeoff is that a regex table is coarse and will misclassify novel phrasings. I accept that and mitigate it two ways: the tier is a *floor* that fails safe upward (the failsafe tier on any classifier error is `high-risk`, `risk_gate.py:67,337`), and the whole Stage-A pattern table is flagged in-code as PROVISIONAL and un-calibrated (`risk_gate.py:20,211`). It is retunable, not settled.

**Catalog versus contract — the distinction that keeps a green run honest.** The deepest design commitment sits at the boundary between what is landed and what is not. A *catalog* (`.ora/evidence.yaml`, Phase 5) says what checks *exist* in a repo. A task-specific *Evidence Contract*, produced at planning time, says what checks *this task* must pass. The distinction is load-bearing: a green evidence run means nothing you knew to check broke — it does not mean the right problem was solved. Conflating "all declared checks passed" with "the task succeeded" is the same category error as the fluent producer claim, one level up. The architecture keeps them separate so that an empty evidence-lane list is treated as *not sufficient* — evidence owed — rather than vacuously passed (`all_evidence_sufficient`, `execution_packet.py:97-105`). This is the honest-by-construction stance; it is also, as the next section makes plain, the part that is designed and implemented-in-a-worktree but not merged and not installed.

**Open problems.** The `enforcement_model` label — which says whether a gate actually constrained the action or merely observed it — is hardcoded `in_harness` in the packet builder with an explicit TODO (`execution_packet.py:352-360`). It is honest for a core in-harness gear turn and wrong for any orchestrated, opaque-shell, or MSI-custom path that later produces a packet; correct enumeration is owned by a future phase. The declared-versus-observed output-type consistency note is wired end-to-end but dormant, because no mode declares an `output_type` — it stays `unknown` on every live turn (`consistency_note`, `execution_packet.py:155-174`). And the risk-tier floors are provisional. None of these are latent bugs; they are named incompletions.

### Implementation

The subsystem is three modules plus two shared substrates, wired at two lockstep seams. I will braid the why into the how.

**Phase 1 — recorder, manifest, gate (`orchestrator/tool_events.py`, gate wired at `dispatcher.py:735`).** Three things share this module deliberately, because they share one lifecycle — the always-on instrumentation substrate. First, the *capability manifest*: three orthogonal axes layered on top of the dispatcher's existing function axis without touching it — `mutability` (`read` / `reversible_write` / `external_write` / `irreversible`), `sensitivity` (`public` / `private` / `sensitive` / `secret`), `egress` (`none` / `local` / `external`) — plus an `enforcement_model` label and the fail-closed default for unknown actions (`tool_events.py:87-106`). Second, the *recorder*: `record()` writes one JSON line per action invocation, mechanically, at the boundary — never from narration. It writes to the active pipeline-trace turn directory when a turn is live, else to the global sink (`data/tool-events.jsonl`, resolved via `runtime_paths`). The write uses `os.open` with `O_APPEND` for atomic concurrent appends (`tool_events.py:563`). Recording is two-layer redacted: a per-path sensitivity check plus a content scrub, and a secret-sensitivity event is reduced to existence-only (`_redact_for_record`, `tool_events.py:463`). Third, the *gate*: `gate()` (`tool_events.py:836`) runs before execution and independently of the permission mode. A blocked action is denied immediately and queued to the oversight Paused queue; approval mints a one-shot token that lets exactly one re-issued matching call through (`grant_approval` / `check_and_consume_approval`, `tool_events.py:610,738`). At `dispatcher.py:735` the call site invokes `tool_events.gate(...)` and, when `decision.allowed` is false, returns `decision.message` — the model gets a gate refusal string, not the tool's effect.

The failure contract here is the reliability spine: recording is best-effort and never raises, but is never silent — a failed write sets a telemetry-incomplete flag that is stamped onto every subsequent event and surfaced via `get_telemetry_health()` (`tool_events.py:422-454`); failures also ride the oversight bus through `_note_failure`. Gate decisions fail closed regardless of whether their recording or queueing succeeded. `ORA_TOOL_EVENTS=off` disables the *write* for test suites — but the docstring is explicit and the code enforces it: the gate still runs and still fails closed even with recording off (`record` docstring and guard, `tool_events.py:508-520`). This is the component that prevents the auto-approve-carries-a-dangerous-action failure mode. The failure surface is qualitative and only partly instrumented: a write failure is counted and flagged (telemetry-incomplete), but there is no success-rate or per-step reliability figure — none is instrumented, and the guarantee is the named contract (never-raises, never-silent, fail-closed), not a measured number.

**Phase 2/3 — tier, hold, criteria, after-clock fold (`orchestrator/risk_gate.py`).** The before-clock is `classify_tier` / `assign_tier` (`risk_gate.py:293,710`): Stage A matches turn-head regex floors seeded from the gate vocabulary (bash irreversible flags, `curl`/`wget` egress), Stage B reads an optional mode `## RISK TIER` heading; the higher floor wins, irreversible over external-write, with no model call. An `irreversible`-tier turn is *held before the executor runs* unless a valid task token is present — `evaluate_hold` (`risk_gate.py:814`) returns a plain-text pause reply and writes a durable Paused card, and it fails closed to a hold on any error. This is the hard boundary: post-hoc routing is permitted only in reversible tiers, so an irreversible action is stopped before it happens, not audited after. `apply_criteria` (`risk_gate.py:935`) runs an acceptance-criteria pass for standard-and-above tiers, failing closed to `WARN` (standard) or `HOLD` (high-risk and above) rather than silently dropping to light execution.

At risk-gated entry points that have both an exact caller string and a cleaned or clarification-enriched request, `assign_tier` classifies the two forms independently under the same mode floor, sticky tier, trivial-text status, and explicit `/risk` override, then enforces whichever result is more severe. Cleanup therefore cannot erase a risk signal. The exact caller string also binds an irreversible hold and its one-use approval token: case, whitespace, and wrapper text remain identity-bearing, so a token cannot admit a merely similar request. Downstream criteria and execution, and each path's existing trace and hold-description behavior, otherwise remain unchanged. Foreground, server, direct/fallback, typed and picker-selected Framework, and clarification-resume paths share this dual-form rule; a picker-selected Framework also remains part of the existing structural identity. Synthesized paths that genuinely have one risk input retain single-form classification. This adds no approval store or alternate gate—the existing Paused card and token lifecycle is unchanged.

The after-clock is `fold_route_observed` (`risk_gate.py:548`), which reads the tool-event log scoped by `conversation_id` and turn timestamp and produces the observed signals: `any_mutation` and `max_mutability` (Phase 2's signal 1), and `source_read_suspected` with its channels and public-safe candidate reads (Phase 3's signal 2 — did the output make claims that imply it read a source it did not disclose). `record_route_observed` (`risk_gate.py:983`) is the terminal recorder that writes the divergence. Note one subtle threshold: `source_read_suspected` is driven partly by whether the output is *substantive*, gated on `_MIN_SUBSTANTIVE_OUTPUT_CHARS = 24`, and the candidate list is capped at `_MAX_SOURCE_CANDIDATES = 64` — both flagged PROVISIONAL in-code (`risk_gate.py:114-128`).

**The single-fold contract.** `record_route_observed` folds the log *once* and returns the signals; the terminal then builds the packet *separately* from those returned signals. There is no second fold. Any refactor that folds again double-counts mutations — this is called out as a drift class and it is worth internalizing before touching either seam.

**Phase 4 — lanes, packet, renderer (`orchestrator/execution_packet.py`).** `route_lanes` (`execution_packet.py:174`) takes the already-folded signals and routes them into `EvidenceLane` / `JudgmentLane` instances. `EvidenceLane.sufficient` is tri-state — `None` means unfilled, which means evidence-owed, which means *not* sufficient; `True`/`False` only once a runner judges it (`execution_packet.py:65-77`). `JudgmentLane` structurally omits the verdict field. `build_execution_packet` (`execution_packet.py:322`) consumes the folded signals plus the in-gear text-review verdict threaded on `context_pkg['execution_review']` and assembles a polymorphic `ExecutionPacket` with reserved blocks for fields a later phase fills. `construct_and_write` (`execution_packet.py:436`) writes `execution-packet.json` trace-local.

**The two wiring seams, in lockstep.** The before-clock and after-clock are invoked at both primary gear terminals. In `boot.py` (foreground/terminal): `assign_tier` (~9279), `evaluate_hold` (~9289), `apply_criteria` (~9304), `record_route_observed` (~9296, ~9416), `construct_and_write` (~9429), plus the task-gate reply handler (~9123). In `server/app.py` (background/server): the same sequence (~2837 / 2845 / 2857 / 2852 / 2968 / 2980, task-gate reply ~3113). The two seams must stay in lockstep — a change to one that is not mirrored in the other silently splits foreground and background behavior. (`server.py` carries additional worker and dirty-state entrypoints, ~3264 and ~3737, that wire the same functions; when auditing coverage, grep the function names, not the offsets — the line numbers here are at 7a5e8f40 and drift with edits.)

**Config surfaces.** `ORA_TOOL_EVENTS=off` (disable recording, gate still fires); `ORA_RISK_TIER` and the `/risk <tier>` sticky-plus-override and `## RISK TIER` mode heading (tier control); `ORA_STEALTH_CONTEXT` (stealth suppression at write, with gate decisions exempted so a blocked action is still logged existence-only, `tool_events.py:524-541`); `config/mcp-servers.json` axes and `config/capabilities.json` slots (the manifest source). The manifest fail-closed default and the PROVISIONAL tuning constants are the surfaces a tuner will touch first.

**Normal-operation vs failure behavior, summarized.** On a normal turn: tier assigned, no hold, tools gated per-call, log written, after-clock folds, packet written trace-local. Only an acceptance-kind verifier item becomes an obligation: it blocks `criteria_met`, enters the existing revision route, and must be corrected, resolved or waived through the existing Paused authority, or escalated at the fixed stop; diagnostic, exploratory, and preference suggestions remain advisory. Required human handback uses the existing redacted, reference-only Paused path and fallback; only a path that accepts it counts as delivery. If both fail, Ora rewrites the existing `ExecutionPacket` with `human_handoff.status: pending`, `retryable: true`, and `human_reached: false`, then returns the response with a visible notice that no human was reached and the handoff remains retryable; it never claims success or adds a waiver store. On a gate block: the tool never runs, the refusal string returns, the block is queued and logged. On an irreversible turn: held before execution with a Paused card. On any recording failure: work proceeds, telemetry-incomplete flag set, health surface reflects it. On any classifier error: failsafe to `high-risk`. Nothing in this subsystem raises into the user's turn; everything degrades to safe-and-noted. These are contract guarantees, not measured reliability rates — no success-rate is instrumented.

### Evidence & Verification

The subsystem carries 206 dedicated unit tests: `test_tool_events.py` (52), `test_risk_gate.py` (93), `test_risk_gate_pipeline.py` (6), `test_execution_packet.py` (55), counts confirmed at 7a5e8f40. The portability retrofit added `test_portability.py`, now 65 tests, of which PR #181 contributed +17 Windows-simulation cases.

The landing gate is a full-suite parity diff, not a green-checkmark. Each phase ran the entire test suite and compared a sorted list of FAIL/ERROR names against a recorded environmental baseline of 22 failures and 5 errors (`baseline-failures.txt`, 27 lines), requiring *zero new* failures — the same 22F/5E set, name-for-name. PR #181's own commit message records the result at 7a5e8f40: 3738 tests, 22F/5E identical to baseline, zero new. This is the right discipline for landing instrumentation into a large tree: you prove you broke nothing, against a known-imperfect starting point, rather than pretending the tree was green.
