**How to confirm the gate is live.** `dispatcher.py:735` calls `tool_events.gate` and returns `decision.message` when the decision is not allowed — a blocked call visibly returns a gate string instead of a tool result. The fail-closed default is confirmable at `tool_events.py:101-106`. The single-fold contract is confirmable by reading `record_route_observed` (returns signals) against the terminal's separate `construct_and_write` call.

**Explicitly untested surfaces — stated plainly.** `render_for_review` and `_format_artifact_for_review` are TESTED-BUT-UNWIRED: the docstring says so, and it is true — no live verifier consumes a packet. They are proven only by a test that feeds their output into the evaluator prompt shape (`execution_packet.py:14-30,236-243`). The evidence-first review the architecture exists *for* does not run in production; only the packet is built and written trace-local. The §6 consistency note is dormant on every live turn because `output_type` is `unknown` — a doc must not imply misroute detection is active; it is exercised only in tests. The evidence lanes are never sufficient, because the runner that fills them is not merged (below). And every Windows and Linux behavior is *simulation*-tested via `test_portability.py` — `ntpath`, `PureWindowsPath`, `os.name` monkeypatching, subprocess env assertions — and has never been run on a native Windows or Linux host. Simulation coverage is real coverage of the branching logic; it is not evidence the code runs off-Mac.

**The Phase-5 boundary, stated exactly.** Phases 1–4 are landed on `~/ora` main (current tip 7a5e8f40, PR #181). Phase 5 — `evidence_runner.py`, the `.ora/evidence.yaml` catalog reader, live sandboxed check execution, the planning-stage Evidence Contract, the dirty-state modes — is **implemented in an unmerged worktree at the code-review gate; it is not committed, not merged to `~/ora` main, and not installed.** The HANDOFF's top block records it as "DESIGN APPROVED + IMPLEMENTED 2026-07-04," implemented in `~/ora-worktrees/phase5-impl` on branch `execution-review-phase5-impl` off `82553162`, with its own `test_evidence_runner.py` (58 tests); the HANDOFF's explicit standing instruction is "NO commit until [the code-review re-review] approves." Its Evidence-Contract wiring at the planning seam is described in the worktree's own material as flag-gated (`ORA_EVIDENCE_CONTRACT`, default OFF) in one revision and as running unconditionally in a later revision — an unresolved internal detail of unmerged code, not installed behavior either way. I verified that `orchestrator/evidence_runner.py` is absent from the pinned tree at 7a5e8f40 (`git ls-files` at that commit returns nothing). (Phase 5 has since merged post-pin as PR #185, one commit past 7a5e8f40, which adds `evidence_runner.py`; this document describes the system AT 7a5e8f40, where it is not present.) So on the *installed* system, Execution-Review coverage ends at Phase 4. The Phase-5 runner vocabulary is pre-seeded into the landed tree — `EVIDENCE_RUNNER_DEFAULTS` (`tool_events.py:983`), `validate_check_declaration` (`tool_events.py:994`), and `evidence.yaml` in `_PROTECTED_BASENAMES` (`tool_events.py:267`) — but I confirmed these have *zero non-test consumers* in the landed code. They are a landed vocabulary with no landed caller. Do not read `phase5-impl.diff` or the `phase5-impl/` worktree as installed behavior.

### Expert Reading Notes

Inspect these first, in order, because this is where a confident reader goes wrong.

**The implemented-versus-landed line.** Before believing any Phase-5 claim, grep the landed tree for consumers of `EVIDENCE_RUNNER_DEFAULTS` and `validate_check_declaration` — there are none outside tests — and confirm `orchestrator/evidence_runner.py` is absent from git history. The runner exists only in an unmerged, uncommitted worktree at the code-review gate. Internalize the catalog-versus-contract distinction: a green evidence run means nothing you knew to check broke, not that the right problem was solved. A reviewer who forgets this will over-trust the very packet the architecture built to be honest about its own limits.

**The two lockstep seams.** `boot.py` (~9123–9432) and `server.py` (~2837–3113, plus the ~3264 and ~3737 worker/dirty-state entrypoints) both wire the before-clock/after-clock/packet sequence. They must move together. A change to one seam that is not mirrored in the other silently diverges foreground (terminal) from background (server) turns — the exact race the chokepoint design was meant to eliminate, reintroduced at the wiring layer. Grep function names, not line offsets; the offsets moved between 82553162 and 7a5e8f40 and will move again.

**The MSI-custom-gear bypass.** MSI's custom gear paths (`msi_run_gear4`, `article_generator`, `backfill`, `gear3_orchestrator`) build their own prompts and do not flow through the `run_pipeline` terminals. Risk tiers and packets therefore do not reach MSI-authored turns. This is a known drift class documented in the project's own convention notes; do not document Execution-Review coverage as universal without checking whether a given surface actually crosses one of the wired seams.

**The reversible-flag gotcha — do not "fix" it.** The `reversible=(tier != 'irreversible')` flag is the post-hoc-routing gate, not a consequence measure. Post-hoc routing is permitted in reversible tiers; irreversible is held before the executor. So `high-risk == reversible` is *correct* — a high-risk turn is dangerous but routable-after, while only irreversible is held-before. A reader who "corrects" high-risk to non-reversible breaks the routing model.

**The enforcement_model hardcode.** `build_execution_packet` hardcodes `enforcement_model: "in_harness"` with a TODO (`execution_packet.py:352-360`). It is honest for a core in-harness gear turn. The moment an orchestrated, opaque-shell, or MSI path produces a packet, that label is wrong until the future-phase enumeration lands. This is where the next change most likely introduces a false claim.

**The PROVISIONAL surfaces.** The Stage-A pattern table, `DEFAULT_TIER='standard'`, `FAILSAFE_TIER='high-risk'`, the token TTLs, `_MIN_SUBSTANTIVE_OUTPUT_CHARS=24`, and `_MAX_SOURCE_CANDIDATES=64` are all flagged in-code as un-calibrated. Treat them as retunable defaults, not settled thresholds, and retune from data rather than trusting the current values.

**The namespace collision.** `server/review.py` is *not* this subsystem. It is an unrelated Phase-10 document-extraction human-review web app (localhost:5005, `data/review-queue/`) that imports none of `tool_events` / `risk_gate` / `execution_packet`. The name collides with "Execution Review"; the code has nothing to do with it. Do not wire the two together, and do not cite `server/review.py` as evidence of anything here.

Finally, the primary architecture map lags the code: `CLAUDE.md` has no dedicated Execution-Review section. The authoritative sources are the landed code and the `~/ora-worktrees` spec and HANDOFF. Where those disagree with `CLAUDE.md`, trust the code.

### Platform compatibility

The primary tested target is macOS on Apple Silicon (M4 Max, macOS 26.3). Every non-macOS cell below is either simulation-tested or untested; none is "works" off-Mac without the qualification stated.

- **Check-execution sandbox backend (`code_execute._sandbox_backend`, `code_execute.py:40-48`).** macOS: works — `sandbox-exec` is the only implemented backend and returns `enforcement_model: orchestrated`. Windows-native: needs-porting — `_sandbox_backend` returns `None` off-darwin, so `code_execute` fail-closes/gates. WSL: needs-porting (same off-darwin path). Linux: needs-porting (same). Phase 5's Rev-2 design proposes an `ORA_EVIDENCE_SANDBOX` wrapper plus native Linux `unshare -rn`, but that is in the unmerged worktree, not installed.
- **Direct `bash_execute` argument-vector execution (`bash_execute.py`, `PreparedCommand`).** macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested. The command is never passed to a shell: platform-appropriate narrow quoting is parsed into one argument vector, shell operators and expansion are refused, and the same reduced environment and executable identity used for classification are rechecked before `shell=False` spawn. Windows parsing is covered by simulation tests but has not been run on native Windows; WSL/Linux use direct process execution and do not require a POSIX shell for this path.
- **Advisory file locking on approvals and sticky JSON (`runtime_paths.locked_file`, `runtime_paths.py:100`).** macOS: works — POSIX `fcntl.flock` branch. Windows-native: untested — the `msvcrt.locking` branch exists and is guarded, but is simulation-tested only, not run native. WSL: untested. Linux: works — POSIX branch.
- **Protected-path and secret/sensitive regex matching (`tool_events._matchable` / `_cmp_key`; `runtime_paths.within_base` / `within_any_base` hardened in PR #181).** macOS: works. Windows-native: untested — forward-slash-plus-normcase folding and the boundary-anchored path helper are coded and Windows-simulation-tested (`ntpath`/`PureWindowsPath`), not run on native Windows. PR #181 fixed a real prefix-sibling boundary bug in the live file read/write gates (`dispatcher.validate_path` and `file_ops._validate_path` treated a mere-prefix sibling directory as "inside base") that was latent on POSIX and active on Windows, replacing raw `startswith(base)` with the shared boundary-anchored helper. WSL: works. Linux: works.
- **Trace-local sinks and packet paths (`record()` `os.open(O_WRONLY|O_APPEND|O_CREAT, 0o644)`, `tool_events.py:563`).** macOS: works. Windows-native: untested — `O_APPEND` is cross-platform, but the `0o644` mode argument is a no-op on Windows; not run native. WSL: works. Linux: works. All roots resolve via `runtime_paths` (no hardcoded `/Users/...` remains in the subsystem after PR #181's slash-command and server.py cleanups).
- **SCRATCH_DIR containment for sandboxed check execution (Phase 5, unmerged).** macOS: the per-run read-side re-allow is macOS-`sandbox-exec`-specific and is the only mechanism that works, and it is not landed. Windows-native: needs-porting. WSL: untested. Linux: untested. Off-Mac containment is entirely Phase-5 design-and-worktree, not installed.
- **MLX/Metal local inference** is Apple-only and sits upstream of this subsystem; Execution Review itself is pure Python and does not touch MLX, but the enclosing orchestrator is Apple-Silicon-tested and untested elsewhere.

The net statement: on the installed system at 7a5e8f40, Execution Review's enforcement (the `code_execute` sandbox) is macOS-only; its recording, gating, tiering, and packet logic are pure-Python with cross-platform *code* whose non-macOS branches are simulation-tested only. No non-macOS surface has been run native. Cross-reference the reference spec *Ora Execution Review Architecture* for the full build spec, and the platform substrate is shared with the roots-and-locking layer described in the runtime-paths portability discussion.

## 14. Operation-Matrix (Mission / Objectives / Milestones)

### Problem

A project needs a durable strategic hierarchy: a Mission that states why the work exists, Objectives that name the focal areas the work organizes around, and Milestones that are verifiable, cadence-specific checkpoints a third party can confirm. The project-management modal's "Mission & Goals" tab has to read and write that hierarchy. Three failure modes threaten it.

First, drift. If the strategic content lived in two places — a copy in the project's JSON record and the "real" one somewhere else — the two copies would diverge the moment either side is edited out of band, and the system would then have to decide which copy is authoritative. That reconciliation is a standing source of bugs: the same class as a cache that outlives the data it caches.

Second, clobbering. A project's Operation-Matrix is a hand-authored Obsidian markdown file. Beyond the three MOM sections it carries Obsidian Bases queries, a `## Problem Solving` section, a `## Spawned Activity Registry` table, and whatever else the user put there. A naive "read the fields, rewrite the file" edit destroys every one of those sibling sections. The write must touch only the bytes it owns.

Third, the vault-less environment. The canonical vault lives on the user's Mac. Cloud-ora — the headless Linux server that runs the news pipeline — has no vault at all. Any code path that reaches for the matrix on the server must degrade to "nothing here" rather than raise, or every server-side call site that touches a project inherits a crash.

This subsystem — `orchestrator/operation_matrix.py` and the `server.py` MOM endpoints — exists to give the modal a drift-free, non-clobbering, best-effort-degrading read/write layer over the MOM sections of a project's matrix file.

One boundary must be stated up front, because it is the most-confused fact about this subsystem. This is *not* the Project Plugin subsystem. The plugin/fork extensibility boundary is `project_registry.py` plus `ora-project.json` manifests — the mechanism documented in the [[Reference — Ora Project Plugin Convention]]. Operation-Matrix is strategy content only: Mission, Objectives, Milestones. The two subsystems key off the same pointer files (see `project_meta.py` below), which is exactly why they get conflated. Keep them apart.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The decision: the vault markdown file is the single source of truth for MOM, and the project JSON record stores no copy of it. There is no second copy, so there is nothing to reconcile.

The matrix lives at `<vault>/Matrix/Project Matrix <Name>.md`, with Mission, Objectives, and Milestones under `## Mission`, `## Objectives`, and `## Milestones` headings (`operation_matrix.py:1-26` docstring, `MOM_HEADINGS` at line 41). The modal reads live from the file on every open and writes straight back to it. The project's pointer record (`orchestrator/project_meta.py`) carries `name`, `status`, `last_accessed_at`, and a set of inert profile slots — and no mission, objectives, or milestones field anywhere. Verify this against the code, not the docstring: `_DEFAULT_SLOTS` (`project_meta.py:42-49`) contains only `default_model_profile`, `interaction_style`, `output_style`, `persona`, `model_locks`, `private`; and `_UPDATABLE_FIELDS` (`project_meta.py:200-203`) whitelists only `name`, `status`, those same profile slots, and `last_accessed_at`. No MOM field can be written to the pointer even by accident, because the patch function filters unknown keys against that whitelist (`project_meta.py:211`). The "no drift" property rests entirely on this whitelist; if a future change adds a `mission` key to it, the property is void.

The alternative rejected: cache the MOM in the project JSON for read speed. It is the obvious optimization — the pointer file is already loaded to resolve the display name, so folding the MOM in would save a filesystem hit. It reintroduces exactly the drift the design exists to prevent, and it violates the vault-canonical rule the repository is built on: the vault is the source of truth for markdown content, and `~/ora` copies are operational, never authoritative (repo `CLAUDE.md`, "Vault Canonical Rule"). A JSON cache of vault prose is a second authority for the same bytes. That is the bug, not the feature.

Writes are section-targeted splices, not rewrites. `_replace_section` (`operation_matrix.py:195-206`) finds the region between a target `## H` heading and the next `## ` heading (or end of file) via `_section_bounds` (lines 143-158), and replaces only that region. Every other byte — frontmatter, Bases queries, sibling sections — is carried through untouched by string slicing: `text[:head_start] + block + text[section_end:]`. A missing MOM section is *inserted*, not appended blindly: `_insert_index` (lines 177-192) places it to keep Mission → Objectives → Milestones order and puts the whole MOM block ahead of any non-MOM section. This is the "leave every other byte untouched" guarantee, and it is the direct answer to the clobbering failure mode.

The tradeoff accepted: no read cache. Every `mom` GET re-reads and re-parses the file from disk. When the `Project Matrix <Name>.md` naming convention does not match, resolution falls back to a scan of `Matrix/*.md`, reading the first 2 KB of each file and parsing its frontmatter for a `nexus` match (`resolve_matrix_path`, `operation_matrix.py:108-133`). At project-modal interaction rates — a human clicking a tab — this is free. At large project counts it is a linear scan on every unmatched lookup. There is no instrumentation on the scan, so there is no measured project-count ceiling, only the shape of the cost: fine at the rates it runs at today, unverified at scale.

Open problems, stated plainly:

- **`_safe_name` does not sanitize Windows-illegal characters.** It strips only `\` and `/` (`operation_matrix.py:59-62`). A project display name containing `:`, `*`, `?`, `"`, `<`, `>`, or `|` produces an invalid `Project Matrix <Name>.md` filename on NTFS. Latent porting bug; harmless on macOS/Linux/WSL, where only `/` is illegal.
- **The map lags the code.** Neither the repo `CLAUDE.md` nor the user's global instructions mention `operation_matrix.py`, the MOM endpoints, or the mom-assist feature. The only provenance is the `G1.33 sub-step 5` tags in the docstrings, which point at an install-overhaul working doc, not the canonical map. A completeness pass flags this subsystem as missing for that reason: it is real, tested, and wired, but undocumented in the richest map. That is a documentation-parity gap, not a code gap.

### Implementation

Five endpoints in `server.py` expose the engine; all of them are thin, and the engine holds the logic.

**Read path.** `GET /api/projects/<nexus>/mom` (`server.py:4540-4565`) resolves a display name — from the `?name=` query param, else by reading the project pointer via `project_meta.read_project_meta` — then calls `operation_matrix.read_mom(nexus, name)`. `read_mom` (`operation_matrix.py:274-297`) resolves the matrix path, reads the file, and extracts each section. It returns both a parsed `milestones` list and the raw `milestones_raw` markdown. The parse (`_parse_tasks`, lines 216-228) turns `- [ ]` / `- [x]` task lines into `{text, done, indent}` dicts; the raw string is returned alongside so the modal can offer checkbox editing when the section is clean task lines and fall back to raw-text editing when it is not. This is a deliberate dual return: structure where structure exists, raw where it does not.

**Resolution.** `resolve_matrix_path` (`operation_matrix.py:108-133`) tries the naming convention first (`Project Matrix <safe-name>.md`), and only if that misses does it scan `Matrix/*.md` for a file whose frontmatter `nexus` field contains the nexus. The nexus `general` is explicitly rejected before the scan (line 124) — the Commons is synthetic and has no matrix file. Frontmatter parsing uses PyYAML with a graceful `ImportError` fallback to `{}` (`operation_matrix.py:36-39`, `_split_frontmatter` lines 69-89), so a runtime missing PyYAML degrades to "no frontmatter matched" rather than crashing.

**Write path.** `POST /api/projects/<nexus>/mom` (`server.py:4568-4603`) accepts any of `mission`, `objectives`, `milestones` (structured list), `milestones_raw`, and `name`, and calls `write_mom` (`operation_matrix.py:353-401`). The write logic:

1. Guard: if nexus is empty or `general`, return `None` — the Commons is synthetic, no file to write (line 373). The endpoint turns that `None` into a 404.
2. Resolve the path; if missing and `create_if_missing` is true and a name is present, create the file from a template (`_create_matrix` / `_new_matrix_text`, lines 300-327) — frontmatter with `type: matrix` and today's dates, an H1 title, and the three empty MOM sections.
3. Splice each provided field via `_replace_section`. `None` leaves a section untouched. `milestones_raw` wins over structured `milestones` when both are supplied (lines 391-394).
4. Bump the `date modified` frontmatter line (`_bump_modified`, lines 330-344).
5. Write atomically and re-read, returning the fresh MOM.

**Atomic write.** `_atomic_write` (lines 347-350) writes to a `.tmp` sibling and calls `os.replace`. This prevents the torn-write failure mode: a crash mid-write leaves either the old file or the new file intact, never a half-written matrix. No advisory lock is taken here — unlike `oversight_actions.py`, which uses `fcntl`. The matrix is edited by one human through one modal at a time; `os.replace` gives atomicity of the swap, but two concurrent writers would still race on the read-modify-write. That race is untested and, at modal rates, unlikely; it is named here rather than claimed impossible.

**Best-effort degradation.** Every failure returns empty or `None`, never raises. Missing vault directory → `resolve_matrix_path` returns `None` → `read_mom` returns `_empty_mom()` (`exists: False`, empty fields, lines 263-271, path-None branch at 282-284). `OSError` on read → same empty result (lines 287-288). This is what lets cloud-ora, with no vault, call these endpoints without inheriting a crash.

**The mom-assist endpoint** — `POST /api/projects/<nexus>/mom-assist` (`server.py:4715-4787`) — is the one path that touches a model, and it is strictly read-only: it drafts suggestions into the modal's editable fields and never writes the matrix. It calls the cheap `sidebar` slot through the standard model seam (`_call_small_model_with_system`, lines 4660-4685) with a doctrine prompt (`MOM_ASSIST_SYSTEM`, lines 4616-4640) that encodes the going-concern rules from the [[Framework — Mission, Objectives, Milestones Clarification]]: Mission as a cycle-shaped Service Statement, every recurring milestone carrying a specific cadence, at least one maturity gate. The response is parsed into MISSION / OBJECTIVES / MILESTONES blocks by `_parse_mom_blocks` (lines 4688-4712), tolerant of code fences and case. The failure taxonomy is explicit and never 500: the Commons → 400 before any model call (lines 4729-4731), model unavailable → 503 (lines 4767-4770), unparseable draft after a one-shot retry → 502 (lines 4774-4777). The retry exists because small models occasionally wrap output in fences or skip a label; one retry, then give up cleanly.

**The files-index endpoint** — `GET /api/projects/<nexus>/files` (`server.py:4805-4846`) — returns a read-only index of the project's vault output folder (via `project_meta.list_project_files`), pins the Operation-Matrix file as a separate `matrix` entry, and attaches an `obsidian://open` URI to each entry via `_obsidian_uri_for` (lines 4790-4802). The default response remains the first 500 files; optional `offset` and `limit` parameters expose deterministic later pages, while `total`, `next_offset`, and completeness fields describe the one enumerated inventory. File management defers to Obsidian by design decision (Q2 LOCKED in the docstrings): Ora indexes and links, Obsidian edits. The sibling reveal action — `POST /api/fs/reveal` (lines 4914-4954) — is macOS-gated: it first sandboxes the path to the vault root or the Ora Exports/Resources folders (403 on escape, lines 4934-4941) and checks existence (404, line 4944), then returns 501 `reveal-in-Finder is macOS-only` on any non-Darwin platform (lines 4946-4948) before shelling out to `open -R` (line 4951).

**Config surface.** One knob: `ORA_VAULT_PATH` (or the alias `ORA_VAULT`) overrides the vault root, which otherwise defaults to `~/Documents/vault` (`vault_root`, `operation_matrix.py:44-52`). `vault_root()` is also imported by `export.py` and `nexus_rename.py`, so it is a shared primitive, not a private helper — a change to its resolution logic reaches beyond this subsystem.

**Canonical active-project read seam (current scoped foundation).** `operation_matrix.list_active_project_meta(pointer_dir=None, *, skipped_authority=None)` is the shared adapter used by Overview's Project priority source. It calls `project_meta.list_project_meta(pointer_dir)` exactly once, passes through optional skipped-authority reporting, then filters that returned sequence in place without sorting it again: the synthetic default/Commons row and every row whose status is not exactly `active` are omitted. Because the adapter does not re-rank survivors, valid numeric ranks keep `project_meta`'s ascending priority order, while unranked projects remain after ranked projects under the existing newest-`last_accessed_at`-first fallback.

Overview calls this helper directly and marks the card `partial` when the underlying inventory reports skipped authority. The helper introduces no task service, task or priority writer, task validator, project-directory map, route, UI, persistent state, or alternate priority authority.

### Evidence & Verification

Twenty-four tests pass across three suites, run at HEAD `7a5e8f40`:

- `test_operation_matrix.py` — 13 tests. Resolution by name and by frontmatter-nexus scan; `read_mom` on an existing file and on a missing one (returns empty); the splice-preserves-siblings invariant (`test_write_mission_preserves_rest`); milestone round-trip both structured and raw, with `milestones_raw` winning; the Commons-is-synthetic no-op guard; matrix creation from template; missing-section insertion. Tests inject a `vault=` temp directory via `tempfile.TemporaryDirectory`, so no real vault is touched.
- `test_mom_assist_endpoint.py` — 7 tests. The degradation taxonomy: unparseable → 502, model-unavailable → 503, the Commons → 400; and block parsing of MISSION/OBJECTIVES/MILESTONES. The model is mocked.
- `test_projects_mom_endpoints.py` — 4 tests. The mom GET/POST HTTP endpoints end to end.

The "no drift" property is verifiable without running anything: read `project_meta.py:42-49` and `:200-203` and confirm no MOM field appears in either `_DEFAULT_SLOTS` or `_UPDATABLE_FIELDS`. If a MOM field is ever present in either, the design's central invariant is broken.

Explicitly untested surfaces:

- **The `_bump_modified` insert-before-fence branch.** `test_bump_modified` (`test_operation_matrix.py:162-166`) uses a fixture whose frontmatter already carries a `date modified:` line (line 27, `date modified: 2025-09-12`), so it exercises the `re.sub` *replace* branch (`operation_matrix.py:336-340`). The *insert* branch — when no such line exists, inserting before the closing `---` fence (lines 342-343) — is a live code path with no test hitting it.
- **The frontmatter-nexus fallback scan at scale.** `test_resolve_by_frontmatter_nexus` covers correctness against a small fixture; behavior and cost against many `Matrix/*.md` files are not exercised.
- **All non-macOS filesystem behavior.** Every test runs on macOS against a `tempfile` directory. `os.replace` atomicity, the `~/Documents/vault` default, and `_safe_name` sanitization are unverified off-Mac.
- **Real model draft quality.** The mom-assist tests mock the model. Whether a real `sidebar`-slot draft adheres to the strict three-block OUTPUT CONTRACT is not measured — only the parse and degradation paths are tested.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `operation_matrix.py` end to end first. It is 412 lines, self-contained, and its docstring (lines 1-26) states the three design commitments — matrix-canonical, section-targeted edit, best-effort/vault-sandboxed — that the rest of the chapter defends. Then read the endpoints at `server.py:4540-4846` plus the reveal sibling at `4914-4954`.

Before trusting the "no drift" property, open `project_meta.py` and confirm that `_DEFAULT_SLOTS` and `_UPDATABLE_FIELDS` carry no MOM field. This is the single most important architectural fact in the subsystem, and it is also the fact most likely to be silently broken by a future change: the natural instinct, when someone wants "faster MOM reads," is to add a cached field to the pointer record. That one edit voids the invariant. Watch that whitelist.

The second drift vector is the naming-convention-versus-scan split in `resolve_matrix_path`. The convention (`Project Matrix <Name>.md`) and the frontmatter-nexus scan are two resolution strategies for the same file; a project rename that updates the pointer name but not the matrix filename shifts resolution from the fast convention path to the slow scan path. It still resolves — the scan matches on frontmatter nexus, which does not change on rename — but the performance character changes with no error. When debugging "why is this project's MOM slow to load," check whether its filename still matches its display name.

Where changes most likely cause drift: `_replace_section` and `_section_bounds` are the byte-preservation core. Any edit to the heading-matching regex (`_H2_RE`, line 140) or the section-boundary logic changes what counts as "the section" and therefore what gets clobbered. The splice guarantee is a regex away from breaking. Add a preserves-siblings assertion to any test that touches those functions.

Finally, note the `_safe_name` gap (lines 59-62) if this is ever ported off macOS — it is the first thing that breaks on Windows.

### Platform compatibility

Every test in this subsystem ran on macOS against `tempfile` directories. The per-subsystem platform posture:

- **`vault_root()` default `~/Documents/vault`** (`operation_matrix.py:44-52`): macOS — works (exercised). Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. `os.path.expanduser` resolves `%USERPROFILE%` on Windows and `$HOME` on Linux, so the mechanism is portable in principle, but the `~/Documents/vault` default assumes a macOS-style Documents directory and has been exercised only on macOS. Overridable via `ORA_VAULT_PATH` / `ORA_VAULT`.
- **Atomic write via tmp file + `os.replace`** (`operation_matrix.py:347-350`): macOS — works (exercised). Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. `os.replace` is atomic within a single filesystem on all three, and no OS-specific primitive is used (no `fcntl`/`flock` here), so there is no known blocker — but it was not run off Mac.
- **`_safe_name` filename sanitization** (`operation_matrix.py:59-62`): macOS — works (exercised). WSL / Linux — works in principle (only `/` is illegal there, and it is stripped; untested). Windows-native — needs porting: strips only `\` and `/`, not the NTFS-illegal `: * ? " < > |`; a display name containing any of those yields an invalid matrix filename.
- **`obsidian://open` URI construction** (`server.py:4790-4802`): macOS — works (exercised). Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. The URI scheme is cross-platform (Obsidian registers it on all desktop OSes) and the code uses only `relative_to` + `urllib.quote` with no OS-specific branch, but it was verified only on macOS.
- **PyYAML frontmatter parse** (`operation_matrix.py:36-39`, `82-88`): macOS / Windows-native / WSL / Linux — no platform concern. Pure-Python with a graceful `ImportError` fallback; no OS-specific behavior.
- **Reveal-in-Finder sibling endpoint** (`server.py:4914-4954`): macOS — mac-only by design (shells out to `open -R`). Windows-native / WSL / Linux — n/a: explicitly gated, returns 501 `reveal-in-Finder is macOS-only` on any non-Darwin platform. This is not part of the core MOM engine, but it is the reveal action wired to the files-index entries this subsystem produces; a Windows or Linux port would need `explorer /select` or `xdg-open` respectively.

## 15. Documentation-Code Parity & Vault-Repo Mirror Governance

### Current implementation and enforcement boundary

Documentation parity is now a same-task, five-repository obligation. The machine-readable ownership and discovery map in [[Reference — Documentation-Code Parity Configuration]] resolves code paths in the vault, Ora, ora-ai-app, ora-ai-org, and MSI to stable documentation surfaces. A behavior, user or operator contract, configuration, route, output, or material failure-mode change updates the owning vault canonical in the same coordinated task. When an owner names a section, that section—not an unrelated edit elsewhere in the same file—must change. A true no-impact change instead carries exactly one `Documentation-No-Impact: <surface-id>` trailer in that repository's final task commit, and the independent reviewer validates that claim. Duplicate, surplus, cross-repository, and otherwise unused trailers are rejected. Every unfamiliar production path still receives its repository's conservative catch-all owner, so it owes either that canonical disposition or reviewed no impact rather than escaping as “unmapped.”

The authoritative deterministic gate is Ora's read-only `scripts/verify-implementation.py --check documentation-integrity --verbose`, supplied with explicit worktree roots and base commits for all five repositories. It checks declared ownership, typed references, task dispositions, registered propagation, lifecycle invariants, and the exact accepted-finding baseline. A vault base from before activation is refused rather than treated as a bootstrap escape. Verbose output prints each root, base, and final head for the coordinator's later identity comparison. The gate never falls back to live checkouts, never runs `--check all`, and does not claim that prose is semantically accurate. The independent reviewer owns that comparison.

Registered Ora documentation mirrors are rebuilt by stripping the vault YAML block and its one following blank line; the rest remains byte-identical. This propagation covers Technical Documentation, Accessible Overview, Using Ora, and the Thinking Tools singleton. Registered public pages use the existing cross-site publisher and reverse-parity assertion. Seven local hooks are installed: a blocking `pre-push` hook in each of the five repositories, plus the Ora and vault fail-open `post-commit` framework-pair queue hooks. The pre-push hook requires the complete explicit five-root task context for an owned-code push. The post-commit audit classifies only the byte-exact accepted E-093 and Video/D35 states as accepted external findings; any new, changed, no-longer-exact, verifier, or queue-write failure remains red without blocking the commit. These are bypassable local controls, not remote certification. Git cannot make five repositories atomic, so the coordinator still withholds every participating merge until the combined gate and semantic review pass. Recovery is Git revert in reverse landing order; the hooks do not roll back changes.

> **Testing is evidence for the changed behavior, not a second project. The task scope lock's named check list is the exact testing ceiling: run every named check and nothing additional. Do not run a full suite, full build, benchmark, broad audit, `--check all`, or an extra reassurance check. If a newly discovered material risk cannot be judged by that list, stop and revise the scope with the user before running another check. Stop when the named checks pass and material review is satisfied. The completion report must list every check actually run.**

### Semantic-review and landing evidence

Before any merge, the coordinator holds one complete evidence packet: all five cumulative repository diffs; the global-instruction and Programming-skill changes; the affected surface identifiers; exact canonical-section changes; every no-impact trailer with its rationale; registered propagation results; the verbose five-root gate output; and the authorized focused-test output. The independent reviewer compares actual behavior with each changed owning section or expressly accepts that surface's no-impact rationale. An accepting review gives exactly one explicit verdict per affected surface. Missing evidence, a missing verdict, or a rejected surface cannot be converted into acceptance by a green mechanical gate.

After the gate and semantic review pass, the coordinator confirms each clean branch head still equals the head printed by the gate. A later correction invalidates the evidence and requires the affected focused checks, five-root gate, and review again. Only those exact reviewed heads may be pushed with `--no-verify`, and only to avoid repeating the DCP pre-push check that just certified them. If another pre-push protection exists, the bypass is not valid. All remote branches and pull requests must be ready before the first merge.

### Vault auto-sync backup transport

The macOS vault auto-sync path is backup transport, not certification of a coordinated code task. It preserves the existing order—local commit, `pull --rebase --autostash`, remote push—and uses `git push --no-verify` deliberately so the installed five-root DCP pre-push hook cannot strand ordinary vault edits on one machine. A failed commit, rebase, or push stays loud in the sync log and is retried only when the next exact vault event arrives; the code does not report a failed backup as successful.

This is a narrow transport exception, not a general manual-push instruction. It neither certifies the vault state nor exempts a later code-changing task from section ownership, propagation, the five-root gate, or independent semantic review. The next coordinated task evaluates any canonical state its changed surfaces consume.

### Problem

Ora keeps authoritative prose in the vault while loading operational copies from `~/ora/`. The vault markdown carries YAML frontmatter plus body; many runtime copies carry the body only. That split is deliberate—the runtime does not need Obsidian frontmatter—but the downstream file remains physically editable. Without enforcement, a developer can change a runtime copy for fast iteration and lose the edit at the next vault→Ora regeneration, or change the vault canonical without regenerating the runtime copy and leave Ora executing stale instructions. The Vault Canonical Rule fixes which side wins; the parity mechanism makes that direction enforceable rather than aspirational.

There is a second, worse failure mode than lag: documentation that describes behavior the code never implemented. A doc claims a RAG provenance score, or a verifier that edits the consolidated output, or a runtime-configuration file that was retired months ago — and the reader trusts it. This is the missing-feature class, and it is more corrosive than staleness because a stale doc is merely old while a fictional doc is actively wrong about what the system does. For a technical documentation project specifically — the deepest source of truth an engineer consults — a fictional claim is a defect that propagates into every downstream decision.

This chapter's own deliverable is subject to exactly this hazard. The technical documentation is meant to live as a vault-canonical file paired with a `~/ora/docs/` mirror. The instant that pairing exists, both copies become writable replicas of each other, and without a governing mechanism they will diverge the first time either side is edited in isolation. So the parity apparatus is not incidental to this document — it is the thing that keeps this document honest over time.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The apparatus is two-tier, and the split is the central design decision. Some parity questions are mechanically checkable; most are not.

**Tier 1 — the Documentation-Code Parity framework specification.** The model-driven framework is specified but not installed or callable. Its five modes are DCP-Sweep, DCP-Audit, DCP-Reconcile, DCP-Specify, and DCP-Deprecate. A person may execute that specification for an explicit semantic audit or reconciliation campaign, but no schedule or hook invokes it automatically. A purely mechanical differ remains insufficient for semantic parity: a byte diff cannot determine whether documentation describes behavior the code never computes.

**Tier 2 — deterministic checks for mechanically decidable claims.** The focused `documentation-integrity` category owns the same-task, five-repository contract. The `drift` category owns exact body parity for 15 registered vault-to-Ora pairs, and `framework-pairs` owns the complete manifest-backed framework lifecycle. Narrow companion scripts still check capability-slot equality, router configuration equivalence, tracked model-refresh state, and portability hazards. These are exit-code contracts, not substitutes for semantic review. Where parity reduces to set equality, declared-reference existence, lifecycle state, or byte equality, code is cheaper and more reliable than a model; where meaning is at issue, deterministic tooling stops and the reviewer decides.

**Direction-of-truth is fixed: vault→ora/site.** The vault is canonical; the `~/ora/` and site copies are downstream mirrors. This removes merge ambiguity entirely. Drift always means the copy lags the canonical, never that the two disagree and a human must adjudicate which wins. A two-way-authoritative design was not chosen because it reintroduces exactly the merge-conflict resolution problem the fixed direction eliminates.

**Task enforcement is deterministic; semantic campaigns remain explicit.** The five-root finalization check follows the code-changing task itself. A full-state model-driven audit still requires an explicit campaign. There is no daily or weekly fallback.

**Open limit, stated honestly.** The ownership map and focused verifier make registered relationships mechanically checkable, but they do not measure semantic completeness. A clean gate means the declared surfaces, references, dispositions, lifecycle states, and derivatives are internally consistent. It does not prove that the documentation chose the right concepts or describes the behavior truthfully; independent review remains load-bearing.

### Implementation

**Implemented entrypoint and data flow.** The task coordinator identifies five explicit task worktrees and their pinned base commits, resolves every changed production path through the ownership registry, updates each owed named canonical section or records its exact no-impact trailer, and regenerates registered derivatives. After the approved focused checks and final branch commits, it runs `scripts/verify-implementation.py --check documentation-integrity --verbose` once as the authoritative finalization check. The command requires all ten root/base arguments; it does not infer related worktrees or fall back to live checkouts. The independent reviewer then receives the complete evidence packet and gives a semantic verdict per surface. Only after the coordinator confirms the current heads still match the printed heads does the bounded DCP-specific push and coordinated merge sequence begin. The gate is state-based and read-only. The separate DCP framework layers describe a model-driven campaign that is specified, not installed as a runtime framework.

**Installed enforcement.** `scripts/install-dcp-hooks.sh` manages seven hooks from tracked source: five blocking `pre-push` wrappers, one for each participating repository, and two fail-open `post-commit` audit wrappers in Ora and the vault. The installer requires five explicit repository roots, rejects duplicate repository identities, refuses to overwrite foreign hooks, and supports read-only inspection and managed removal. A pre-push wrapper exports its repository identity and then requires the task coordinator's complete roots and bases. The two post-commit wrappers run the narrower framework-pair audit and may update the existing queue; because they run after a commit exists, they cannot block that commit. Their signal is deliberately narrow: exact accepted E-093 and Video/D35 evidence is labelled accepted, while a changed accepted finding, a new finding, or a verifier/queue failure remains visibly unsuccessful.

**No scheduler.** Neither the deterministic task gate nor the five model-driven framework modes run on a clock. Historical Drift Reports, the Decision Log, the Escalation Queue, and the Run History under the resolved vault's `Administration/DCP/` directory are records from explicit campaigns and fail-open audits, not evidence of a live scheduled routine. Full-state semantic work remains an explicit campaign.

**The pairing convention** fixes the direction of truth at vault→Ora/site and registers each deterministic transformation rather than guessing from prose. The current `DRIFT_PAIRS` table contains 15 canonical/runtime pairs: architecture mirrors and selected operational framework copies. It includes `Registry — Signal Vocabulary Registry.md` under the correct Registry prefix, omits the retired runtime-configuration file, and has grown beyond the earlier architecture-only list. `ARCHITECTURE_PAIRS` remains only a compatibility alias for callers that imported the old name. The same verifier separately reads the complete framework-pair manifest and the ownership registry, so the 15 direct drift pairs are not presented as the whole documentation surface.

**The mechanical checks are deliberately distinct:**

*`documentation-integrity`* is the authoritative coordinated-task gate. It reads the five explicit worktrees at their five explicit bases, resolves changed code through the ownership/discovery registry, requires a canonical change or a complete no-impact disposition, checks typed references and registered derivatives, applies the accepted-finding ratchet, and confirms every required repository was read.

*`drift`* checks the 15 registered canonical/runtime bodies under the established normalization rule. *`framework-pairs`* checks the broader manifest-backed framework inventory, including paired, no-runtime-twin, and specified-not-built lifecycle states. These narrower categories remain useful diagnostics and post-commit audit inputs; neither replaces the five-root task gate.

The capability, configuration-equivalence, model-refresh, and portability scripts protect their own narrower contracts. They are supporting checks, not a second enforcement topology, and their presence or call sites do not determine whether documentation-impact review is active.

**Normal operation** is one passing verbose five-root result, one accepting per-surface semantic review, mechanical identity between current heads and the heads printed by the gate, the bounded push of those exact heads, and only then coordinated merges. Missing roots or bases, a pre-activation vault base, a missing or section-wrong disposition, a surplus no-impact trailer, a broken typed reference, stale propagation, an invalid lifecycle state, or an unaccepted baseline change fails the gate. An unreadable repository is a failed task contract rather than a clean partial result. The coordinator does not treat a green narrow linter as five-repository certification or a green mechanical result as semantic acceptance.

### Evidence & Verification

**Historical DCP records are not schedule evidence.** The vault's `Administration/DCP/` directory contains dated Drift Reports, a Decision Log, an Escalation Queue, and a Run History. Those artifacts show that explicit campaigns and audits ran. There is no DCP scheduler, recurring sweep, daemon, or clock fallback in the installed mechanism.

**The five-root contract is explicit in the verifier.** `documentation-integrity` is a dedicated category rather than an alias for `crossref` or an item smuggled into `--check all`. Its command-line parser requires `--<repository>-root` and `--<repository>-base` for vault, Ora, app, org, and MSI; supplying only part of that set is an error. The loader then verifies each base as a full commit, requires it to be an ancestor of the task head, and records every repository as read before evaluating the coordinated state.

**The direct drift registry is current.** `DRIFT_PAIRS` contains 15 entries. It names the Signal Vocabulary Registry under its real `Registry —` filename, contains no runtime-configuration pair, and covers both architecture mirrors and selected runtime framework copies. The legacy `ARCHITECTURE_PAIRS` name is a compatibility alias to this current table, not a second stale registry.

**The installed hook topology is visible in one source.** `scripts/install-dcp-hooks.sh` calls its hook manager five times for `pre-push`—Ora, vault, app, org, and MSI—and twice for `post-commit`—Ora and vault. Its `--check` mode compares each installed wrapper with the exact wrapper the current source would render. The pre-push source runs the focused documentation-integrity contract for an owned-code push; the post-commit source remains the narrower fail-open queue audit.

**Enforcement boundary — stated plainly.** The seven hooks are installed local controls and can be bypassed. There is no remote cross-repository enforcement and no atomic five-repository commit. The explicit finalization check and coordinator merge hold remain authoritative even with hooks installed. DCP-Sweep, Audit, Reconcile, Specify, and Deprecate remain model-driven specification modes rather than installed runtime capabilities.

**This document is itself a registered parity surface.** `Reference — Ora Technical Documentation.md` is the vault canonical and `docs/technical-documentation.md` is its Ora body-only mirror. The ownership registry declares the `ora_body_only` transformation, and the focused gate strips the canonical YAML plus exactly one separator blank line before requiring every remaining byte, including the trailing newline, to match.

### Expert Reading Notes

**Inspect first: the ownership block and `documentation-integrity`.** The ownership/discovery registry defines what changed code owes; the verifier shows how that declaration is enforced against five explicit roots and bases. Read the `DRIFT_PAIRS` table next to understand the 15 direct body-parity relationships, but do not mistake that table for the full ownership surface.

**Registered facts outrank prose discovery.** The verifier reads the machine-readable ownership block, pair manifest, typed references, and propagation declarations. It does not search ordinary prose for filenames or code symbols and then pretend that matching text proves a relationship. A new surface or transformation belongs in the owning registry, after which the deterministic gate can enforce it.

**Body-only installed-documentation mirrors use propagation, not the 15-pair table.** Technical Documentation, Accessible Overview, Using Ora, and the Thinking Tools singleton are declared as `ora_body_only` surfaces in the ownership registry. The focused gate applies their exact frontmatter-stripping rule. Thinking Tools remains outside the framework-pair manifest because it is a Reference singleton, not an active Framework row. Keeping these surfaces out of `DRIFT_PAIRS` is a separation of mechanisms, not a workaround for a broken table.

**A passing gate is not semantic proof.** The scripts can establish that a declared canonical changed, that a typed endpoint exists, and that a derivative matches its source. They cannot establish that prose describes the right behavior, default, limitation, or failure mode. Independent review remains the only semantic judgment in the same-task path.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift.** Adding a new top-level code area without an owner, changing a transformation without regenerating its derivatives, or treating a no-impact trailer as self-validating. The gate catches the first two only when the registry truthfully describes the surface; the reviewer owns the third. There is no scheduler to catch work later, so the same-task gate is the protection.

### Platform compatibility

The installed topology is verified on macOS. The focused verifier no longer embeds the author's `/Users/oracle/...` checkout paths: its Ora root comes from `ORA_HOME` or the script's repository, its ordinary vault root comes through `runtime_paths`, and the authoritative documentation-integrity path requires five explicit worktree roots and base commits. That removes the former user-specific-root defect. Native Windows, WSL, and Linux execution of the complete five-repository gate and hook topology remains unverified.

- **Five-root verifier contract.** macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested. Root arguments are ordinary resolved paths and Git operations use argument arrays rather than a shell command, but no off-Mac end-to-end result is claimed.
- **Hook installer and wrappers.** macOS: works. WSL / Linux: untested. Windows-native: needs-porting because the installed hooks and installer are POSIX `sh` scripts; Git for Windows may provide `sh`, but that path has not been verified.
- **Case-sensitive vault names.** macOS: works. Linux / WSL: untested and case-sensitive. Registered paths carry their exact `Reference —`, `Registry —`, `Framework —`, and `Specification —` spelling, so a mismatch fails rather than being silently normalized.
- **Supporting linters.** Their portability varies independently of DCP. A Homebrew interpreter example or a Mac-specific portability rule in a supporting script does not reintroduce hard-coded live roots into the five-root documentation-integrity contract.
- **Model-driven framework specification.** Platform-neutral as prose, but not installed or callable on any platform. No scheduler or platform-specific campaign runner is part of DCP.


---

# Part V — Operate & Extend

## 16. Update, Recovery, Troubleshooting & Diagnostics

### Problem

An install is the one operation that runs before the system it depends on exists, on a machine whose state you do not control. Four things go wrong in practice: the network drops mid-download, Python 3.11+ is missing or is the wrong interpreter, the model catalog comes back empty, or the OpenRouter key is bad. A naive installer that re-runs from the top on failure either redoes expensive work (catalog refresh, model registry sync) or wedges — and without a resume affordance the only recovery is a full reclone. That is the failure mode the recovery tooling exists to prevent: **irrecoverable partial install**.

There is a second, more honest problem: Ora has no packaged updater. There is no `.pkg`, no `.msi`, no Sparkle feed, no self-update code anywhere in the tree. "Update" is a `git pull` of the framework library followed by a re-run of the idempotent installer. I state this plainly because CLAUDE.md does not mention the update/recovery subsystem at all and its Commands section shows only `./start.sh` — the recovery surface is under-documented relative to its runtime weight, and pretending an updater exists would be the worse error.

At runtime, three independent things degrade silently. Models and endpoints fail intermittently — a 5xx, a transport error, a local model that will not load — and a router that keeps hammering a broken endpoint just converts one failure into many while burning retry budget. That is **retry-storm-against-a-dead-endpoint**. A pipeline step can produce structurally-wrong output — the cleanup model narrates instead of emitting a cleaned prompt, and the rest of the pipeline runs against the narration — which is **silent semantic degradation**: the user gets a confident wrong answer with no signal. And the observational daemon can stop beating, leaving event/deadline liveness unreported — **unnoticed-observer-failure**.

Finally, Ora is macOS-Apple-Silicon-first. Unguarded Homebrew-Python shebangs, `os.fork` detach paths, bash-subprocess index regeneration, and an MLX-Metal heartbeat all break on a Windows-native port — and they break *quietly* unless something enumerates them. That is **silent-Mac-assumption-breaks-the-port**.

Each of these gets a dedicated, named safety net. None of them is a dashboard.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

**Idempotent state-file installer over transactional rollback.** `scripts/install.py` runs an ordered pipeline of nine steps — `preflight`, `dependencies`, `converters`, `profile`, `catalog`, `registry_sync`, `autopopulate`, `smoke_test`, `external_api_walkthrough` (`install.py:1877-1885`) — and records each completed step name into `install-state.json`. On failure it halts, logs `INSTALL_HALTED at step '<name>'. Fix the issue above and re-run with --resume.` (`install.py:1893`), and exits 1. `--resume` skips any step already in `steps_completed`. `--dry-run` previews without writing. `--reset` clears state and exits.

The alternative I rejected is a transactional installer that rolls back every file write on failure. Ora's install writes into `~/ora` (catalog JSON, model registry, populated configs, downloaded Pandoc/Typst binaries), installs Python and npm packages, and can create the vault — a true transaction would need to journal and reverse third-party package-installer side effects, which is not worth the machinery for a single-user install. The one deletion it does perform is narrow and self-limited: a vault creation that fails part-way removes only the empty directories that same run made. The tradeoff is explicit and sharp: **`--reset` forgets progress but does not roll back files.** It `unlink`s `install-state.json` and `install.log` (`install.py:512-519`) and nothing else. Re-running after a reset relies on each step being individually idempotent, not on the filesystem being clean. An operator who expects `--reset` to undo a half-written registry will be surprised; the recovery doc's nuke-and-reclone is the real "start clean" path.

**Git-pull-and-re-run as the update path.** No auto-updater is the deliberate current state, not an oversight I am papering over. The upside is that update carries no signing/notarization/feed infrastructure and inherits the installer's idempotency for free. The open problem is that this is undocumented as an update procedure — it lives implicitly inside `docs/install-testing.md`'s "Rolling-main install" language. The honest fix is to either build a packaged updater or write down "git pull + re-run installer" as the supported, named procedure. Today it is neither.

**In-band chat-banner health over a separate dashboard.** All three runtime health surfaces prepend a markdown banner to the *next chat turn* rather than exposing a `/health` endpoint or a status page. The rationale: the user is already in the conversation; a health signal that requires them to look somewhere else is a health signal they will miss. The tradeoff is that a degraded state is only observed on the next turn — there is no push, no alert, no polling surface for an external monitor.

**Three parallel health surfaces over one health bus.** `endpoint_health` (circuit breaker), `pipeline_health` (per-thread in-pipeline warnings), and `oversight_health` (heartbeat staleness) are three separate modules that deliberately mirror each other's shape — each exposes a collector plus a `format_warnings_as_chat_note`. I chose parallel modules over a unified event bus because the three failure domains have nothing in common at the data layer: a circuit breaker is per-endpoint global state, pipeline warnings are per-request thread-local state, and oversight staleness is derived from files on disk written by a separate daemon. A unified bus would force a lowest-common-denominator schema on three genuinely different lifetimes. The tradeoff is duplicated formatting code and three things to keep in sync instead of one.

**Portability linter with a hard exit code over per-platform CI.** `scripts/check-portability.py` statically walks the tree, regex-matches Mac/POSIX-only constructs, downgrades platform-guarded/docstring/allowlisted hits, and exits 1 on any CRITICAL. It is pure stdlib so it runs anywhere as a gate. The rejected alternative is spinning up Windows and Linux CI runners to catch these at test time. The linter is cheaper and catches constructs before they ship, but it is a heuristic — it reasons about source text, not runtime behavior, so it both misses dynamically-constructed Mac assumptions and over-flags guarded ones, which is why it carries an allowlist (`ALLOWED_MAC_ONLY_FILES`) and a platform-guard downgrade. **Open problems, stated honestly:** the 12 CRITICAL findings are a real, unpaid Windows/Linux porting backlog; the `mlx_worker` heartbeat is inapplicable-but-unguarded on non-Apple hosts; and the two startup bash index scripts fail silently on a bash-less host. All four are unresolved at 7a5e8f40.

### Implementation

**Installer state machine.** `install.py` defines `STATE_PATH = REPO_ROOT / "install-state.json"`, `LOG_PATH = REPO_ROOT / "install.log"`, and `COMPLETION_MARKER = "INSTALL_COMPLETE: 0 warnings, 0 errors"` (`install.py:123-125`). `main()` parses `--profile/--dry-run/--reset/--resume` (`install.py:1855-1858`). On `--reset` it calls `reset_install`, which under dry-run logs `[dry-run] would clear install-state.json and install.log` and otherwise unlinks both files (`install.py:512-519`) — note again that no installed artifact is removed. Otherwise it seeds `started_at`, loads `steps_completed`, and walks the nine-step pipeline; a step already in `completed` under `--resume` logs `✓ Step '<name>' already completed; skipping`, and any step returning falsy triggers the `INSTALL_HALTED` message and `sys.exit(1)` (`install.py:1888-1894`). Success writes `COMPLETION_MARKER`. The recovery doc orchestrates around exactly these two artifacts: grep `install.log` for `INSTALL_COMPLETE`, inspect `install-state.json` for the last completed step.

**Manual fallback and server path.** When the installer itself is broken, `help/install-manual.md` reproduces the Solo desktop install by hand (clone → `pip install -r requirements.txt` + the pinned MCP runtime → vault skeleton → `converters.py` → `refresh-catalog.py` → `sync_model_registry.py` → `auto-populate-configuration.py budget` + preset bake → free smoke config → optional live smoke → launchd or `start.sh`) with Windows PowerShell variants (`py -3`, `$env:USERPROFILE`). The manual MCP steps apply `node mcp-runtime/patch-playwright.cjs` after `npm ci --ignore-scripts` and before installing the pinned Chromium; this is the same required patch used by the automated installers. The Linux server path is `scripts/install-server.sh` (apt-get, ollama, `.venv`, sourcing `~/.config/ora-server.env`); `uninstall/uninstall.py` (present on disk, `--dry-run`/`--include-models`/`--include-conversations`) provides the clean-room reset the test protocol calls for.

**Endpoint circuit breaker.** `orchestrator/endpoint_health.py` holds per-endpoint state in a module-global `_state` dict (`endpoint_health.py:43`) guarded by `_registry_lock` (`endpoint_health.py:33`). Tunables default to a 60-second failure window, a 3-failure threshold, and a 300-second cooldown (`endpoint_health.py:47-49`), mutable via `configure()`. `record_failure` appends a timestamp; once the rolling-window count hits the threshold it sets `cooldown_until = now + _cooldown_seconds`, and a failure while a probe is pending re-extends the cooldown — the half-open behavior (`endpoint_health.py:82-94`). `record_success` clears state. The router consults the breaker inside its chain walk at two sites: `router.py:387` (`if endpoint_health.is_in_cooldown(ep_id)`) and `router.py:1088` (`is_in_cooldown(resolved_ep_id)`), each with a try/except covering both bare and package-qualified imports (`router.py:383-388, 1084-1088`). The module docstring pins the defaults to "concurrency spec section 5" — a vault reference (`Reference — Ora Concurrency Architecture`) that can drift from the code constants if the spec is retuned. These constants are provisional and configurable; treat the numbers as the current default, not a measured optimum.

**Per-thread pipeline warnings.** `orchestrator/pipeline_health.py` stores warnings in `threading.local()` (`pipeline_health.py:32`) because each chat request runs on its own Flask worker thread and warnings must not bleed across concurrent turns. A pipeline step calls `record(category, message, **extra)` (`pipeline_health.py:35`); the chat handler calls `collect_and_clear()` (`pipeline_health.py:50`) at turn end and `format_warnings_as_chat_note()` (`pipeline_health.py:62`) to build the `> ⚠️ **Pipeline-execution warning**` banner. The concrete producer is `parse_step1_output` in boot.py (`boot.py:5753`), which records `phase_a_parse_failed` (`boot.py:5806, 5818`) when neither `### CLEANED PROMPT` heading is found in the cleanup model's output — the exact silent-semantic-degradation case the module was added to close (path S3, per its docstring). The drain is wired at `server.py:6143` importing `collect_and_clear`.

**Oversight heartbeat surface.** `orchestrator/oversight_health.py` reads watcher heartbeat state under `data/oversight/` and reports actual staleness, daemon liveness failure, and incomplete tool-event telemetry. Missing legacy heartbeat files remain ambiguous and do not manufacture a warning. The former `simulated_oversight` warning was removed with the automatic Process Coherence route; there is no live-mode flag to enable.

**Diagnostics.** `scripts/ora-test` POSTs a prompt to the running `/chat` server under a *named saved configuration* (`--config NAME`, resolved from `config/configurations/<name>.json`) without touching the active config, streams SSE stages, prints the pipeline-trace path, and joins `cost-summary.json` for an inline cost table. `--no-wait` detaches via `os.fork()` → `os.setsid()` → `os.dup2` of `/dev/null` onto fds 1/2 (`ora-test:109-127`) so the run outlives the CLI — POSIX-only by construction. `scripts/ora-cost-summary` computes per-model and grand-total token cost by joining `usage.jsonl` against `model-registry.json` pricing via `boot.compute_cost_summary` (`boot.py:13254`); `--latest` picks the newest turn dir, `--write` persists `cost-summary.json`. Both scripts hardcode `#!/usr/bin/env /opt/homebrew/bin/python3`. **There is no instrumented reliability budget here** — cost is measured (token counts joined against a pricing table), but there is no success-rate, per-step-failure, or uptime metric anywhere in this subsystem; where a budget would be useful, nothing measures it.

**Trace Walk operator and API surface (2026-07-16, post-pin).** The primary interface is already in the Findings pane: make the problematic turn current, hover its lower-right edge, and click **Trace** / **How this was made**. The modal shows trace-kind, terminal-status, effective-Gear and retention badges; the truthful manifest categories; a per-step structural projection; lineage links; and **Pin trace**, **Investigate**, and **Export HTML** controls. There is no `/trace` slash command.

Trace expiry has two routes: the explicit/manual sweep and the exact one-shot deadline registered at finalization. Both treat a missing, unreadable, non-object, malformed, symlinked, changed-on-open, or identity-inconsistent manifest as uncertain; an unfinalized, malformed, or timezone-unqualified `finalized_at` is equally non-authorizing. They retain the trace and report the reason. The manual sweep removes only an ordinary trace directory past the configured age cutoff whose manifest is readable, finalized, identity-matching, and unpinned. The deadline queue waits until `finalized_at + ORA_RETENTION_TRACES_DAYS`; its handler additionally requires the manifest's current `finalized_at` to equal the exact value bound into that deadline. Only a readable, finalized, expired, unpinned exact match is deleted. An open trace cannot be pinned until finalization.

The terminal `/trace-probe prepare|approve|execute` flow is separate from Trace Walk's browser/API reads. On `execute`, the terminal handler first asks the real `get_active_endpoint` resolver whether a current endpoint is available, before it calls the one-shot approval consumer. If none resolves, it returns `Trace probe error: no AI endpoints configured` before any model call and leaves the approval unused. Endpoint resolution itself is not approval: `execute_probe` must still authenticate the approval id/digest, conversation, granted/unconsumed/unexpired state, and unchanged source trace/step identities before consuming the match once. When readiness, that approved identity, and the recorded endpoint match all hold, the model callback makes exactly one `call_model` invocation; a replay is refused as already consumed.

The browser calls `GET /api/trace/list/<conversation_id>`, `GET /api/trace/manifest/<conversation_id>/<turn_ts>`, `GET /api/trace/step/<conversation_id>/<turn_ts>/<step_name>`, and `GET /api/trace/export/<conversation_id>/<turn_ts>`; pin/unpin uses `POST /api/trace/retention` with `{"trace_ref":"<conversation>/<turn>","pinned":true|false}`. These endpoints return safe projections or a redacted HTML artifact, never the raw turn files. The module CLI is intentionally narrow: from `~/ora`, `python3 -m orchestrator.pipeline_trace status|pin|unpin '<conversation>/<turn>'` inspects or changes retention state only.

For a controlled reproduction, first copy the exact server URL reported by the launcher (the active port may be 5000–5010), then run `./scripts/ora-test --server "$ORA_URL" --id <diagnostic-id> [--config NAME] [--mode MODE] '<prompt>'`. `--list-configs` and `--list-modes` enumerate valid names. The final two components of the printed trace directory form the trace reference. This direct CLI route is itself traced, including explicit direct-entry behavior and the exact terminal value after routing.

`scripts/diagnose-extraction-pipeline.py` verifies the knowledge-extraction path end to end (transcript build → input-type detect → `source_type` override → Pass A prompt → optional full model run producing `notes_extracted > 0`); tests 1–4 run modelless, tests 5–6 need `--full` and a live model. It inserts `orchestrator/`, `orchestrator/tools/`, and the repo root onto `sys.path` (`diagnose-extraction-pipeline.py:19-21`) and imports `runtime_pipeline`, `input_detect`, and `extraction_engine`. It is a manual script, not part of `unittest discover`.

`scripts/check-portability.py` scans the tree with a CRITICAL/HIGH/MEDIUM/LOW/INFO ruleset, downgrades platform-guarded, docstring, and allowlisted hits, and runs `check_missing_files` (`check-portability.py:275`), whose `missing-windows-launcher` rule (`check-portability.py:278-286`) would flag an absent `start.bat`/`stop.bat` next to `start.sh`/`stop.sh` as CRITICAL — but at 7a5e8f40 both `.bat` files ship, so it does not fire.

**Startup maintenance.** At server startup `server.py` shells out to two bash scripts when their outputs are stale: `generate-indexes.sh` (regenerates the modes and framework indexes from their directories) via `subprocess.run(["bash", _gen_idx], capture_output=True, timeout=30)` (`server.py:14884`) only after comparing directory contents against the index text, and `compile-rag-manifest.sh` (recompiles `rag-manifest-compiled.md`) when the canonical is newer than the compiled (`server.py:14893-14897`). `rag_engine.py:451` invokes the manifest compile on its own path as well. **Failure behavior is silent by design and dangerous off-Mac:** `capture_output=True` swallows stdout/stderr and no exception is surfaced, so on any host without bash in PATH — Windows-native without Git-Bash or WSL — both scripts no-op and the server runs with stale indexes and a stale RAG manifest with no diagnostic. This is the one place I would change the design: fail loudly or provide a Python fallback.

### Evidence & Verification

**Automated tests.** `orchestrator/tests/test_endpoint_health.py` covers the breaker (trip, cooldown, half-open probe, reset). `test_router_chain_walk.py` exercises `is_in_cooldown` skipping inside the router chain walk. `test_heartbeat.py` covers the watcher heartbeat read/write path `oversight_health` depends on. `test_portability.py` backs the linter's intent — Windows path tiering, shell-profile command availability, `fcntl`→`msvcrt` fallback, and no hardcoded user paths in checked-in config.

**Live checks I ran at 7a5e8f40.** `git rev-parse HEAD` confirms `7a5e8f40` ("Execution Review Phase 1-4: macOS+Windows portability retrofit (#181)"). `python3 scripts/check-portability.py` returns `CRITICAL: 11, HIGH: 32, MEDIUM: 69, LOW: 59, INFO: 8` — the 11 CRITICAL are the Windows/Linux porting backlog, led by `homebrew-python-hardcode` in `orchestrator/output_runtime.py:474,626,911` not a missing Windows launcher — `start.bat` and `stop.bat` both ship at this commit, so the `missing-windows-launcher` rule does not fire. I confirmed `install.py` exposes `--dry-run/--reset/--resume`, `STATE_PATH`, `COMPLETION_MARKER`, and the `INSTALL_HALTED …--resume` message; that `reset_install` unlinks state and log only (no artifact rollback); that `compute_cost_summary` is defined at `boot.py:13254`; that the breaker is consulted at `router.py:387,1088`; and that both index scripts are invoked via `subprocess.run(["bash", …], capture_output=True)` at `server.py:14884,14897`.

**The clean-room protocol is the installer's real end-to-end verification.** `docs/install-testing.md` defines a three-environment matrix — Mac Mini A, Mac Mini B, and a Parallels Windows 11 ARM VM — reset via Parallels snapshot or `uninstall/uninstall.py`, run in both no-key and keyed modes, against its pass checklist, with "shipped = all three pass with zero glitches under the Solo profile." This is a human protocol, not automated CI.

**Explicitly untested surfaces.** The `ora-test --no-wait` `os.fork` path has never been run on Windows-native — it would raise, because CPython on Windows has no `fork`. `diagnose-extraction-pipeline.py` tests 5–6 need a live model and are not in the automated suite. The server-install path has not been exercised on WSL or Windows. And the biggest untested claim of all: **a fresh `install-server.sh` run pulls `nomic-embed-text` (768-dim) while the live embedding stack migrated to `bge-m3` (1024-dim) on 2026-05-29.** I verified this drift directly — `config/chromadb.json` documents the nomic→bge-m3 / 768→1024 migration and the `_v2` collection suffix (retaining the v1 nomic collections for rollback), while `install-server.sh:174-175`, `install-manual.md:185`, and `install-recovery.md:240` still `ollama pull nomic-embed-text`. A fresh server install would build a nomic-dimensioned ChromaDB inconsistent with the documented runtime. This needs a decision: update the installer to bge-m3, or document that server installs intentionally stay on nomic and the dimension mismatch is handled.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `install.py`'s nine-step pipeline (`:1877-1894`) and `reset_install` (`:512-519`) first — the single most misunderstood fact in this subsystem is that `--reset` clears progress but rolls back no files, so recovery correctness rests on each step being idempotent, not on a clean filesystem. Then read `router.py:383-388` and `router.py:1084-1088` to see exactly when the breaker skips an endpoint in the chain walk. Then read `oversight_health.py:36-48` (`HEARTBEAT_INTERVALS`) and hold it against the oversight-daemon defaults — the module comment asserts they must match, and if a daemon interval env var changes without updating this table, staleness thresholds silently diverge.

Drift is most likely at five places. First, the **nomic-vs-bge-m3 installer lag** — the highest-value fix; the installer and three docs pull the pre-migration embedder while the runtime is on bge-m3. Second, the **stale historical pair** `docs/upgrade-report-2026-04-06.md` + `docs/pre-upgrade-packages.txt`: both reference `~/local-ai` (the pre-rename repo), commit `49d2f8a`, and a 3-local-model / `index.html`-era system. They are frozen history — keep them, but never cite their "15 dispatcher tools" count or `cd ~/local-ai && git checkout .` rollback as current. Third, the **two bash index scripts** silently no-op on any bash-less host. Fourth, the **portability allowlist** (`ALLOWED_MAC_ONLY_FILES`) will rot as new Mac-only code lands — the CRITICAL count is only meaningful if the allowlist stays honest. Fifth, the **`mlx_worker` heartbeat**: it is an unguarded entry in `HEARTBEAT_INTERVALS`, correct-by-accident on non-Apple hosts (no file → skipped, not stale) but not conditioned on local-MLX presence, so a future change to the "missing file" semantics could start reporting a phantom stale watcher off-Mac.

The subsystem's biggest honest gap remains the **absence of any packaged updater**. Do not read `git pull` as a documented update procedure — it is the de facto one, and writing it down (or replacing it) is open work.

For cross-references: the router's endpoint selection and machine-mutex logic are covered under the routing chapter; the oversight daemon and watcher lifecycle under the meta-layer chapter; the extraction pipeline that `diagnose-extraction-pipeline.py` inspects under the knowledge-extraction chapter. Note that Execution-Review Phase 5 — the specification-driven, judge-gated review the portability retrofit belongs to — is **implemented in an unmerged worktree, at the code-review gate; it is not merged to ~/ora main and not installed at 7a5e8f40.** Phases 1–4 are landed and are exactly what commit `7a5e8f40` ("Execution Review Phase 1-4: macOS+Windows portability retrofit") delivers; the installed system's Execution-Review coverage ends at Phase 4.

### Platform compatibility

Per-subsystem platform checks, labeled by evidence. "Works" appears only where the construct is portable or was actually exercised; everything else is named as needs-porting, untested, or n/a.

- **`ora-test` / `ora-cost-summary` shebang `#!/usr/bin/env /opt/homebrew/bin/python3`** — macOS: works. Windows-native: needs-porting (Windows ignores shebangs; there is no Homebrew path). WSL: needs-porting (Homebrew path absent; Linux Python lives elsewhere). Linux: needs-porting. The portability linter flags `homebrew-python-hardcode` as CRITICAL for exactly this reason.
- **`ora-test --no-wait` (`os.fork` + `os.setsid` + `os.dup2`, `ora-test:109-127`)** — macOS: works. Windows-native: needs-porting (CPython has no `fork`; the `--no-wait` path raises). WSL: untested (POSIX `fork` is present, but not exercised). Linux: untested (same).
- **Startup index regen via `subprocess.run(["bash", …])` (`generate-indexes.sh`, `compile-rag-manifest.sh`; `server.py:14884,14893`, `rag_engine.py:451`)** — macOS: works. Windows-native: needs-porting (no bash without Git-Bash/WSL; `capture_output=True` swallows the failure, so indexes and RAG manifest silently go stale with no diagnostic). WSL: untested (bash present; not exercised). Linux: untested (same).
- **`compile-rag-manifest.sh` stat portability (`stat -f %m || stat -c %Y`, BSD-then-GNU fallback)** — macOS: works. Windows-native: needs-porting (still bash-dependent). WSL: untested (GNU `stat` branch present; not exercised). Linux: untested (same).
- **`oversight_health` `mlx_worker` heartbeat (30s, `mlx_mutex.start_heartbeat`, `server.py:14822`)** — macOS: works (Apple-Silicon MLX/Metal). Windows-native: n/a. WSL: n/a. Linux: n/a. On non-Apple hosts no MLX worker exists, so no heartbeat file is written; `check_health` skips absent files (`oversight_health.py:114-115`), so this correctly does *not* report stale — but the entry is unguarded in `HEARTBEAT_INTERVALS`, correct by behavior rather than by explicit platform check.
- **Desktop recovery/manual assumptions (`~/ora`, `chown -R $USER:staff`, `brew install python@3.12`, `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3.12`)** — macOS: works. Windows-native: needs-porting (`install-manual.md` provides `py -3` / `$env:USERPROFILE` PowerShell variants — the only Windows-native affordance in the whole subsystem). WSL: untested (`install-recovery.md` gives a Linux `chown` variant, but Step-1 fixes are Homebrew-centric). Linux: untested (same).
- **Server-install recovery/manual path (`apt-get`, `ollama`, `.venv`, `source ~/.config/ora-server.env`)** — macOS: n/a. Windows-native: needs-porting (Debian/apt + POSIX venv sourcing). WSL: untested (Ubuntu-shaped; not exercised). Linux: untested (Debian-target; not exercised at 7a5e8f40).
