- **Embedding model pulled by installer (`ollama pull nomic-embed-text`, `install-server.sh:174-175`)** — macOS: works-but-drifted. Windows-native: untested. WSL: untested. Linux: works-but-drifted. This is a drift, not a platform gap: the runtime migrated to `bge-m3` (1024-dim) per `config/chromadb.json`, so any fresh install on any platform builds a nomic-dimensioned (768) ChromaDB inconsistent with the documented runtime.
- **`check-portability.py`** — macOS: works. Windows-native: untested (pure stdlib `pathlib`/`os`/`re`, no shell-out; designed to run everywhere but not exercised off-Mac). WSL: untested. Linux: untested. It is the porting gate. Current run on Mac: 12 CRITICAL.
- **`diagnose-extraction-pipeline.py`** — macOS: works. Windows-native: untested (uses portable `expanduser`; only the docstring's suggested run command hardcodes Homebrew python). WSL: untested. Linux: untested. Tests 5–6 need a live model config.
- **`install.log` committed sample** — macOS: works (reference capture from a Mac run; 16 lines carry `/Users/oracle`, `/opt/homebrew`, `launchctl`, `osascript`). Windows-native: n/a. WSL: n/a. Linux: n/a. It is a sample artifact, not executed off-Mac.
- **Windows-native launcher is present but untested.** `start.bat` and `stop.bat` ship. The start path now honors relocation, resolves the checkout interpreter, validates an explicit port, enables oversight by default with `--no-oversight` as the diagnostic opt-out, forwards other arguments safely, and records an identity-bound PID for stop/health handling. It still sets only the Execution Review flag itself, not the POSIX launcher's additional feature defaults, so reduced or differently configured Windows behavior remains possible. It has not completed a clean-room native-Windows run, and several surrounding recovery/diagnostic paths remain POSIX- or Mac-oriented.

## 17. The Project Plugin Convention

- **Current Project preflight boundary.** Project registration and removal do not request approval until their exact pointer target is usable; protected tool and slash invocation checks readable regular program/script files and execute permission for the launched program before approval and again before spawn, without requiring an execute bit on an interpreter-read script. The resolved, authenticated absolute program path is the subprocess argv's first element.
- HTTP and slash callers share `prepare_pointer_mutation` with the registry sink, which repeats the strict preflight under the pointer lock. Symlinked, malformed, non-regular, or changing pointers fail before review; missing and container-only pointers fail before unregister review, while registration may create an absent pointer or extend a valid container-only record.
- Registration carries the canonical project root, manifest path, and byte digest from one stable manifest snapshot through approval. The sink re-reads that canonical root and refuses manifest drift before atomically replacing the shared pointer.

### Problem

Ora is a generic prototype. It is meant to be forked. But the moment it did real work — running Main Street Independent, an AI news publication — it grew code that has nothing to do with a generic orchestrator: a FRED economic-data fetch, an article schema, a `msi_news_articles` ChromaDB collection, a 19th-century-press publication theme, editorial framework tuning. That code has to live somewhere, and the tempting place is `~/ora/orchestrator/`, right next to the pipeline it calls.

That is the failure mode this convention prevents: core-fork contamination. If MSI's FRED fetch lands in `orchestrator/`, then every fork of Ora inherits one publication's specifics, the question "what is core Ora versus what is a downstream project?" stops having an answer, and you can no longer cleanly separate the generic engine from the thing built on top of it. A second, related failure mode is blast radius: if a project's code runs in Ora's own Python process, a project bug — an unhandled exception, a runaway loop, a bad import — takes down the orchestrator with it. A generic prototype that crashes because one downstream publication shipped a bad tool is not a prototype anyone can fork with confidence.

The convention answers both by drawing a hard line: project-specific code lives in the project's own directory, and Ora reaches it only across a process boundary. The mechanism is a directory boundary, a declarative manifest, and subprocess isolation.

This convention is not the first-party **feature-plugin** boundary in Chapter 11.
A Project Plugin belongs to an external named project, is registered through an
`ora-project.json` manifest and pointer file, and executes tools across a JSON
subprocess boundary for failure and dependency isolation. The current video
feature is trusted code shipped under Ora's own `plugins/video` directory and
runs in process so it can share conversation lifecycle safety. Neither mechanism
discovers or installs the other.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The decision is a three-part contract:

1. A hard directory boundary. A project is any directory on disk containing an `ora-project.json` manifest. Project code lives there — `<project>/ora-project/tools/`, `scripts/`, `config/`, `themes/` — never in `~/ora/orchestrator/`. The module header of `project_registry.py` states the rule in the first person of the codebase: "This module is project-agnostic — nothing here references any specific project."

2. A declarative manifest. The manifest declares what the project exposes: `tools`, `slash_commands`, `frameworks`, `peds`, `workflow_specs`, `chromadb_collections`, plus three advanced surfaces — `capability_slots` (see The Capability Registry), `themes` (see The V3 Theme System), and `framework_configurations` (see The Framework Configuration Engine). Frameworks reference tools by abstract name (`news-index`, `news-related-find`), never by file path; the dispatcher resolves the name against the registered manifest at runtime.

3. Subprocess isolation over a JSON-on-stdout contract. Ora invokes a project tool by running it as a subprocess and parsing its stdout as JSON — it does not import the project's Python modules. This is the load-bearing choice, and it is a Unix pipe, not an in-process plugin.

The main alternative rejected was in-process plugin imports (Python `entry_points`, or `importlib` loading the project's modules into Ora's interpreter). That is the conventional Python plugin pattern, and it is faster — a function call instead of a process spawn. It was rejected for two reasons. First, it couples core Ora to every project's dependency tree: if MSI imports a library at version X and Ora needs version Y, they fight inside one interpreter. Second, it destroys failure isolation — an exception in project code propagates straight into the orchestrator's stack. The subprocess boundary makes a project tool's failure a `ToolInvocationError` the caller can catch, never a core crash.

A second rejected alternative was auto-scanning the disk for projects. Ora could walk the filesystem for `ora-project.json` files and register whatever it finds. That was rejected for explicit pointer files: a project becomes visible to Ora only when someone writes `~/ora/data/projects/<nexus>.json` pointing at its root — by hand, programmatically, or via `/project-register`. Registration is a deliberate act, not a side effect of where a directory happens to sit. The cost is one extra step to install a project; the benefit is that Ora's set of active projects is an explicit, inspectable list rather than an emergent property of a disk walk.

The accepted tradeoff: subprocess-per-call has real cost. Every invocation pays process-spawn latency and JSON serialization at the boundary, and the tool cannot share live Python state with the orchestrator — each call starts cold. For a news pipeline that fires tools in bulk, that overhead is not free. It is accepted because the isolation it buys — language-agnosticism (a project tool could be a shell script or a Rust binary as long as it speaks JSON on stdout), dependency independence, and crash containment — is exactly what a forkable prototype needs. This is the microservices tradeoff in miniature: you pay serialization and process cost at every boundary crossing to buy independent failure domains.

Open problems, stated plainly:

- Framework configurations are not wired. The `framework_configurations` surface has a complete engine (`framework_config.py`, 35 tests) and its manifest block parses and validates, but the engine is imported nowhere in the framework-execution path. `compose_framework_spec` never runs at framework-invocation time. This is the largest gap in the convention and is detailed below.
- A hand-mirrored drift trap. To validate `capability_slots` at registration without importing `capability_registry` (which would create a circular import at load time), `project_registry.py` hand-copies the registry's accepted input-type and execution-pattern sets into local frozensets, with a comment: "Update when the registry's accepted sets change." Nothing enforces that update. If someone adds an input type to `capability_registry` without editing `project_registry`, valid project slots get silently rejected at registration.
- Active-project binding is provisional. The active-project pointer does no existence check when set, and new conversations still rely on a global pointer rather than an explicit per-request project. Both are documented deferrals.

### Implementation

The load-bearing module is `orchestrator/project_registry.py` (`/Users/oracle/ora/orchestrator/project_registry.py`). It is project-agnostic by design and holds the entire contract: dataclasses, manifest parsing, pointer-file discovery, and the subprocess invocation API.

Manifest parsing and validation. `load_project_at(root)` (line 750) reads `<root>/ora-project.json` and hands it to `_parse_project_from_manifest` (line 700), which validates every block and returns a `Project` dataclass (line 230). The `nexus` must be lowercase kebab-case (`_NEXUS_RE`, line 39); `name` is required. Each block has its own parser:

- `_parse_tools` / `_parse_slash_commands` validate that each entry has a name and a non-empty `command` argv, and that its `interface` is one of the two known contracts (`argv-stdout-json` or `stdin-stdout-json`). Duplicate names raise. A slash-command name carrying a leading slash raises — the manifest stores it without.
- `_parse_str_list` handles the simple string-list blocks: `frameworks`, `peds`, `workflow_specs`, `chromadb_collections`.
- The three advanced parsers — `_parse_capability_slots`, `_parse_themes`, `_parse_one_framework_configuration` — follow a deliberate graceful-degradation rule: a wrong top-level type raises `ManifestError`, but an individual malformed entry is skipped with a printed diagnostic rather than failing the whole manifest. One bad capability slot costs you that slot, not the project.

Two validations happen at registration time specifically to fail loudly and early rather than silently at invocation: `_parse_one_theme` checks that the theme directory exists and contains both `theme.css` and `manifest.json` (line 518), and `_parse_one_framework_configuration` checks that every overlay file exists on disk (line 634).

Pointer-file discovery. `list_projects` (line 772) globs `~/ora/data/projects/*.json`, sorted for determinism, and for each pointer loads the manifest at its `root`. It skips malformed pointers, skips manifests that fail to load, and enforces one integrity check: if the pointer names a `nexus` that disagrees with the manifest's `nexus`, it skips with a diagnostic (line 803). `get_project(nexus)` (line 813) is the single-project lookup; `register_project(root)` (line 833) validates the manifest before writing the pointer `{nexus, root}`, so registration never leaves a pointer to an invalid project; `unregister_project` (line 852) deletes the pointer and leaves the project's files on disk untouched.

The pointer file has two readers — this is the key subtlety. The same `~/ora/data/projects/<nexus>.json` file carries two meanings:

- The plugin view (`project_registry`) requires a `root` key. A pointer with no `root` is a container-only record, and `list_projects` skips it silently (line 792) — not an error, simply not a plugin.
- The container view (`project_meta.py`, `/Users/oracle/ora/orchestrator/project_meta.py`) reads every pointer for conversation-container metadata: `name`, `status`, `last_accessed_at`, and a set of inert default slots. Here `is_plugin` is just `bool(root)` (line 89). A project can be both a plugin and a conversation container, or a container only.

This dual meaning is deliberate ("unify the two meanings," per the module header) but it is a place a careless reader trips: the two views disagree on whether a `root`-less pointer is meaningful, and they are correct to disagree.

The subprocess invocation contract. `invoke_project_tool(nexus, tool_name, args=/stdin_json=, expected_binding=)` is the load-bearing runtime API. It resolves the project, looks up the tool, and resolves the command argv via `_resolve_command`: the second argv element is treated as a script path and resolved against the project root when relative. It resolves and authenticates the first element (the interpreter or program), then replaces that element with the exact authenticated absolute executable path used by `subprocess.run`; a form that cannot produce that exact executable is refused. The runner uses `shell=False`, an argv list, `capture_output=True`, `timeout=120`, and the bound canonical project root as `cwd`; that same root is exported as `ORA_PROJECT_ROOT`. The two interfaces differ in wiring: `argv-stdout-json` appends `args` to the argv; `stdin-stdout-json` serializes `stdin_json` and pipes it to stdin. Both must produce JSON on stdout (empty stdout returns `None`). The full execution binding covers Project nexus, canonical registered root, registered name, manifest, command, interface, executable/script paths and byte identities, and the exact arguments or stdin digest. Direct chat adapters bind it through the existing Paused one-shot route. An active Trigger binds it at activation and passes the claimed snapshot as `expected_binding`; manual draft/paused Trigger runs use Paused one-shot approval. In every path, the pointer, root, manifest, command, executable/script, interface, and input are re-resolved against that expected binding immediately before the subprocess boundary, so drift starts no child.

Normal-operation and failure behavior. On success, stdout is parsed as JSON and returned. Every failure mode maps to a `ToolInvocationError` carrying exit code, stderr, and stdout: non-zero exit, `TimeoutExpired`, `FileNotFoundError` (executable not on PATH), and a `JSONDecodeError` on malformed stdout. This is the crash-containment guarantee made concrete — an opaque subprocess can fail any way it likes and the caller sees one typed exception. `invoke_project_slash_command` (line 1063) is the same contract with one difference: its output is free-form markdown for the chat UI. Once its protected one-shot approval is consumed, operational failures raise the same typed error so the adapter can write a truthful failed receipt rather than presenting failure as successful output.

The standard environment. `_build_subprocess_env` sets four variables every project tool can rely on: `ORA_HOME` (the Ora install root, derived from `Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent` — platform-neutral), `ORA_VAULT` (`~/Documents/vault`), `ORA_PROJECT_NEXUS`, and `ORA_PROJECT_ROOT`. It optionally honors `ORA_CONFIG_NAME`. It also plumbs Execution-Review tool-event context to children that import Ora — `ORA_TOOL_EVENTS_PATH`, `ORA_STEALTH_CONTEXT`, `ORA_CONVERSATION_ID`, and `ORA_RISK_TIER` — all wrapped in a bare `except Exception: pass` so tool-event plumbing can never break a tool invocation. The env-plumbing code comments reference Execution-Review Phases 1 and 2 specifically; at this commit the installed Execution-Review work runs through Phase 4 (HEAD is "Execution Review Phase 1-4"), so the surrounding subsystem is further along than the local comments.

The Execution-Review boundary event. Because the tool body is an opaque subprocess, `invoke_project_tool` records the invocation and exit mechanically at the parent, regardless of what the child does — the boundary recorder (line 992 onward) emits a `project_tool` event with `enforcement_model: "boundary_only"` (line 1011), wrapped so a recorder failure can't break the tool. This is the Execution-Review posture for opaque children: the parent watches the boundary because it cannot watch inside.

The model-dispatch seam. A project tool that needs a model does not hardcode one. It calls `model_dispatch.invoke_chat(system_prompt, user_prompt, slot=...)` (`/Users/oracle/ora/orchestrator/model_dispatch.py`, line 42), which resolves the requested slot against the publisher's routing config and calls back into `boot.call_model`. The seven slots are `breadth` (default, high-context generative work like article authoring), `depth`, `sidebar`, `consolidator`, `evaluator`, `step1_cleanup`, and `classification`. The project declares a slot preference; the publisher's `routing-config.json` decides which concrete model fills it. This keeps model choice a publisher decision, out of project code.

The chat surface. `slash_commands.py` exposes four project-agnostic commands: `/project-tool` (invoke a tool; it selects stdin versus argv from the registered `interface` and parses stdin JSON when required), `/project-list`, `/project-register`, and `/project-unregister`. `/project-tool` and `/projects tool` use the exact `project_tool_execute` adapter; `find_project_for_slash_command` routes a project-declared slash command through the exact `project_slash_execute` adapter. Those direct calls remain one-shot. The same module exposes `/maintenance rollback <event-id>`, which calls the existing EventLedger rollback helper: it starts only while every current after-identity's existence, bytes, and permission mode match, restores independently authenticated before-bytes with the bound before-mode, and records `rolled_back` only after durable terminal evidence. The manifest binds each existing backup's `st_mode & 0o7777`; backup chmod drift refuses before restoration, and target chmod drift is a third state that refuses rollback. That authenticated path applies only when the manifest digest and mode-bearing snapshot were independently bound during preparation. Older mode-less material fails closed without inferred compatibility. Startup keeps the refusal as a distinct event diagnostic instead of reporting that event as restored, and continues recovery for later independent events. A partial restore remains `rollback_applying` and resumes only while each path is still one of the bound before/after byte-and-mode identities; it is distinct from `/trigger rollback`, which remains email-only before provider contact. On a cross-project command-name collision, the alphabetically-first nexus wins with a diagnostic, and the convention advises project authors to namespace their command names.

The three merge lifecycle points. The advanced surfaces integrate at three different moments in the runtime lifecycle, and the difference is the point:

- `capability_slots` — at capability-registry build. `capability_registry.merge_project_capability_slots` (defined line 674) is called during registry construction (line 858) when any project declares slots, merging project-defined visual-capability slots into the core `capabilities.json` in place. MSI declares two — `image_generates_cartoon` and `image_generates_barb_cartoon`. This surface is fully wired and exercised in production. Note the guardrail: a project's slot `routing` block may declare only structural directives (`inherits`, `prepend_fallback`, and the like); the keys `preferred` and `fallback` are publisher decisions that belong in `routing-config.json`, and declaring them in a manifest raises a `ManifestError` (line 422). The project may say "I inherit from X"; only the publisher may say "route to model Y."

- `themes` — merged on each theme-list request, served at request time. The V3 server layers project themes onto the core theme index through `_v3_aggregate_themes()` and serves assets from the project's own directory via `/themes/project/<nexus>/<path:filename>`, annotating entries `origin = "project:<nexus>"`. Registration and requests resolve the project root, declared theme directory, and asset and require containment. Before consuming bytes, the opener validates the kernel-reported path of the already-open file against the captured resolved project/theme paths, refusing outward-symlink redirection. Direct Flask serving, list previews, duplication, and export consume that file through the same opener without reopening its pathname. Legitimate nested assets and inward symlinks remain supported. The project directory remains the source of truth; there is no startup copy.

- `framework_configurations` — designed for framework-invocation time, but not wired. This is the honest gap. The design is that a project's configuration profile binds dynamically at framework-invocation time, because which profile applies depends on which project is invoking — the engine (`framework_config.py`) resolves `${config.<key>}` references and splices `<!-- ora-project-extension: <id> -->` overlays into the framework spec. The engine exists and is tested in isolation. But `compose_framework_spec` is imported nowhere in `milestone_executor.py`, where frameworks actually execute — a grep over the framework-execution path confirms no reference. The only Python runtime consumer is `server.py`, and it does not compose a spec: at line 4329 it reads `project.framework_configurations` in a list comprehension purely to list the declared profiles in the project-inspection API. So this surface's status is partial: manifest parse works, the substitution/overlay engine works and is tested, but there is no end-to-end path from a manifest `framework_configuration` to a composed framework prompt at invocation time. MSI declares one profile (framework `document-processing`, profile `msi-editorial-research`), which parses and lists but never composes.

Container binding and the rename cascade. Two more modules ride on the same pointer files. `active_project.py` (`/Users/oracle/ora/orchestrator/active_project.py`) tracks which project new conversations bind to via `~/ora/data/active-project.json`; `resolve_project_ids` maps a nexus to the `project_ids` stamped on a new conversation, with the synthetic Commons project (internal id `general`) mapping to the empty list `[]`. Its `set_active_project` does no existence validation — the module docstring states this is "intentionally NOT done here yet." `nexus_rename.py` cascades a slug change across four surfaces — vault `.md` frontmatter, conversation `project_ids`, the project pointer file, and the active-project pointer — and defaults to a dry run, with a path-traversal guard (`_NEXUS_RE`) on both the old and new slugs.

The reference implementation. `~/sites/mainstreetindependent/ora-project/` is the worked example of "what belongs in a project, not core Ora." Its manifest (`ora-project.json`, nexus `main-street-independent`, v0.1.0) declares 16 tools, 8 slash commands, one ChromaDB collection (`msi_news_articles`), two capability slots, one theme, and one framework configuration (list-only) — these counts are read from the MSI project at `~/sites/mainstreetindependent/`, which lives outside the pinned Ora commit and so cannot be verified from `~/ora` at 7a5e8f40. Its directory carries `tools/`, `scripts/`, `config/`, `themes/`, `framework-overlays/`, and — tellingly — both a `launchd/` (macOS) and a `systemd/` (Linux) scheduling directory, because MSI runs on a Mac in development and on a Linux cloud host in production.

### Evidence & Verification

How to confirm plugin discovery works. Run:

```
python3 -c 'from orchestrator import project_registry as p; print([x.nexus for x in p.list_projects()])'
```

At this commit the three registered pointers are `main-street-independent`, `ora-ai-app`, and `ora-ai-org`, all plugin pointers carrying absolute macOS roots under `/Users/oracle/sites/`.

Test coverage. The suites live in `orchestrator/tests/`:

- `test_project_registry.py` (1277 lines) — the largest suite, covering both invocation interfaces, the error paths (non-zero exit, timeout, missing executable, malformed JSON), manifest validation for every block, pointer/manifest nexus-mismatch, and collision handling.
- `test_project_meta.py` (158 lines) — the container view over the same pointer files.
- `test_active_project.py` (59 lines) — active-pointer read/write and `resolve_project_ids`.
- `test_nexus_rename.py` (234 lines) — the four-surface rename cascade, dry-run default.
- `test_capability_registry.py::TestMergeProjectCapabilitySlots` — the capability-slot merge, including project-shadows-core and project-shadows-project collision handling.
- `test_framework_config.py` (557 lines, 35 tests) — the framework-configuration engine, tested in isolation only.
- `test_project_execution_binding.py` (115 lines) and `test_conversation_projects.py` (132 lines) — conversation-to-project binding and `project_ids` membership stamping.

The live parity check. MSI is the production proof. It exercises the capability-slot surface (two cartoon capability slots) and the theme surface (one theme) in a running publication on both macOS and Linux. That is the strongest verification the convention has — not a test asserting the contract, but a downstream project depending on it in production.

Explicitly untested surfaces:

- Framework-configuration end-to-end is unverified. The engine is tested in isolation, but there is no test — and no code path — exercising a manifest `framework_configuration` through to a composed prompt at framework-invocation time, because the wiring does not exist. Do not read the presence of `test_framework_config.py` as evidence that the surface works end-to-end; it verifies the engine's substitution logic, nothing downstream of it.
- Windows-native is entirely untested. See Platform compatibility.
- The `ora-ai-app` and `ora-ai-org` manifests were not inspected at this pass. Their pointers are present and carry `root` keys (so the plugin view treats them as plugins), but the contents of their manifests were not read; only the pointers were confirmed well-formed and Mac-rooted.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `project_registry.py` first, end to end — the whole contract is in one file: the dataclasses (lines 119–271), the parsers, pointer discovery (lines 772–864), and `invoke_project_tool` (line 944 onward). Then read the MSI manifest as the worked example, and note that the manifest's tool commands all begin `["python3", "tools/<script>.py", ...]` — the `python3` interpreter is a POSIX assumption baked into every command.

Inspect these three drift sites, in priority order:

1. The framework-configuration wiring gap (highest priority). `CLAUDE.md` describes `framework_configurations` with "`${config.*}` substitution + extension-point overlay splicing at framework invocation time" as if it were live, and the `ProjectFrameworkConfiguration` docstring (project_registry.py:200–227) says the runtime consumer "lives wherever framework prompts are assembled (typically `milestone_executor.py`)." Both are aspirational. `framework_config.py` is imported only by `project_registry.py` and `server.py` (list-only), never by `milestone_executor.py`. A reader who trusts the docstring or `CLAUDE.md` will over-trust this surface. Trust the code: the surface is partial. This is the change most likely to close silently-wrong — if someone wires the splice into `milestone_executor`, the docstring becomes true and this note becomes stale.

2. The hand-mirrored frozensets (project_registry.py:60–64). `_KNOWN_INPUT_TYPES` and `_KNOWN_EXECUTION_PATTERNS` are hand-copied from `capability_registry` to dodge a circular import. This is a live drift trap: change the registry's accepted input types without updating these frozensets, and valid project capability slots get silently rejected at registration. Decide whether to replace this with a lazy import or a shared constants module the next time either side changes.

3. The dual-reader pointer files. Before touching pointer-file logic, hold both readers in mind: `project_registry` (plugin, requires `root`, skips `root`-less pointers at line 792) and `project_meta` (container, `root` optional, `is_plugin = bool(root)`). A change that assumes every pointer has a `root` will break the container view; a change that assumes every pointer is a container will confuse the plugin view.

Capability-slot merging and project-theme serving are wired. Theme aggregation runs on each request, not at startup; the request-time behavior and bound-file reading contract above supersede the older startup wording in `CLAUDE.md`.

### Platform compatibility

The convention assumes POSIX. MSI exercises macOS (development) and Linux (the cloud host) in production; Windows-native is entirely untested; WSL is portable-in-principle but not run on a dedicated matrix. Project-tool and project-slash execution now resolve the declared program to an authenticated absolute path and use that exact path as argv[0]; native Windows behavior remains untested. Per-subsystem:

- Pointer directory path (`~/ora/data/projects/`, via `os.path.expanduser`) — macOS: works. Linux: works (cloud-ora runs MSI here; the `systemd/` directory in the reference impl confirms POSIX paths are exercised in production). WSL: works (POSIX `~` expansion). Windows-native: untested — and the roots inside the live pointer files are absolute macOS paths (`/Users/oracle/sites/...`), so a Windows host would need its own pointer copies regardless.

- Project-tool subprocess (`subprocess.run`, argv list, `cwd=project.root`, `shell=False`) — macOS: works. Linux: works. WSL: works. Windows-native: untested. The invocation itself is portable in principle (`shell=False`, argv list), but see the interpreter row.

- Manifest tool interpreter (`python3`, the first argv element) — macOS: works. Linux: works. WSL: works. Windows-native: needs validation and likely manifest changes. Binding resolves `python3` through the current host's executable search, authenticates the resolved regular executable and its execute permission, and supplies that exact absolute path to the child. Every MSI tool command still declares `python3`, while a Windows host may expose only `py` or `python`.

- Atomic pointer writes (`os.replace` + `.tmp` sidecar, in `project_meta`, `active_project`, `nexus_rename`) — macOS: works. Linux: works. WSL: works. Windows-native: untested. `os.replace` is a cross-platform atomic rename, but Windows filesystem rename-over-existing semantics were not exercised.

- `ORA_HOME` derivation (`Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent`) — macOS: works. Linux: works. WSL: works. Windows-native: works (pathlib is platform-neutral). This is the one subsystem with no platform caveat.

- Vault Projects folder index (`project_meta.list_project_files`, reads `~/Documents/vault`) — macOS: works (requires Full-Disk-Access to `~/Documents`). Linux: works, degrades cleanly — returns `exists: False` when the vault is absent, and cloud-ora has no vault. WSL: works. Windows-native: untested. Never raises regardless of platform.

- Reference-impl scheduling (MSI ships both `launchd/` and `systemd/`) — macOS: `launchd` works (Apple-only). Linux: `systemd` works. WSL: untested. Windows-native: not applicable — no Windows scheduler equivalent is shipped. This is project-specific, outside the core convention.

- MSI `osascript`-based notify tool — macOS: works. Linux: mac-only (no-ops or degrades off-Mac). WSL, Windows-native: not applicable. Apple-only; project-specific, outside the core convention, and named here only because it is the clearest example of a platform assumption living correctly inside a project rather than in core Ora.

# Part VI — Standalone Programming

## 18. Explicit Repository Programming

### Problem

A user must be able to ask Ora to change a real repository without depending on Codex or Claude Code, while retaining a clear scope decision, actual execution, independent verification, safe recovery, and an intentional Git finish line.

The former governed-Process stack did not supply this simply. It intercepted Inquiry, persisted plans and Runs, projected lifecycle state, and accumulated process registries, process-bound automation and triggers, inspectors, receipts, and event records around a repository executor. Standalone Programming replaces that stack with the smallest complete loop. The separate Scheduled Trigger facility described in Chapter 12 is not part of Programming and was not removed by this replacement.

### Entry and planning

Programming is entered only through the Programming toolbar action. Ordinary `/chat` and `/chat/multipart` requests continue through Inquiry without programming classification or interception. Programming is not a framework-picker row.

`POST /api/programming/plan` accepts an objective, Git worktree name or path, question round, and prior answers. Exact paths and `~` paths remain valid. A simple name resolves only when exactly one Git root with that name exists directly under the user's home, `~/Documents`, or `~/sites`. `orchestrator/programming.py::inspect_repository()` then reads:

- current `HEAD`, branch, and porcelain status;
- applicable `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` instructions;
- tracked implementation shape;
- likely test files; and
- visible CI or live automation configuration.

The configured planner must then successfully read or search actual repository content; the coordinator snapshot and repository status alone do not unlock either questions or a plan. Only after that direct inspection does it decide whether a material choice remains. It may return one to three questions in each of at most two question rounds. If both rounds ask questions, a third planning pass must produce one concise plan using reasonable defaults.

The returned plan visibly contains outcome, component scope, non-goals, protected work, milestones, completion criteria, checks, authorized effects, and one Git finish line; the coordinator rejects a proposal missing any category or whose visible finish wording disagrees with the structured authority. It also carries `documentation_impact`: when repository instructions place Documentation-Code Parity in scope, this object declares `required: true` and a unique ordered list of stable affected surface identifiers. Runtime independently reads tracked top-level `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md`; their active DCP mandate determines whether the task uses DCP, and the planner declaration must agree. A DCP plan uses `local_commits` when approval stops with reviewed local branches, or `coordinated_dcp` when approval includes the five-repository coordinator landing the exact reviewed heads; ordinary single-repository push, pull-request, and merge values are invalid for DCP. This planning declaration routes later evidence; it is not a semantic verdict and cannot replace one.

A push, pull request, or merge also names an exact configured remote and its single push URL, while pull request and merge name an explicit base branch. The response carries those authorities plus the inspected root, commit, branch, dirty state, and documentation-impact declaration as runtime input, not a persistent plan ledger.

### Approval and repository authority

`POST /api/programming/run` requires `approved: true`, the objective, repository path, and exact proposed plan. It accepts an optional `documentation_review` object, but a documentation-impacting execution does not treat evidence supplied before mutation as final evidence. After milestone execution and commits, it returns a resumable evidence-required result carrying the current branch and head. The browser then accepts freshly generated packet JSON, passes the raw text through the existing privacy screen, and calls the same endpoint with that object plus `resume_branch`; no new endpoint or persistent evidence record exists. Before mutation, the coordinator verifies the repository root, `HEAD`, branch, and planned dirty state. A dirty path explicitly named in Component scope is task-owned; other pre-existing staged, unstaged, untracked, or symlink paths are protected from executor writes and excluded from review and accepted commits. Programming continues while those paths remain in place. A newly appearing unattributed path or ambiguous recovery path returns `ASK USER` with all work preserved.

The coordinator creates `ora/<objective-slug>-<baseline>` and owns all Git effects. The executor receives repository-scoped tools for read, search, command, write, edit, and delete. Paths are resolved under the worktree and `.git` is inaccessible. Planning commands are read-only. Executor commands cannot perform coordinator Git operations, external network commands, destructive utilities, publication, deployment, messaging, or credential work.

The executor receives the approved plan and current milestone, edits the real worktree, and runs only the exact checks the approved plan schedules for that slice. Work is divided into coherent reviewable milestones rather than mechanical steps.

### Fresh independent review

Every review is a new model call. Its context contains only:

- the approved plan;
- current milestone or `FINAL`;
- runtime Git baseline;
- raw task diff; and
- repository and task-required evidence tools.

It does not contain the executor transcript, hidden reasoning, a summary, or executor claims. The reviewer independently reads repository files and runs only the exact checks the approved plan schedules for review. Neither role adds a test, build, benchmark, audit, lint, or reassurance check; a material risk that cannot be judged within the ceiling requires changed authority before another check runs.

When the approved plan declares Documentation-Code Parity impact, milestone reviews deliberately receive no final documentation packet: later milestone commits would make it stale. Only cumulative `FINAL` review receives the complete cross-repository evidence object. It must contain all five cumulative repository diffs; a `repository_states` mapping keyed by vault, Ora, app, org, and MSI whose records each contain exactly `root`, `base`, `branch`, and `head`; unversioned global/Programming-skill instruction changes; the exact affected-surface list; a nonempty canonical-section change or one exact no-impact trailer plus rationale for every surface, with no overlap; registered propagation results; the complete verbose five-root gate result; and the authorized focused-test output. A passing gate must print exactly one `affected surfaces:` line. Its stable unique set is authoritative, and the plan, packet, canonical/no-impact coverage, and reviewer verdict sets must equal it; list order is retained only for stable presentation.

Programming parses the verbose gate's printed root, base, and head for vault, Ora, app, org, and MSI. Before final review it requires five distinct exact Git roots on explicit `codex/` or `ora/` task branches, verifies every submitted state against both the gate and live worktree, verifies that every base resolves and is an ancestor, and compares each nonempty submitted cumulative diff byte-for-byte with the raw live clean branch's base-to-head diff, including all trailing spaces and newlines. The exact `[no changes]` value is the only sentinel and is valid only when that raw diff is genuinely empty. The current Programming root, baseline, branch, and head must be the gated ones. It repeats that live comparison after review. Missing, malformed, stale, dirty, detached, or drifting evidence returns to the evidence-entry pause and cannot support acceptance.

For every affected surface, an accepting review includes exactly one machine-readable semantic verdict after its first-line outcome: `Documentation-Verdict: <surface-id>: ACCEPT` when the owning canonical section changed, or `Documentation-No-Impact-Verdict: <surface-id>: ACCEPT` when the exact no-impact declaration is honest. A false disposition uses `REJECT` with `FIX`. Repeated, missing, wrong-type, or rejected verdicts make acceptance invalid. The deterministic gate establishes mechanical correspondence; this fresh reviewer owns the prose-versus-behavior judgment.

Reviewer-only tools provide read-only outside and non-text evidence:

- `web_search` and `web_fetch` inspect the smallest sufficient authoritative outside source;
- `inspect_image` attaches image bytes directly to the review model; and
- `inspect_pdf` renders all pages by default, or an explicitly requested page range, in a temporary directory and attaches those page images.

Temporary PDF renders are deleted when inspection returns. A citation or executor description without source inspection is not evidence, and a description of an image or PDF without direct inspection cannot satisfy a non-text criterion.

The review parser accepts exactly four first-line outcomes:

| Outcome | Coordinator action |
|---|---|
| `CONTINUE` | Accept and commit this sound slice, then proceed to remaining milestones. |
| `FIX` | Return one consolidated substantive defect explanation for correction; at DCP `FINAL`, hand it to the five-repository coordinator without invoking the single-repository executor. |
| `DONE` | During ordinary final cumulative review, perform the approved finish line; for DCP, return the five reviewed local branches to the coordinator without single-repository landing. |
| `ASK USER` | Stop for changed scope or authority, an unapproved external effect, human-only access or decision, inseparable user work, three repeated no-progress cycles, or the soft spend boundary. |

The reviewer judges only the approved plan. It may reject wrong user-visible behavior, unmet criteria, content or data loss, runtime failure, unauthorized scope, broken atomicity, or lost user work. It may not add tracking, preferred abstractions, speculative safeguards, style work, or unrequested requirements.

### Commits, correction, and finish line

After `CONTINUE`, the coordinator applies the exact reviewed patch to a temporary index, creates the accepted-slice commit with `commit-tree`, advances the attached task branch atomically, and refreshes only the committed paths in the real index. Concurrent work remains unstaged and uncommitted. Rejected work remains uncommitted until corrected. At `FINAL`, only `DONE` establishes semantic acceptance.

For DCP, a final `FIX` or non-completing `CONTINUE` returns the full consolidated defect in a coordinated-correction handoff. Programming invokes neither the single-repository executor nor the commit path, so it cannot create a synthetic correction or no-op rollback point. The external five-repository coordinator corrects the participating task branches; the task then resumes from Git with a fresh current gate and packet. On DCP `DONE`, Programming rechecks all five live states and returns their roots, bases, branches, heads, and approved finish line. `coordinated_finish_required` is true only for an approved `coordinated_dcp` finish; `local_commits` stops locally and authorizes no push, pull request, or merge. Programming never calls the ordinary single-repository finish function from either result.

Three consecutive review cycles that reproduce the same substantive failure against an unchanged task diff return `ASK USER`. A changed defect list or task diff resets the count. There is no fixed attempt ceiling. The default soft boundary is 90 minutes.

Supported ordinary finish lines are `local_commits`, `push`, `pull_request`, and `merge`; DCP supports only `local_commits` and `coordinated_dcp`. Before a consequential non-DCP finish, runtime rechecks the approved remote's single push URL and refuses drift. It pushes only that remote; `gh` is explicitly bound to the repository derived from the approved URL and to the approved PR base. Before PR creation it queries that repository for the task branch, rejects cross-repository results, and reuses one matching open PR. An already merged PR completes a retry only when its head OID equals local `HEAD`; an open or new PR is merged with `--match-head-commit` bound to that same commit. Target mismatches, closed PRs, and ambiguous matches are refused. This makes create/merge retries idempotent without custom state. DCP never enters this one-repository path. Newly discovered deployment, publication, messaging, credential, or other external-write consequences return `ASK USER` rather than expanding authority.

### State, rollback, and user surface

Git is the execution state:

- the baseline commit proves the starting point;
- the task branch isolates the work;
- accepted-slice commits are rollback points;
- the current diff shows unaccepted correction work; and
- checks prove current behavior.

No Programming-owned Run, artifact, event, receipt, lifecycle, checkpoint, Trigger, automation, process library, or recovery database exists. Resume uses the approved plan, branch, commits, diff, and checks. This says what Programming does not create; it does not deny the separate user-authored Scheduled Trigger objects in Chapter 12.

`server/static/js/programming.js` supplies the minimal browser surface: repository name or path, planning status, material questions, proposed plan, approval/cancel controls, milestone progress, accepted commit identifiers, reviewer outcomes, genuine user decisions, and completion. At the DCP final boundary it renders a JSON evidence field and **Resume final review** control, rejects invalid or non-object JSON locally, sends the raw packet through the existing privacy check exactly as other Programming text is screened, and posts the parsed object only after acceptance. A final DCP defect instead renders **Coordinated documentation correction required**, shows the complete defect, clears the submitted packet, and directs recovery through the five-repository coordinator with fresh evidence. A successful DCP result says that final review accepted five local branches and reports whether the approved finish stops locally or permits coordinated landing; it does not claim that one repository was pushed or merged. The run endpoint streams newline-delimited JSON so the surface updates while the coordinator works. Closing the panel returns to ordinary Inquiry.

The old management interview, Principal/Technical plan projections, Process Library, process-bound Trigger Manager, Run Inspector, process-automation controls, process-attention groups, and automatic entry router are not compatibility surfaces. Scheduled Triggers now live separately under Oversight and do not wrap Programming work.

### Model and capability boundaries

Planning, execution, and review use Ora's configured model endpoints. Endpoints whose transport or identity is Codex, Claude Code, or `claude_code` are rejected. A vision-capable configured slot is preferred for review so direct image/PDF inspection remains available; separate model families are preferred when configuration permits but not required.

Programming preserves Ora's ordinary external and multimodal capabilities. Removing the former process stack does not remove `execution_packet.py`, `execution_families.py`, browser/runtime/render/vision support, audio/video processing, PDF handling, or ordinary Inquiry.

### Verification

Focused proofs cover:

- repository inspection before questions, including instructions, tests, Git, and automation;
- the two-round and three-question limits;
- one approvable plan;
- real edits and tests in a disposable Git repository;
- fresh review without executor claims;
- task-branch creation and accepted-slice commits;
- baseline drift returning `ASK USER` before mutation;
- an unsupported executor completion claim never producing `DONE`;
- independent outside-source retrieval;
- direct image bytes reaching the reviewer; and
- rejection of Codex and Claude Code transports.

Documentation-impact proofs also cover complete packet validation, exact agreement between plan and packet surfaces, rejection of absent or contradictory dispositions, and one correctly typed accepting semantic verdict per affected surface.

Browser tests prove Programming starts inactive, activates only from its toolbar event, plans only after explicit submission, waits for approval before `/api/programming/run`, displays milestone/review progress, and returns a concise terminal message.

## Appendix B — Implemented-vs-Planned Ledger

*Everything in the installed system at `7a5e8f40` that is not fully implemented, so the prose never overstates. Status vocabulary: partial (present but flag-gated-off or incomplete), planned (designed, not built), deferred (explicitly punted), stale (drift/superseded), unmerged (built but not on `~/ora` main).*

## Appendix C — Vault↔Repo Mirror Parity Rule

This document exists in two places, and two copies of the same document are a drift hazard the moment either side is edited alone. This appendix states the rule that prevents it. The rule is not decoration; it is the reason the Documentation-Code Parity chapter's machinery applies to this document as much as to any framework spec.

**The pairing.** The canonical file is the vault document `[[Reference — Ora Technical Documentation]]`. Its repository mirror is `~/ora/docs/technical-documentation.md`. The vault file carries YAML frontmatter (nexus / type / tags / dates); the repo mirror carries the body only. This is the same vault-canonical, ora-operational convention every paired Ora document follows.

**The sibling mirrors (added at Gate 7).** The identical rule governs the two derived installed-system documents created alongside this one: `[[Reference — Ora Accessible Overview]]` ↔ `~/ora/help/accessible-overview.md`, and `[[Guide — Using Ora]]` ↔ `~/ora/help/user-guide.md`. Both are body-only mirrors under the same vault-canonical direction of truth and are part of Aside's tracked five-file help corpus.

**Direction of truth.** The vault is canonical. Edits happen in the vault file; the repo mirror is regenerated from the vault body. If the two disagree, the vault wins and the mirror is rewritten — never the reverse. This mirrors the standing rule in `~/ora/CLAUDE.md`: files under `~/ora` are operational copies; the vault is where the source of truth lives.

**What must stay in sync.** The entire body: every chapter, every status label, every platform matrix cell. Nothing that changes the meaning may live in one copy and not the other. The only permitted difference is the vault frontmatter, which the mirror does not carry.

**Registration.** The machine-readable ownership block in [[Reference — Documentation-Code Parity Configuration]] declares all three pairs with `ora_body_only` propagation. The focused five-root gate strips the vault YAML block and exactly one following blank line, then requires every remaining byte, including the trailing newline, to match the registered Ora path.

**When the code moves.** If a code change alters a load-bearing claim, the change updates this vault canonical in the same coordinated task and regenerates the mirror before the focused final gate. If the change truly has no documentation impact, its final task commit names the owning surface in a `Documentation-No-Impact` trailer and independent review confirms the judgment.

**Enforcement limit.** Seven local hooks are installed: five blocking pre-push hooks and the fail-open Ora/vault post-commit audits. The authoritative protection remains the coordinator's explicit five-root finalization check and merge hold, with semantic comparison performed by the independent reviewer. Hooks remain bypassable, no remote cross-repository CI guarantee is claimed, and the repositories do not commit atomically.

## Appendix D — Glossary

Precise, one-sentence definitions of the load-bearing terms, defined here once so the chapters can use them without re-explanation. Terms are ordered for reading, not alphabetized.

- **Assisted human intelligence (AHI).** The framing this document uses for the technology: intelligence stays in the human's loop and the system is a tool that becomes useful when a skilled human directs it — the reframe is structural, not cosmetic, and the prose does not relativize it.
- **Foreground vs. background execution.** The operative runtime distinction: a task that finishes while you watch runs in the foreground; a task that outlives your patience runs in the background and reports through the observability log — same architecture, two runtime modes. This replaces the "manual vs. agentic" framing.
- **Orchestrator.** The Python process (`orchestrator/boot.py` and the modules around it) that sits between the user and the model, assembles context, runs the pipeline, executes tools, and persists state — the thing a raw chat interface is missing.
- **Gear.** A named pipeline configuration selecting how much adversarial machinery a prompt receives: Gear 1–2 are single-model direct responses; Gear 3 is sequential adversarial review; Gear 4 is parallel adversarial review with independent retrieval. The gear is chosen deterministically from the mode file, not by the model.
- **Mode.** A specification file (`modes/<name>.md`) for one analytical approach — its trigger conditions, epistemic posture, default gear, retrieval profile, model instructions, content contract, and success criteria — loaded to govern a single pipeline run.
- **Territory.** One of the twenty-one analytical regions the pre-routing pipeline disambiguates before it selects a resident mode; territory routing is the selection layer above the flat mode list.
- **The adversarial pipeline.** The eight-step sequence (analyst → cross-evaluator → reviser → verifier → consolidator → formatter, with a quality gate) in which an independent model reviews another model's output, on the premise that a model cannot catch its own self-generated errors.
- **Slot.** A named role a model fills — `breadth`, `depth`, `sidebar`, `consolidator`, `evaluator`, `step1_cleanup`, `classification` — decoupled from any specific model so a configuration can bind any model to any role.
- **Configuration (preset).** A named set of slot-to-model bindings (Free / Budget / Speed / Premium and user-defined customs) plus a fallback chain per slot; the active configuration is a pointer the router consults.
- **Provenance weighting.** Retrieval priority assigned by the epistemic status of a source — a confirmed synthesis (engram) outranks raw conversation (chat), which outranks unreviewed working notes — applied when the RAG layer ranks candidate context.
- **Engram.** A refined, confirmed knowledge asset in the vault (`type: engram`), the highest-provenance retrieval source; distinct from a raw conversation chunk or a working note.
- **The capability gate.** The always-on enforcement-and-audit boundary at the dispatcher chokepoint: every tool call is classified on risk axes, recorded, and allowed or refused fail-closed, before execution and independent of the user's permission mode.
- **Framework.** A background-executable procedure specification, invoked by name and run as a sequence of milestones through the gear pipeline — the precise construct the industry markets as an "agent." The framework registry holds many (about two dozen registered user-facing frameworks); the **canonical framework suite** is the closed six that cover the space of specifiable cognitive work — PEF (Problem Evolution), PIF (Process Inference), PFF (Process Formalization), CFF (Corpus Formalization), OFF (Output Formalization), and DCA (Decision Clarity Analysis, formerly the Wicked Problems Framework).
- **PED (Project Evolution Document).** The vault file that records a project's Mission, Excluded Outcomes, Constraints, Milestones, and Decision Log, and that the oversight apparatus watches for drift.
- **Process Coherence.** An explicitly invoked project/framework judgment that returns exactly PROCEED, REVISE, or ESCALATE; it does not fire automatically or participate in Programming.
- **Execution Packet.** The evidence-first record built at each gear terminal that separates an evidence lane (what was observed) from a judgment lane (what was claimed), so a reviewer grades observed reality rather than a fluent claim.
- **Evidence Contract.** The task-specific set of checks a given task must pass, produced at planning time and distinct from the repository's evidence catalog (which merely declares what checks exist) — the load-bearing distinction is that a green evidence run means nothing broke that you knew to check, not that the right problem was solved.
- **MLX.** Apple's array framework for on-device model inference on Apple Silicon (Metal); the local-inference engine on Mac, guarded so non-Apple hardware resolves to Ollama instead.
- **`runtime_paths`.** The single module that resolves every Ora filesystem root from the home directory with per-root environment overrides and supplies the cross-platform advisory file lock — the project's portability strategy in one place.
- **Documentation-Code Parity (DCP).** The framework and linters that detect drift between the vault's canonical markdown and the operational copies under `~/ora`, and the governance that keeps this very document's mirror in sync (see Appendix C).

## Changelog

- **2026-08-29** — Reconciled scoped current behavior: Windows launch now defaults oversight on with `--no-oversight`, checkout-bound process identity, safe argument forwarding, and native slash-path preservation; Chapter 12 now documents the separate Scheduled Trigger routes/UI plus durable publication, completion-delivery, timeout, and health behavior; Chapter 15 now documents section-specific DCP dispositions, complete semantic-review evidence, exact-head push boundaries, the Thinking Tools mirror, and vault backup transport; Chapter 18 now documents Programming's evidence packet and per-surface verdict contract.
- **2026-08-05** — Replaced the removed governed-process Chapters 18–21 with the current standalone Programming architecture: explicit entry, inspect-first planning, one approval, real repository tools, fresh reviewer calls, direct outside/non-text evidence inspection, accepted-slice commits, and Git-native recovery. The former process-bound entry, persistence, automation, trigger, inspector, attention, and lifecycle modules are not compatibility surfaces; the later separate Scheduled Trigger facility is described in Chapter 12.
- **2026-07-24** — Corrected G1.19 after gate review: the firing claim now persists and exclusively supplies the complete prepared invocation and deterministic Run identity; activation is draft-only, lifecycle retries are original-result idempotent, and every new claim atomically reauthenticates active state; the 30-second scan was replaced by startup reconciliation plus lifecycle-recalculated one-shot calendar wakes.
- **2026-07-24** — Added Chapter 21 for G1.19: immutable separately approved Trigger specs, content/source-bound exactly-once firing, ordinary governed Run invocation/recovery, OS file and accepted-framework events, Project milestone snapshots, named-zone justified app-only calendar operation, Trigger Manager UI/API, and the held G1.17/G1.21/G1.22 boundaries.
- **2026-07-24** — Closed the remaining G1.20 integrity findings: Pause authenticates before mutation and atomically commits its checkpoint/state pair, so rejected, stale, and racing losers leave no checkpoint; quality starts independently derive the current source, subject, and evaluator binding at the runtime boundary, and direct fabricated start/outcome records are unavailable.
- **2026-07-24** — Corrected the G1.20 gate submission: user Pause is now a persistent authority state releasable only by an authenticated Resume control; execute/retry, direct runtime calls, concurrency, interruption, and restart fail closed around it. Removed inferred zero token/cost claims from no-tools workers. Layer 2 now receives digest-verified bounded Artifact content, exact inputs, criteria, instructions, and failure trace, or deterministically returns `INDETERMINATE` without a model call.
- **2026-07-24** — Added Chapter 20 for G1.20: deterministic Run telemetry, authenticated worker liveness, restart-safe pause/resume/stop for G1.18 automated Runs, authority-inert opt-in quality evaluation only at handoff/output-failure seams, browser/sidebar surfaces, and explicit scheduling/Persona/channel/security boundaries.
- **2026-07-24** — Corrected the G1.18 gate submission after independent review: replaced presence-only verification with structured criterion-by-criterion assessment plus controller recomputation; bound verification starts, failures, checkpoints, attempts, restart retries, and exhaustion; enforced the complete admitted JSON Schema subset while rejecting unsupported keywords; reserved every action and final-verification baseline separately from the shared correction allowance; and isolated G1.18 attempt creation/completion from the generic runtime APIs. Added adversarial proofs for wrong/partial results, fabricated `PASS`, verifier outage/restart/recovery/exhaustion, input and output constraints, public API refusal, full action-retry consumption before final review, restart at that exact boundary, deterministic admission-ceiling blocking, and mutation-free generic wrong/future-node refusal followed by legitimate `ACCEPT`.
- **2026-07-23** — Added Chapter 19 for the G1.18 gate submission: exact G1.1 reconciliation, strict reusable-definition authoring and approval, authenticated registration/promotion, schema-driven Project-confirmed Library execution, existing Model/Style Run binding, no Persona/Trigger/external effects, isolated worker lineage, human checkpoint and recovery behavior, and the end-to-end unsent-email proof.
- **2026-07-19** — Added the Phase 3.5 closeout evidence binding and rendered-interface provenance against accepted Gate 3.4 commit `6824bb03`. Recorded the seven screenshot surfaces, final packet path, explicit residual limitations, and Gate 3.4 acceptance without changing runtime behavior or issued definition/package identity.
- **2026-07-19** — Expanded Chapter 18 for G1.1 Phase 3.4 against accepted Gate 3.3 commit `5bcba502`: added the maintainer reference for runtime objects/storage, contracts and bounded judgment, graph grammar, transitions, correction, parent-child continuation, recovery, evidence/identity, packages/catalogs/versioning, lifecycle, regression/change protocol, migration/disablement/rollback, troubleshooting, and known limits. Updated the browser plan surface/API map and retained exact vault-canonical/body-mirror parity. No runtime behavior or issued definition/package changed.
- **2026-07-19** — Added Chapter 18 as the G1.1 as-built governed-process reconciliation pinned to Ora commit `6740f2fc`. Replaced the opening closed-suite and Agent-object claims, documented the generic kernel, exact package/registry identity, Gear 3/F-Quality boundary, management interface, code/API map, authority/defaults, evidence, deviations, and limitations, and preserved G1.2's ownership of the full Gear 1/2/3 specification.
- **2026-07-16** — Added a scoped post-pin technical record for the landed PR #269 Trace Walk/trace-completeness subsystem: complete Gear 1–4 and direct/framework entry coverage, post-routing terminal evidence, truthful manifest categories and effective-Gear fallback, fail-open/privacy/Stealth boundaries, UI/API/CLI invocation, retention, behavioral verification, and source-map entries. The remainder of the document remains pinned to `7a5e8f40`.
- **2026-07-12** — Post-pin closure currency note: `commons` is the canonical runtime sentinel, `general` remains a permanent legacy input/wire alias, and Commons is the universal view rather than a stored membership—empty `project_ids` and empty YAML `nexus:` are its representation, while explicitly assigned project material also remains visible there. Commons output now defaults to the vault root; dormant config-driven layout/legacy-theme APIs and files are retired in favor of the fixed V3 workspace and V3 Theme Library; server launch paths are consolidated around a foreground launcher suitable for launchd supervision. Chapter bodies and line references remain pinned to `7a5e8f40`; this is a reader-orientation note, not a re-pin.
- **2026-07-11** — Default project renamed in user-facing prose: General → **Commons** (nomenclature note extended; seven body mentions updated in the Operation-Matrix and project-container chapters). The internal nexus id at the pinned commit is still `general` — the code-side rename is pending — so code identifiers, quoted literals, and line references are unchanged.
